Chapter 1-2: Introduction & Regulatory Philosophy

p4-p22

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture | p4-p22

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the core mission of Risk-MaPP: managing cross-contamination risk in shared facilities while balancing patient safety and employee safety
  • Grasp the five drivers behind the second edition of this guide, especially the updates to EU GMP Chapters 3/5 and the EMA HBEL guideline
  • Distinguish the fundamental differences and use cases of HBEL (Health-Based Exposure Limit) and OEL (Occupational Exposure Limit)
  • Know the main regulatory drivers: EMA HBEL Guideline (2014), EU GMP Annex 15 (2015), 21 CFR 211.42(c)
  • Understand the classification logic for high-hazard drugs: cytotoxics, hormones, sensitizers (beta-lactams)
  • Master the eight steps of the risk management lifecycle: Identification → Analysis → Evaluation → Control → Reduction → Acceptance → Review → Communication
  • Understand the difference between the GMP perspective (patient protection) and the industrial hygiene perspective (worker protection), and strategies for balancing them

Section Contents

Preface Acknowledgements Ch.1 Introduction 1.1 Scope 1.2 Background 1.3 Reasons for Revision 1.4 Guide Structure Ch.2 Concepts & Regulatory Philosophy 2.1 EudraLex Updates 2.2 EMA HBEL Guideline 2.3 Annex 2 2.4 Annex 15 2.5 21 CFR 211.42 2.6 Risk Management Overview 2.7 HBEL Use 2.8 GMP/IH Balance
Preface

Original Text

The use of multiproduct facilities for the production of pharmaceutical products is necessary to allow products to be brought to patients in a timely and cost-effective manner. Doing this safely requires an understanding of the products, the facilities, the processes, and the equipment and the risks posed by this combination. The major risk of concern within shared facilities is the risk of cross-contamination.

New GMP guidance for the EU came into effect in 2015 that requires a risk management process to determine if products can be safely manufactured in shared facilities. This new GMP requirement left industry unsure how to perform and document these risk assessments.

This Baseline® Guide provides a scientific risk-based approach based on ICH Q9 to manage the risk of cross-contamination to maintain product quality and operator safety. This allows the selection of the appropriate risk control strategies to be implemented on a case-by-case basis to maintain an appropriate balance between assurance of product quality and worker safety. This Guide also provides examples of risk assessments and the summary report.

This second edition of the Guide is updated to include the new EU GMP requirements as well as additional information for cleaning, HVAC, and examples to assist the reader.

During the development of the first edition of this document, the FDA and EMA were actively involved and other regulatory agencies reviewed the document prior to publication. For the second edition there were discussions with the EMA to ensure that the Guide did not contradict the new GMP requirements and again other regulatory agencies, including the FDA, reviewed the document prior to publication.

Advanced Topics Notice: Advanced Topics are noted and represent topics that require subject matter experts to address. These issues should not be addressed by those that are not familiar with the topic or do not have sufficient experience to understand how to apply the concepts appropriately.

Tutorial Commentary

Why Risk-MaPP Exists

Risk-MaPP = Risk-Based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products. This guide addresses a real pain point in the industry:

  • To save costs, manufacturers make multiple products in the same facility (shared facilities)
  • The 2015 EU GMP update requires a risk assessment but does not tell companies how to do it
  • Risk-MaPP is the practical guide that fills this gap

There is only one core question: "When manufacturing medicinal products in a shared facility, is the risk of cross-contamination acceptable?"

Analogy: Food Allergy Risk in a Shared Kitchen

Imagine a restaurant whose kitchen prepares both peanut dishes and allergen-free meals. How do you make sure the peanuts do not contaminate the other food? You need to: assess how hazardous peanuts are → analyze the contamination pathways → set cleaning standards → confirm that residues are below the safe threshold.

Risk-MaPP systematizes this logic, makes it scientific and documents it, for multi-product pharmaceutical manufacturing.

Second Edition vs. First Edition: Key Changes

  • Incorporates the new requirements of EU GMP Chapters 3 & 5 (effective 2015)
  • Integrates the EMA HBEL Guideline (published November 2014)
  • Adds content on cleaning and HVAC, with updated examples
  • Both FDA and EMA took part in the review, to ensure the guide does not conflict with the new regulations

CDMO Practical Considerations

As a sterile fill-finish CDMO, your facility makes products for several clients at the same time, which is exactly the most direct application of Risk-MaPP. When a client auditor asks, "How do you make sure my product is not contaminated by other products?", Risk-MaPP gives you a complete framework for the answer.

Tutorial Commentary

What the Authorship Means: Cross-Disciplinary Integration

The author team of this guide is unusual: rather than experts from a single field, it deliberately brings together:

  • Toxicologists (Naumann PhD, DABT): set the scientifically based safety limits
  • Industrial hygienists (Abromovitz CIH, Burgess): manage employee exposure
  • Process engineers (Marshall CEng): facility design and cleaning
  • Quality assurance (Holmes, Hamilton): GMP compliance
  • Regulators (FDA, EMA, SwissMedic): ensure regulatory consistency

This diverse mix is the greatest strength of Risk-MaPP: it is neither a "pure GMP perspective" nor a "pure industrial hygiene perspective", but a balance of the two.

Analogy: Building Codes Written by Many Hands

It is like drafting a building safety code, which needs structural engineers (safety), fire engineers (escape), architects (design), the health department (hygiene) and the building authority (regulation) all at the table; if any one is missing, the code has blind spots.

Why EMA's Deep Involvement Matters

The second edition specifically stresses the participation of the EMA HBEL implementation team. This means the core framework of the second edition of Risk-MaPP, with HBEL as the basis for decisions, has received EMA's tacit endorsement. In an EU GMP inspection, citing the methodology of this guide is more persuasive.

Chapter 1: Introduction

Original Text

This ISPE Baseline® Guide: Risk-Based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products (Risk-MaPP) provides a scientific risk-based approach based on ICH Q9 to manage the risk of cross-contamination to maintain product quality and operator safety. This allows the selection of the appropriate risk control strategies to be implemented on a case by case basis to maintain an appropriate balance between assurance of product quality and worker safety.

Tutorial Commentary

The Dual Objectives of Risk-MaPP

The opening sentence captures the essence of Risk-MaPP: it serves two parties at the same time:

  • Patient safety: ensure that the product's efficacy or safety is not affected by cross-contamination (GMP perspective)
  • Employee safety: ensure that operators are not harmed by exposure to highly active drugs (industrial hygiene perspective)

These two objectives sometimes pull against each other; the value of Risk-MaPP lies in providing a systematic method to satisfy both at once.

1.1 Scope of this Guide

Original Text

When considering multiproduct facilities, to satisfy regulatory requirements risk management processes are necessary to determine and document reasonable and acceptable risk. This Guide provides a process that allows manufacturers to assess risk and determine where control strategies are necessary to meet acceptable limits for cross-contamination. The control strategies to manage risk can vary from administrative to full dedication or segregation.

This Guide is intended to provide professionals in the pharmaceutical industry with a consistent approach on setting acceptable limits to assess the potential of cross-contamination causing an undue risk to patient safety.

It should be noted that the use of the principles in this Guide assumes the following:

  1. There is a basic understanding of the hazard spectrum of compounds and containment fundamentals
  2. Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) are part of the team for the quality risk management process
  3. The integrity of the data used to support the quality risk management process is of high quality and meets regulatory expectations
  4. Risk management principles are not used to justify bad design or engineering practices

The principles described in this Guide can be applied equally to large and small molecular weight APIs, pre-clinical and clinical materials, and commercially marketed products in all dosage forms.

The term "controls" as used throughout this Guide encompasses all types of controls including:

  • Process controls
  • Operational controls
  • The full range of engineering controls
  • Procedural controls

Tutorial Commentary

Scope of Application: Broader Than You Think

Risk-MaPP applies to:

  • Large-molecule APIs (biologics) and small-molecule APIs (chemically synthesized drugs)
  • Preclinical and clinical trial materials, as well as marketed commercial products
  • All dosage forms (injectables, oral solids, topicals, etc.)
  • Facilities of any size, from small R&D facilities to large commercial manufacturing plants

Key prerequisite: this is not a handbook for beginners; point 2 explicitly requires the involvement of SMEs.

The Four Levels of "Controls"

Note the broad definition of "controls" in this guide: it is a continuum from lightest to heaviest:

  • Procedural controls (lightest): SOPs, work instructions, training
  • Operational controls: scheduling segregation, campaign manufacturing
  • Engineering controls: equipment design, HVAC, closed systems
  • Full dedication/segregation (heaviest): separate facilities, dedicated equipment

The core contribution of Risk-MaPP: it lets companies choose the appropriate level according to the level of risk, rather than always applying the strictest measures.

Analogy: Levels of Driving Safety

Consider risk management at a hazardous-chemicals transport company: low-risk cargo (procedural control: watch the road signs) → medium risk (operational control: drive only at specified times) → high risk (engineering control: sealed tanker) → very high risk (full segregation: dedicated road). Risk-MaPP helps you decide which level of "transport method" each drug product should use.

A Practical Problem for a Sterile Fill-Finish CDMO

Your facility fills general antibiotics, hormonal drugs and ordinary small-molecule drugs at the same time. Questions:

  • Which level of controls should each of these three product types use?
  • If a client asks to introduce a new cytotoxic drug, which assessment steps are triggered?
  • What professional backgrounds should the SME team include?
1.2 Background

Original Text

The science of toxicology recognizes the principle of a continuum of hazard associated with all compounds, even within individual classes of compounds. Zero risk is considered scientifically unachievable and not necessary. Some classes of compounds, that have historically included hormones, cytotoxic compounds, genotoxic compounds, pathogenic organisms, and veterinary products, have traditionally been placed in dedicated and segregated facilities to manage the risk. Sensitizers such as Beta-Lactam antibiotics have received particular scrutiny due to the perceived severity of potential allergic reactions.

Dedicated facilities are required for manufacturing active substances and medicinal products with a high sensitizing potential for which scientific data does not support an acceptable level of exposure or the risk associated with the handling of the product at the facility cannot be adequately controlled by organizational or technical measures.

The ICH in their Q9 Quality Risk Management document state: "The manufacturing and use of a drug product, including its components, necessarily entail some degree of risk."

The FDA also has acknowledged: "Although medicinal products are required to be safe, safety does not mean zero risk. A safe product is one that has reasonable risks, given the magnitude of the benefits expected and the alternative available."

The EMA acknowledged: "the concept of 'zero risk' does not apply to medicinal products. The licensing of medicinal products needs to be assessed in the context of the benefit/risk balance concept."

When processing compounds in a multiproduct facility the risk of cross-contamination should be controlled so that products do not have any cross-contamination above established safe levels.

Tutorial Commentary

Debunking "Zero Risk" Scientifically

This section of the background sets out the most important philosophical premise of Risk-MaPP: zero risk is scientifically unattainable. ICH Q9, FDA and EMA all agree on this position.

This premise has practical consequences: it allows manufacturers to use "acceptable risk" rather than "zero risk" as the decision criterion. If your auditor insists on "zero residue", this concept is your scientific basis.

The Historical Classification of High-Hazard Drugs

This section lists the types of compounds that have traditionally required dedicated facilities; it is important to understand the historical background of this classification:

  • Cytotoxic/genotoxic drugs: cancer chemotherapy agents, effective/toxic at low doses
  • Hormones (especially sex hormones): trace amounts can affect homeostasis
  • Beta-lactam antibiotics: a special case; the problem is not toxicity but sensitization (cannot be handled with an HBEL calculation; dedicated facilities are required)
  • Veterinary drugs: not intended for humans, so the risk of cross-contamination into human medicines is unique

Note: the Risk-MaPP methodology is not applicable to highly sensitizing substances (such as beta-lactams), which must go straight to dedicated facilities.

Analogy: Allergen Management in the Food Industry

A person with a peanut allergy reacts to peanuts much as beta-lactam sensitization works: even a very small residue can trigger a severe reaction (anaphylaxis). Such substances cannot be managed with an "acceptable residue level"; they can only be fully segregated. The risk quantification method of Risk-MaPP applies to compounds for which "how much dose produces how much toxic effect" can be calculated, and not to sensitizers for which "any amount may trigger an exaggerated immune reaction".

1.3 Reasons for Revisions

Original Text

Since the original publication of this Guide there were some significant changes in regulations and application of regulations. These include:

  1. The updated EU GMPs relative to managing the risk of cross-contamination:
    • Chapter 3 was updated to allow the use of quality risk management principles to determine if products can be produced in shared facilities.
    • Chapter 5 was also updated to incorporate quality risk management principles and require toxicological assessments which refers to the EMA guideline.
  2. The EMA issued "Guideline on setting health based exposure limits for use in risk identification in the manufacture of different medicinal products in shared facilities" to provide guidance on how to set health-based limits. This guideline also states "PDE and ADE are essentially synonymous".
  3. ICH issued M7 "Assessment and Control of DNA Reactive (Mutagenic) Impurities" which sets a Threshold of Toxicological Concern (TTC) for mutagenic impurities.
  4. The adoption by US and EU regulators of a "life cycle" approach to process validation incorporating stages of process design, process qualification, and continued process verification.
  5. Industry began implementing risk-based approaches to managing the risk of cross-contamination and sharing some lessons learned.

Tutorial Commentary

Timeline of the Five Revision Drivers

From the first edition (2010) to the second edition (2017), the regulatory environment changed dramatically over seven years:

  • August 2014: EU GMP Chapters 3 & 5 updated, formally requiring a toxicological assessment
  • November 2014: EMA HBEL Guideline published, defining PDE = ADE
  • 2014: ICH M7 published, setting the TTC concept for genotoxic impurities
  • 2015: EU GMP Annex 15 updated; cleaning validation limits must be based on a toxicological assessment
  • From 2011: FDA Process Validation Guidance promotes a lifecycle approach

The Key Equation: PDE = ADE

This equation is very important:

  • PDE (Permitted Daily Exposure) = EMA term, derived from the ICH Q3C residual solvent concept
  • ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure) = ISPE/Risk-MaPP term, from the industrial hygiene tradition

The two are calculated in essentially the same way: both are based on toxicological data and take into account the NOAEL, uncertainty factors, body weight adjustment, route of administration and so on. In its 2014 guideline, EMA formally stated that the two are "essentially synonymous", unifying industry terminology.

Practical tip: in documents, using either PDE or ADE is acceptable, but it is best to be consistent and to explain the relationship to the other term.

An Important Shift to the Lifecycle Approach to Validation

The cleaning validation lifecycle concept in point 4 has far-reaching impact:

  • Old view: cleaning validation = three-batch testing, done once it passes
  • New view: cleaning validation = ongoing process control, which requires:
    design stage (understand residue behavior) → qualification stage (verify cleaning effectiveness) → continued verification (monitor routine cleaning performance)

This concept fits Risk-MaPP closely: risk management is not a one-time assessment but a continuous lifecycle activity.

1.4 Structure of this Guide

Original Text

This Guide should be read and considered holistically as reading individual subsections in isolation can lead to the unjustifiable conclusions.

The layout of this Guide mirrors the Quality Risk Management Process as defined in ICH Q9:

  • Chapter 2 — Concepts and Regulatory Philosophy: general overview of regulatory expectations and key concepts
  • Chapter 3 — Quality System fundamentals and the "Logic Diagram" (balancing GxP and Industrial Hygiene)
  • Chapter 4 — Risk Assessment overview (identification, analysis, evaluation)
  • Chapter 5 — Risk Identification: health-based exposure limits
  • Chapter 6 — Risk Analysis: factors affecting exposure
  • Chapter 7 — Risk Evaluation: comparison of risk criteria
  • Chapter 8 — Risk Control strategies
  • Chapter 9 — Risk Reduction: areas to address
  • Chapter 10 — Risk Acceptance
  • Chapter 11 — Risk Management Tools
  • Chapters 12 & 13 — Risk Review and Risk Communication (ongoing processes)
  • Appendices — References, glossary, risk assessment examples

Tutorial Commentary

Risk Management Lifecycle: The Structure of the Guide

The chapter structure of the whole of Risk-MaPP is the concrete implementation of the ICH Q9 risk management process:

Ch.5 Identification

Ch.6 Analysis
→
Ch.7 Evaluation
→
Ch.8 Control

The Warning to Read the Guide as a Whole

The guide specifically stresses that it must be understood as a whole and not quoted out of context. Common mistakes in practice:

  • Reading only Chapter 5 (HBEL setting) and skipping Chapter 6 (exposure analysis), resulting in wrongly set limits
  • Reading only Chapter 9 (risk reduction measures) without carrying out Chapters 5-7, so there is no way to demonstrate that the measures are adequate
  • Quoting only the ADE value without understanding its underlying assumptions, and being challenged by the auditor

Analogy: How to Read a Recipe Book

It is like a recipe book: you cannot look only at the step "add one teaspoon of salt"; you have to understand the whole cooking process. Every step has its causes and consequences. This applies even more to the logical flow of Risk-MaPP: the output of one chapter is the input of the next.

Chapter 2: Concepts and Regulatory Philosophy

Original Text

The current situation is that there exists some variation between regulatory authorities in the interpretation of commensurate control strategies associated with pharmaceutical manufacturing. Worldwide, regulators are beginning to support the use of risk-based approaches to determine the acceptable level of controls required for the compliant manufacture of highly hazardous compounds within multiproduct facilities, but there are still several regions that have not embraced a risk-based approach.

The US FDA and the EMA are expecting a documented risk assessment to determine if products can be produced in shared facilities as well as helping to identify the controls necessary to manage the risk of cross-contamination in these shared facilities.

Tutorial Commentary

The Global Regulatory Picture: A Transition in Progress

As of 2017 (when the second edition was published), global regulations were split between "progressive" and "conservative":

  • Actively accept the risk-based approach: FDA, EMA, SwissMedic
  • Allow conditional shared manufacturing: WHO, MHLW (Japan)
  • Still insist on strict segregation: some regulators in Asia and emerging markets

FDA and EMA explicitly require a "documented risk assessment"; this is not optional but an obligation.

2.1 EudraLex – EU GMP Updates

Original Text

In August of 2014 the European Commission updated Chapters 3 and 5 to provide improved guidance on managing the risk of cross-contamination. These changes encourage the use of risk management principles to manage the risk of cross-contamination. Both chapters reference a toxicological evaluation as described in the EMA "Guideline on setting health based exposure limits."

Section 3.6 has updated guidance on when dedicated facilities are required:

"Dedicated facilities are required for manufacturing when a medicinal product presents a risk because:
i. the risk cannot be adequately controlled by operational and/or technical measures,
ii. scientific data from the toxicological evaluation does not support a controllable risk (e.g. allergenic potential from highly sensitising materials such as beta lactams) or
iii. relevant residue limits, derived from the toxicological evaluation, cannot be satisfactorily determined by a validated analytical method."

Section 5.20 states: "A Quality Risk Management process, which includes a potency and toxicological evaluation, should be used to assess and control the cross-contamination risks presented by the products manufactured. Factors including; facility/equipment design and use, personnel and material flow, microbiological controls, physico-chemical characteristics of the active substance, process characteristics, cleaning processes and analytical capabilities relative to the relevant limits established from the evaluation of the products should also be taken into account."

Tutorial Commentary

The Three Situations That Require Dedicated Facilities

The three triggers listed in EU GMP 3.6 are very clear and are also the auditor's checklist:

  1. Technical/operational measures are insufficient: even with all engineering controls, the risk still cannot be adequately controlled
  2. Toxicological data do not support it: the classic example is the sensitization of beta-lactams, where science cannot set a "safe residue level"
  3. Analytical methods cannot be validated: if residues cannot even be reliably detected, the effectiveness of cleaning cannot be confirmed

Note: these three points are "required" dedication, not "recommended".

Practical Application: The Audit Implications of Section 5.20

Section 5.20 is the clause most often cited in EU GMP inspections. Auditors will ask:

  • Does your QRM process include a toxicological assessment? (Where is the ADE/PDE report?)
  • Are facility/equipment design and the flow of personnel/materials considered in the risk assessment?
  • Is your analytical method sensitive enough to detect the relevant residue limits?
  • Is there a periodic review mechanism to ensure the controls remain effective?

The Revolutionary Change in EU GMP Chapters 3 & 5

The August 2014 EU GMP update was the main driver of the second edition of Risk-MaPP. A comparison of before and after the change:

2.2 EMA HBEL Guideline

Original Text

The EMA introduced this guideline in November 2014. This guideline introduces the Permitted Daily Exposure (PDE) to determine a substance specific dose that is unlikely to cause an adverse effect if an individual is exposed at or below this dose every day for a lifetime. This is an extension of the PDE introduced in ICH Q3C(R6) for residual solvents.

This guideline outlines the process used to develop a substance specific PDE and a suggested summary document. The EMA document also states that the PDE and ADE are essentially synonymous. This guidance document is applicable to API manufacture as well as finished dosage manufacture.

Tutorial Commentary

Origin and Extension of the PDE Concept

How the PDE concept developed:

  • ICH Q3C (1997): PDE first used for safety limits of residual solvents in drug products
  • ICH Q3D (2014): extended the PDE concept to elemental impurities
  • EMA HBEL (2014): further extended to the control of cross-contamination by active pharmaceutical ingredients in shared facilities

Key wording of the PDE definition: "a dose unlikely to cause an adverse effect if an individual is exposed at or below this dose every day for a lifetime". Note the two important assumptions: "lifetime" and "any route".

PDE/ADE Calculation Framework (Overview)

PDE = NOAEL × weight adjustment factor / (F1 × F2 × F3 × F4 × F5)

  • NOAEL: No Observed Adverse Effect Level (from toxicology studies)
  • F1: interspecies differences (animal → human, typically ×5 or ×12)
  • F2: interindividual differences (generally ×10)
  • F3: study duration (short-term vs. long-term studies)
  • F4: adjustment for severe/irreversible toxicity
  • F5: other uncertainties

Calculating a PDE requires an SME with a toxicology background. It is not recommended that non-specialists calculate it themselves.

Scope: API Manufacturing + Drug Product Manufacturing

The EMA HBEL guideline explicitly covers two levels: active substance (API) manufacturing sites and finished dosage form sites. This means:

  • An API supplier that shares equipment among multiple APIs needs an HBEL
  • A drug product manufacturer filling multiple products likewise needs an HBEL
  • A CDMO that takes on both API synthesis and drug product work must assess both scenarios
2.3 EU GMP Annex 2 – Biological Medicinal Products

Original Text

Annex 2 states "Unlike conventional medicinal products, which are manufactured using chemical and physical techniques capable of a high degree of consistency, the manufacture of biological active substances and medicinal products involves biological processes and materials, such as cultivation of cells or extraction from living organisms. These biological processes may display inherent variability, so that the range and nature of by-products may be variable. As a result, quality risk management (QRM) principles are particularly important for this class of materials and should be used to develop the control strategy across all stages of manufacture so as to minimise variability and to reduce the opportunity for contamination and cross-contamination."

Tutorial Commentary

Special Challenges of Biological Products

Annex 2 is a reminder of why biological products need QRM thinking even more:

  • Inherent variability: biological processes (cell culture, fermentation) naturally show batch-to-batch variation, and the composition of by-products is not fixed
  • Complexity: large molecules such as proteins and polysaccharides are far more complex than small molecules, and their risk is hard to assess with a single chemical indicator
  • Special forms of cross-contamination: cross-contamination of biologicals can include host cell proteins, DNA, viruses and so on

The Risk-MaPP principles can be applied to biological products, but the reference to Annex 2 is a reminder that the assessment must take their unique complexity into account.

What This Means in Practice for a Sterile Fill-Finish CDMO

If your CDMO fills both biologics (such as monoclonal antibodies) and small-molecule injectables, pay particular attention to the following:

  • ADE calculation for biologics requires different toxicological considerations (potency, immunogenicity, etc.)
  • Cleaning validation must take into account how difficult large-molecule proteins are to clean
  • Analytical methods must be able to distinguish and detect residues specific to biologics
2.4 EU GMP Annex 15 – Qualification and Validation

Original Text

The cleaning validation section of Annex 15 (as well as other sections) was updated in 2015. The Annex states that "Limits for the carryover of product residues should be based on a toxicological evaluation." and the EMA guideline on setting health-based exposure limits is referenced. PIC/S Annex 15 adopted the same language in a recent update.

Tutorial Commentary

PIC/S Follows: Impact on Taiwan GMP

Note in particular: PIC/S Annex 15 adopted the same language. Taiwan is a PIC/S GMP member, so for PIC/S-certified sites in Taiwan the way cleaning validation limits are calculated should also move toward a toxicology-based approach.

If your facility still uses "10 ppm" or "1/1000 TDD" as the sole basis for cleaning limits, you may need to update to a calculation based on ADE/PDE.

Analogy: Making Maximum Residue Limits (MRL) in Food Scientific

Just as food pesticide residue standards evolved from "seems reasonable" to being "scientifically calculated from the Acceptable Daily Intake (ADI)", cleaning validation limits for drugs have made the same transition, from rules of thumb to a scientific basis.

The Cleaning Validation Revolution in Annex 15

A comparison of cleaning validation approaches before and after the 2015 Annex 15 update:

2.5 US FDA 21 CFR 211.42

Original Text

The US FDA regulation that often is cited when there are concerns with cross-contamination is 21 CFR 211.42 that states "There shall be separate or defined areas or such other control systems for the firm's operations as are necessary to prevent contamination..."

During the planning meetings for the original ISPE Risk-MaPP Baseline® Guide published in 2010, the FDA specifically requested that this Guide:

  • Provide an approach to identify highly hazardous drugs
  • Provide a risk management/assessment model that gives a clear view on how to address the controls to comply with 21 CFR 211.42(c)
  • Discuss how the approach fits into cleaning validation

2.5.1 Approach to Identify Highly Hazardous Drugs

The original intention of this Guide was to provide a method to identify highly hazardous compounds. However, during the development of the first edition, it became apparent that an approach to identify acceptable risk in the manufacture of pharmaceutical products was more appropriate.

Health-based limits should be developed by toxicologists from available toxicological and pharmacological data. This Guide refers to this health-based limit as the Acceptable Daily Exposure (ADE) value. The ADE value represents a dose that is unlikely to cause an adverse effect if an individual is exposed, by any route, at or below this dose every day for a lifetime.

This Guide is predicated on some key concepts:

  • Hazard: the inherent property of a compound to produce harm.
  • Exposure: contact with the hazard; dependent on the control systems used.
  • Risk: the probability of occurrence of harm at a given level of exposure, reflecting severity of harm.

2.5.2 Risk Management Model for 21 CFR 211.42(c)

21 CFR 211.42(c) states that "Operations shall be performed within specifically defined areas of adequate size. There shall be separate or defined areas or such other control systems for the firm's operations as are necessary to prevent contamination or mixups..."

2.5.3 How the Approach Fits into Cleaning Validation

Residues from a compound which remain after cleaning on equipment or other product contact surfaces may pose a risk to patient safety. Cleaning procedures should be developed to minimize the risk from residues. To reduce risk, cleaning procedures should be capable of reducing residues to, or below, a predetermined safe level based on the risk assessment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Points in Interpreting 21 CFR 211.42

The key to this regulation is: "separate or defined areas or such other control systems".

"Or such other control systems" is the legal room in which Risk-MaPP exists: the FDA text itself already allows "other control systems" as an alternative to "physical segregation". What Risk-MaPP provides is the scientific basis and documentation structure for these "other control systems".

Hazard × Exposure = Risk: The Triangle Relationship

The core risk formula of Risk-MaPP:

  • Hazard: an inherent property of the compound that cannot be changed (ADE is the quantitative indicator)
  • Exposure: the degree of contact with the hazard, which can be reduced through controls
  • Risk = severity of the hazard × probability of exposure

Conclusion: because the hazard (the toxicity of the compound) cannot be changed, the only way to reduce risk is to reduce exposure. This is the theoretical basis of all controls.

A Shift in Thinking on "Identifying High-Hazard Drugs"

A notable shift in thinking: the first edition originally intended to provide a "list of high-hazard drugs" but ultimately provided an "acceptable risk assessment framework" instead.

Reasons:

  • "High hazard" is a relative concept that depends on the ability to control it
  • Labels (cytotoxic, potent) easily lead to emotional decisions
  • The scientific approach is: set the ADE → assess exposure → calculate risk → decide the level of control

Integrated Framework for Cleaning Validation

The integration logic between Risk-MaPP and cleaning validation:

  1. Obtain the compound's ADE (toxicological assessment)
  2. Calculate the cleaning limit (swab or rinse limit) from the batch size, equipment contact surface area and the minimum dose of the next product
  3. Confirm that the sensitivity of the analytical method is sufficient to detect that limit
  4. Verify that the cleaning procedure can reduce residues below the limit
  5. Monitor continuously (lifecycle approach)
2.5.4 Regulatory Environment

Original Text

From 2003 through 2015, ICH working groups produced several significant documents: ICH Q3C(R6), Q3D, Q7A, Q8(R2), Q9, Q10, Q11, Q12 and ICH M7. These documents have been or are being adopted by the ICH parties (US, Japan, and EU) and used worldwide.

Key milestones:

  • ICH Q3C(R6): Introduced the PDE concept for residual solvents
  • ICH Q3D: Clarifies PDE limits for elemental impurities
  • ICH Q7 (section 4.4): Dedicated production areas for highly sensitizing materials (penicillins, cephalosporins) and consideration for high pharmacological activity compounds
  • ICH Q8(R2): Introduced Quality by Design — "quality should be built into a product with a thorough understanding"
  • ICH Q10: Effective pharmaceutical quality system throughout the product life cycle
  • ICH M7: Mutagenic impurities — limit of 1 in 100,000 excess cancer risk

In 2002, the FDA announced its Pharmaceutical cGMPs for the 21st Century – A Risk-Based Approach initiative. In 2007, it was renamed to "Pharmaceutical Quality for the 21st Century" to capture the larger issue of product quality.

In 2011 the FDA Process Validation Guideline states quality product is defined as the absence of unacceptable adulteration due to cross-contamination with other products produced in a shared facility.

The FDA OPQ key concepts applied to cross-contamination:

  1. Risk is a function of hazard and exposure
  2. There is a continuum of hazard — no distinct lines between low and high hazard
  3. Pharmacological and toxicological descriptions (NOAEL, ADE) should be used — not subjective terms like "potent"
  4. A consistent, robust, science risk-based approach across the hazard continuum
  5. Health-based exposure limits should not be artificially reduced
  6. GxP is primary; IH (worker safety) also must be addressed holistically

Tutorial Commentary

The Important Warning against "Artificially Lowering the ADE"

Point 6 (health-based exposure limits should not be artificially lowered) is a direct response to a common industry mistake:

Wrong approach: the toxicologist calculates ADE = 1 mg/day, and management says, "That's too high; change it to 0.1 mg/day to be safer."

Problem: if 0.1 mg/day is the correct scientific limit, the original toxicological assessment was wrong; if 1 mg/day is scientifically correct, the only effect of lowering it artificially to 0.1 mg/day is to make unnecessary "non-compliances" more likely, which then have to be explained as "why exceeding an unnecessarily strict limit is acceptable", and this is logically self-contradictory.

The Cultural Significance of "Avoiding Emotional Labels"

Point 3 criticizes subjective labels such as "cytotoxic" and "potent", which challenges the old culture. In the pharmaceutical industry, "this is a cytotoxic drug, so it must have the highest level of protection" is an automatic decision pattern that requires no scientific calculation. Risk-MaPP says: no. You need to calculate the ADE and assess exposure before deciding the level of control. This shift requires a change in organizational culture.

2.6 Overview of the Risk Management Approach

Original Text

A risk management approach should be applied to the handling of pharmaceutical products. This should be a holistically defined process to formally identify hazards and understand risks to assist decision making for the implementation of appropriate approaches for risk control. Although this process should be a formal part of an organization's quality system, the level of effort and detail for the risk management process can, and should, vary based on the perceived risks which are based on hazard, including severity and probability of occurrence.

The risk assessment process should be used to derive criteria that can assist in decision making and control strategies to manage the risks to the patient. The risk assessment should be a transparent evaluation of all data relevant to hazard identification, assessment of potency (dose response) and severity, exposure assessment, and characterization of risk.

ICH Q9 outlines a quality risk management process that is iterative and consists of the identification of the hazards and the analysis and evaluation of risks associated with exposure to those hazards, as well as control strategies to manage the risks.

Figure 2.1🔍: ICH Q9 Quality Risk Management Process [referenced in original — includes Risk Identification, Risk Analysis, Risk Evaluation (with loop back for Unacceptable risk), Risk Control (→ Risk Reduction / Risk Acceptance), Risk Review, and Risk Communication as an overarching thread]

Tutorial Commentary

The ICH Q9 Risk Management Process Explained

Figure 2.1🔍 is a diagram, but the core logic is:

① Hazard identification
→
② Risk analysis
→
③ Risk evaluation
→
④ Is the risk acceptable?
If not acceptable ↓ If acceptable ↓
⑤ Risk reduction
→ (loop back to ③)
⑥ Risk acceptance
→
⑦ Risk review
⑧ Risk communication (throughout)

The Principle That "Effort Is Proportionate to Risk"

This principle is very practical:

  • Low-ADE compounds (high hazard) → need a thorough quantitative risk assessment
  • High-ADE compounds (low hazard) → a qualitative or semi-quantitative assessment may be sufficient
  • Familiar compounds with a large body of historical data → can refer to existing assessments
  • New compounds with sparse data → need a more conservative assessment approach

Not every compound needs a complete 500-page risk assessment report, but making that judgment itself takes professional judgment.

2.7 Use of Health-Based Exposure Limits

Original Text

An API represents a hazard, as it is capable of producing biological effects (e.g., pharmacological or toxicological). The likelihood that these effects will occur depends on:

  • The dose-response relationship
  • The amount absorbed as determined by the route, level, and duration of exposure

The ADE takes these into consideration and reflects a safe dose which incorporates a margin of safety. The main premise of this Guide is that if exposure to an API is kept below a prespecified level that is determined to be safe, this would indicate low risk for adverse health effects.

A number of different terms have been used to describe health-based limits:

  • PDE — Permitted Daily Exposure (ICH Q3C, Q3D; EMA 2014)
  • ADE — Acceptable Daily Exposure (ISPE Risk-MaPP; industrial hygiene)
  • ADI — Acceptable Daily Intake (food additives)
  • TI — Tolerable Intake
  • RfD / RfC — Reference Doses/Concentrations (US EPA)
  • OEL — Occupational Exposure Limits (OSHA PELs, ACGIH TLVs, EC SCOEL IOELVs)

For carcinogens: a different risk-based approach — ICH M7 uses 1 in 100,000 excess cancer risk for mutagenic impurities.

Tutorial Commentary

Special Handling of Carcinogens: The Linear No-Threshold Model

General toxic compounds use a "threshold model" (NOAEL → safety factors → ADE); for genotoxic/mutagenic substances (mutagens), however, a different "linear no-threshold model" is used:

  • ICH M7 sets a lifetime excess cancer risk of 1/100,000 (one in a hundred thousand)
  • TTC (Threshold of Toxicological Concern) = 1.5 µg/day (a conservative default when data are insufficient)
  • This figure is generally much stricter than a typical ADE

Analogy: How Speed Limits Are Set

The ADE is like a road speed limit: slower is not always better. A speed limit is a "balance point" set scientifically from data such as road design, accident statistics and user characteristics. Artificially lowering the ADE tenfold is like changing a safe speed limit of 60 km/h to 6 km/h: although more "conservative", it is unnecessarily strict and not scientifically justified.

HBEL vs. OEL: Key Differences

This is one of the most important conceptual distinctions in Risk-MaPP:

2.8 The GMP and Industrial Hygiene Balance

Original Text

A clear strategy is necessary to assess risk in either the Industrial Hygiene (IH) or cGMP setting and to define scientifically based criteria in order to make decisions for managing risk. Although criteria for both are derived from the same toxicological or clinical data, the way in which these data are used to derive acceptable limits and adequate control strategies will differ.

The key differences relate to the application and interpretation of target population, routes of exposure, and acceptability criteria. These are summarized in the table below:

Balancing the needs of both IH and GMP interests is very important in ensuring that all risks, to product or worker, are adequately managed. Only through recognizing the differing needs and identifying a strategy that can address both is it possible to achieve safe, efficient, and economic operation of a manufacturing facility.

Tutorial Commentary

GMP Perspective vs. Industrial Hygiene Perspective: Why Both Matter

In a pharmaceutical plant these two perspectives represent two different functional departments:

  • GMP/Quality department: focuses on patient safety, product residues, cleaning validation
  • Industrial hygiene/HSE department: focuses on employee safety, workplace exposure, PPE

Problem: the two use different systems of standards, which can sometimes conflict. For example, a drug has an OEL = 0.01 mg/m³ (industrial hygiene considers it extremely hazardous) but an ADE = 5 mg/day (GMP considers the residue risk very low). How do you reconcile them?

The Numerical Relationship between OEL and ADE

The two can be converted into each other but cannot be compared directly:

  • The OEL (mg/m³) must be multiplied by the breathing volume (about 10 m³ per 8-hour working day) to obtain the daily intake
  • The ADE (mg/day) is the more general basis and can be converted into a limit for any exposure scenario
  • Normally: ADE < the daily dose converted from the OEL (because patient protection requires a stricter limit)
  • There are exceptions, however: for some drugs the inhalation route is particularly dangerous for workers (occupational asthma), and the OEL may be stricter

Analogy: Coordinating Fire Safety and Production Safety in a Factory

The fire department says, "The storage quantity of this chemical exceeds fire regulations and must be reduced"; production says, "We need this quantity to keep production continuous." Both are right, but an integrated solution is needed. The IH-GMP balance in Risk-MaPP applies the same integrative thinking: you cannot look after one side and ignore the other.

Implications for CDMO Organization Structure

This section has direct implications for the organization of a CDMO:

  • The Risk-MaPP risk assessment team should include both GMP/quality and industrial hygiene/HSE representatives
  • The new product introduction assessment process should trigger both a GMP risk assessment (cross-contamination) and an IH exposure assessment (employee safety)
  • Production can start only when both consider the risk "acceptable"; clearing only one review is not enough
  • If the two conclusions conflict (for example, GMP says OK but IH says higher protection is needed), a clear decision procedure is needed

Section Summary

  • Risk-MaPP mission: provide a scientific, ICH Q9-based systematic approach to managing cross-contamination risk in shared facilities, protecting both patients (GMP) and employees (IH)
  • The zero-risk philosophical breakthrough: ICH, FDA and EMA agree that "zero risk is neither attainable nor necessary", and use "acceptable risk" in place of "zero tolerance" as the decision criterion
  • Five revision drivers: EU GMP Ch.3/5 update, EMA HBEL guideline (2014), ICH M7, lifecycle approach to validation, accumulated industry experience
  • The key equation PDE ≡ ADE: EMA explicitly states the two are "essentially synonymous", unifying the EU (PDE) and industry (ADE) terminology
  • Three conditions requiring dedicated facilities (EU GMP 3.6): controls are insufficient, toxicology does not support controllable risk (such as beta-lactam sensitization), analytical methods cannot be validated
  • Core difference between HBEL and OEL: the HBEL (ADE/PDE) protects patients and is expressed in mg/day; the OEL protects workers and is expressed in mg/m³. The two sets of standards must be assessed separately and both satisfied
  • Risk management lifecycle: Identification → Analysis → Evaluation → Control → Reduction → Acceptance → Review → Communication (throughout), corresponding to Chapters 5-13 of Risk-MaPP
  • The Annex 15 cleaning validation revolution: the 2015 update requires cleaning limits to be based on a toxicological assessment (ADE/PDE), replacing the old 10 ppm or 1/1000 TDD rules of thumb; PIC/S followed at the same time
3

Chapter 3: Quality System & Logic Diagram

p23-p36

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture | p23–p36

Learning Objectives

  • Understand how the quality system applies to cross-contamination risk management and integrates with the ICH Q10 lifecycle approach
  • Master the structure and decision flow of the Logic Diagram: GMP/regulatory logic vs. industrial hygiene logic
  • Analyze how each GMP factor (cleaning, mix-up prevention, mechanical transfer, airborne transfer) affects the feasibility of shared facilities
  • Evaluate the importance of health/hygiene factors (exposure assessment, engineering controls, PPE) for personnel protection strategies
  • Understand what the control strategy outcomes (dedicated facility vs. campaign manufacturing vs. concurrent manufacturing) mean for CDMO business decisions

Contents of This Section

3.1 Application to Cross-Contamination 3.1.1 Recommended Quality Policy 3.2 Logic Diagram 3.2.1 GMP/Regulatory Factors 3.2.2 Health and Hygiene Factors 3.2.3 Control Strategy Outcomes Logic Diagram Overview

Quality System Requirements

Chapter 3, pp. 21-22 | Quality System Requirements: Overall Framework

3. Quality System Requirements

A well-defined quality system should consist of governance documents which are complemented by a concise set of tactical or procedural requirements. The overarching documentation matrix should provide the basis of consistency required for the creation of a sustainable quality system model which enables the ICH Q10 life cycle approach across the GxP regulated functions within an organization.

3.1 Application to Cross-Contamination

The key concepts discussed in this Guide should be used as a framework from which to develop standards, tools, and approaches to manage the risk of cross-contamination.

A case by case assessment should be performed of the risk factors that the concurrent manufacture of several products may cause. Risk assessments should be performed to properly understand the implications for both IH and GxP. While each is normally considered separately, to prevent any significant imbalance, the implications of decisions taken during an assessment for IH should be checked against those for GxP, and vice versa.

Figure 3.1🔍: Quality Management System — illustrates the governance hierarchy from quality policy through standards, SOPs, and forms, embedded in the ICH Q10 lifecycle framework.

Commentary: Overall Quality System Framework

Core Concept: Three-Tier Quality System Structure

ICH Q10 requires the quality system to cover the entire product lifecycle. In the context of cross-contamination management, the document hierarchy runs from the top down:

  • Policy level: a high-level statement approved by senior management
  • Standard level: technical requirements, such as the ADE determination procedure
  • SOP level: specific operating steps (cleaning, changeover, risk assessment)

Key Point: Dual-Track IH and GxP Assessment

The same hazardous substance must be assessed on two tracks at the same time, and the results must be cross-checked against each other:

  • GxP perspective: protects the patient, using the ADE (acceptable daily exposure)
  • IH perspective: protects the operator, using the OEL (occupational exposure limit)

If an IH decision (such as an engineering control) affects GxP performance (such as cleaning accessibility), the assessment must be redone.

Analogy: The Quality System as a City's Rules

The quality policy is the constitution, standards are the laws, and SOPs are the construction codes. All three are indispensable: the constitution establishes the principles, the laws set the boundaries, and the construction codes ensure consistent execution every day.

3.1.1

Suggested Quality Policies / Standards for Managing CC Risk

p. 22-23 | Six Recommended Quality System Areas

3.1.1 Suggested Quality Policies / Standards for Managing the Risk of Cross-Contamination

While many aspects for managing the risk of cross-contamination are covered in various quality documents such as cleaning and cleaning validation, line clearance, etc., it is suggested that overarching quality policies, standards and SOPs are put in place to manage the risk of cross-contamination that tie together these various aspects.

Other quality systems that are impacted or related to managing the risk of cross-contamination include:

1. Technology Transfer (New Product Introduction)

Firms should have a well-documented procedure for assessing potential products prior to introducing them into the facility. This should include:

  • A mechanism that triggers an assessment of the potential cross-contamination risks prior to bringing the product/compound into the facility.
  • The hazards of the product(s) should be identified typically via an ADE monograph.
  • Cleaning process development prior to introducing the product into the facility.
  • Analytical methods sensitive enough to meet the required acceptance criteria.

2. Risk Management

The Risk Management quality system should provide a common understanding of risk and how to assess it. Senior management should provide guidance on how to determine acceptable and unacceptable risk. It is suggested that this be established at the corporate level so that there is a common understanding across the organization.

3. Change Control

It is suggested that the change control process includes a trigger for risk review when the change may impact the risk of cross-contamination. Such changes may include: new product introduction, the ADE value of product, maximum daily dose for products, equipment changes, facility changes including changes to HVAC or water quality/pressure, process changes, or procedure changes.

4. Corrective and Preventative Action (CAPA)

An outcome of the risk assessment activities may be to implement some risk reduction controls. The CAPA system may be used to track the risk mitigation measures until implemented and confirmation of performance is complete.

5. Product Quality Review

Results from the risk management process may become part of the product quality review as appropriate. In addition, requalification or monitoring programs should also provide an indication that the risk of cross-contamination is in a controlled state.

6. Training

It is important that basic GMP training for all relevant staff includes practices that are designed to avoid cross-contamination as well as understand how cross-contamination occurs. Staff involved in managing the risk of cross-contamination should have further training in both how to avoid cross-contamination and quality risk management.

Commentary: The Six Quality System Areas

A Systematic Management Framework: Six Intersections

ISPE Vol. 7 identifies six quality systems that intersect with cross-contamination risk management, each of which is a potential control point or point of failure:

CDMO Business Focus: Technology Transfer Triggers

For a CDMO, new product introduction (NPI) is the highest-risk moment. ISPE requires that the following be completed before the compound enters the site:

  • ADE monograph analysis (toxicological assessment)
  • Cleaning development or historical-data risk assessment
  • Confirmation of analytical method sensitivity

This trigger mechanism should be built into the contract review process (Business Development, then Quality, then Engineering).

Practical Application: Change Control Cross-Contamination Trigger List

Any of the following changes should automatically trigger a cross-contamination risk reassessment:

  • Introduction of a new product or compound
  • Update of the ADE value or change of the maximum daily dose
  • Change of equipment design or materials
  • Change of HVAC configuration or water system pressure/quality
  • Process scale-up or formulation change
  • Change of cleaning procedure or cleaning agent

Analogy: Change Control as the "Early Warning System for Cross-Contamination"

Like an aircraft maintenance system, where any structural modification automatically triggers a safety review without relying on people's memory or manual initiation. The cross-contamination risk assessment trigger should be built into the change control process rather than added after the fact.

3.2

The Logic Diagram

p. 23 | Logic Diagram: Overview of the Decision Framework

3.2 The Logic Diagram

A logic diagram is a simple tool that can be used to guide a team through a process to determine the adequacy of existing controls or the need for dedicated facilities. (Section 3.6.1 should be read in conjunction with the flow diagram in Chapter 14.) Determining the need for dedicated facilities should be performed by a multidisciplinary team, so that all required areas are addressed appropriately.

The logic diagram is divided into two, addressing both GMP/Regulatory logic and IH logic. Both are equally important as they deal with aspects of risk associated with the different populations that may be exposed to the hazard under review:

  • Left side — product (patient risk)
  • Right side — worker (personnel risk)

Where dedicated facilities are implemented for business reasons, the risk assessment should clearly state that this is a business decision and not a cross-contamination issue.

Commentary: The Central Role of the Logic Diagram

What Is the Logic Diagram?

The Logic Diagram is a structured decision tool that uses a series of yes/no questions to guide a cross-functional team in determining:

  • Can a given product be manufactured in a shared facility?
  • What level of dedication is required?
  • Are the existing controls sufficient?

There are three possible outcomes: Shared facility feasible Partial dedication Fully dedicated facility

Key Point: Distinguishing Business Decisions from Cross-Contamination Risk Decisions

ISPE specifically emphasizes that if a dedicated facility is chosen for commercial reasons (customer requirements, market segregation), the risk assessment document must state clearly that this is a business decision and not a cross-contamination control necessity.

This prevents a business decision from being misread as a regulatory requirement, and avoids setting a wrong precedent when similar products are introduced in the future.

CDMO Application: The Logic Diagram as a BD Screening Tool

For a CDMO, the Logic Diagram should become the standard process for business review of new customers and new products:

  • BD stage: preliminary ADE screening to quickly judge whether the product is acceptable
  • Technical evaluation stage: full Logic Diagram assessment to determine facility requirements
  • Quotation stage: reflect the cost of dedicated equipment or segregation in the contract terms

Analogy: The Airport Security Decision Tree

The Logic Diagram works like airport entry procedures: each question node filters out one layer of risk. Most travelers (low-risk compounds) pass through smoothly; a few need further checks (partial dedication); very few are stopped for special handling (dedicated facility). What matters is that the process treats everyone the same and decides by risk rather than by intuition.

3.2.1

Considering the GMP / Regulatory Factors

pp. 23-29 | The GMP Logic Chain: From Regulatory Requirements to Technical Controls

3.2.1 GMP / Regulatory Factor — Logic Chain

3.2.1.1 Gathering Hazard Information (e.g., Health Based Criteria)

Both the IH and GMP criteria are drawn from common toxicological or clinical data sources. The ADE value is normally the health-based criterion used in assessing GMP concerns related to cross-contamination. The OEL is normally the health-based criterion used in assessing airborne product for worker protection.

3.2.1.2 Specific Requirements for Handling Product in a Dedicated Facility

"Is there a written regulation that would preclude the co-manufacture of this product with other non-related materials?"

The usual example is the beta-lactam class of antibiotics where 21 CFR 211.42(c)(d) requires separate facilities. The updated EU EudraLex Vol. 4 Section 3.6 requires dedicated facilities if:

  1. "the risk cannot be adequately controlled by operational and/or technical measures"
  2. "scientific data from the toxicological evaluation does not support a controllable risk (e.g. allergenic potential from highly sensitizing materials such as beta lactams)"
  3. "relevant residue limits, derived from the toxicological evaluation, cannot be satisfactorily determined by a validated analytical method."

Additional reasons to dedicate include: agency inspection consequence, internal QA recommendation, unique manufacturing technology, or high sales demand utilizing all capacity.

3.2.1.3 Determining an ADE

"Can an ADE be determined?"

If the scientific data supports the determination, then an ADE value should be determined and passed on to other stakeholders. If the information does not support the determination of an ADE value, then a dedicated facility is required. This is where the beta-lactam family of products would be addressed.

3.2.1.4 Detection of Residue Limits

"Can relevant residue limits be detected?"

Compounds that can be denatured/degraded/deactivated require studies to ensure the effectiveness of the process to render the residues safe. If analytical methods cannot be validated to the appropriate level, then a dedicated facility is necessary.

3.2.1.5 Technical and Operational Controls

"Do technical and operational controls manage the risk?"

Each of the modes of cross-contamination should be assessed to determine whether technical and operational controls can manage the risk. Care should be taken when assessing manual operations and the controls in place to detect manual errors.

Commentary: The GMP Factor Decision Chain

Key Point: The First Gate Is Regulatory Prohibition

The first question in the Logic Diagram asks directly: Is there regulatory text that explicitly prohibits shared manufacturing?

The answer is "yes" only rarely, but when it is, it is clear-cut:

  • Beta-lactam antibiotics (21 CFR 211.42 / EU GMP annex)
  • Certain biological products (live virus vaccines)

If the answer is "yes", the assessment ends immediately and a dedicated facility is mandatory. No further ADE calculation is needed.

Key Point: No ADE Means No Shared Manufacturing

If the toxicological data for a compound are insufficient to derive an ADE (for example, early clinical trial compounds or missing toxicology data), the default conclusion is that a dedicated facility is required.

This is the key logic of ISPE Vol. 7: it is not "no data, therefore safe" but "no data, therefore a conservative assumption must be made".

Practical Application: Analytical Method Sensitivity Requirements

The analytical method must be able to detect residues below the cleaning limit calculated from the ADE. The common calculation path is:

  • ADE (mg/day) → calculate the maximum residue limit (MRL, µg/cm²)
  • LOQ must be ≤ MRL (typically LOQ ≤ 50% of MRL is required)
  • If a method meeting the requirement cannot be developed → consider partial or full dedication

3.2.1.6 Cleaning (Retention) Aspects

"Can cleaning be carried out to meet the required criteria?"

Factors to be considered include:

  • Feasibility: analytical detection capability; effects of aggressive cleaning agents on equipment materials
  • Practicability: consistency of manual vs. automated cleaning; procedure clarity
  • Cost: direct and indirect costs of extended clean-down, particularly for frequent changeovers
  • Patient: patient risk is the primary focus and supersedes operational considerations

If these factors can all be addressed satisfactorily, then the use of a multiproduct facility with shared equipment may be a viable option.

3.2.1.7–3.2.1.8 Cleaning Resolution and Partial Resolution

If the current method cannot be validated to appropriate sensitivity, consider improving the method. If partial resolution is possible, dedicating specific equipment in the train (rather than the entire facility) may be a cost-effective option.

3.2.1.9 Mix-Up Aspects

"Are procedures, controls, and facilities designed such that mix-up is avoided?"

Factors include:

  • Procedures/SOPs: areas where procedures may fail or be too complex to follow consistently
  • Controls: adequacy of existing controls; need for additional controls
  • Facility Design: no shared process flows or areas of overlap; effective procedure + control combination

3.2.1.12 Mechanical Transfer

"Is the potential for mechanical transfer to be controlled to safe predetermined levels?"

Factors include:

  • Process: is the process closed? Can material escape and be transported via equipment, personnel, or supplies?
  • Procedures: gown changes, equipment wipe-down prior to leaving processing rooms, foot/wheel transfer prevention
  • Facility: differential pressures between zones; GxP issues with personnel/material flow
  • Operational Standards: adequate training; engineering vs. procedural controls; CMO-specific considerations

3.2.1.15 Airborne Transfer

"Is the potential for airborne transfer to be controlled to safe predetermined levels?"

Factors include:

  • Process: is the product exposed to the room environment during processing, transfer, or cleaning? Is the process closed?
  • Facility: adequacy of differential pressures; material and personnel airlocks; HVAC performance

3.2.1.18 Accommodating Product in a Multiproduct Facility

"Can the product be accommodated in a multiproduct facility, but with more specific dedication?"

Options for limited dedication include:

  • Dedicated Suite/Room in a multiproduct facility
  • Dedicated Equipment: individual vessels, contact parts (feed chutes, compression tooling, filling heads)
  • Disposable Equipment: filling tubing, filters, glassware — with proper decontamination and disposal assessment

Commentary: Controls for Cleaning, Mix-Up, and Mechanical/Airborne Transfer

Key Point: Four Dimensions of Cleaning Assessment (Patient First)

ISPE states explicitly that cost and feasibility considerations must not override patient safety. The order of evaluation is:

  1. Feasibility: can the analytical method reach the required detection limit?
  2. Operability: consistency of automated vs. manual cleaning
  3. Cost: economics of periodic cleaning (annual vs. weekly)
  4. Patient safety: the final criterion, which cannot be compromised

CDMO Application: Special Considerations for CMOs

ISPE 3.2.1.12 specifically calls out the CMO situation:

  • Operating standards must be adequate to handle the contracted products
  • If relying on engineering controls, the segregation changeover procedure at product changeover needs special evaluation
  • Do staff need compound-specific specialist training?
  • Is the cross-contamination risk from maintenance personnel included in the assessment?

These questions should be confirmed one by one when signing a technical agreement with a new customer.

Analogy: Partial Dedication Is Like Zoning a Shared Kitchen

A high-end restaurant kitchen may have a separate allergen preparation area (dedicated cutting boards and knives) but use a shared oven. This is the spirit of "partial dedication": isolate precisely at the risk points rather than abolishing sharing across the whole kitchen. The three options in ISPE 3.2.1.18 (dedicated suite, dedicated equipment, disposable equipment) embody this graduated thinking.

(Document risk assessment conclusion)

3.2.2

Considering the Health and Hygiene Factors (Personnel)

pp. 30-33 | Industrial Hygiene Logic: Personnel Exposure Assessment and Control

3.2.2 Health and Hygiene Factors (Applicable to Personnel)

3.2.2.1 Gather Hazard Information

The OEL is normally the health-based criterion used in assessing airborne levels of API from bulk or finished product for protection of workers.

3.2.2.2 Assess Exposure Potential

Risk is a function of both hazard and exposure. Several factors should be considered:

  • Who is at risk? — Workers at the line, maintenance personnel, general personnel, QA personnel when sampling
  • What factors influence exposure? — Dusty powder? Liquid? Closed or open process? Training level? Routine or frequent task?
  • What is the exposure pathway? — Inhalation, ingestion, or skin absorption?
  • When is exposure most likely? — Sampling, process breakdown, clean down, or product changeover?

3.2.2.3 Control of Exposure

"Are the exposures adequately controlled?"

Review available information: adverse health effects reported? Monitoring data available? Similar processes in other facilities? Controls should be commensurate with the risk and consistent with manufacturing process requirements (e.g., engineering controls must not make interventions or cleaning difficult).

3.2.2.4 Process Changes

"Is elimination, substitution, or process change possible?"

Examples where this has been effective:

  1. Removal of a solid recovery step between stages in a chemical process
  2. Change to a wet granulation process from a direct blend process
  3. Addition of a film coat to a tablet to reduce exposure during packing
  4. Formulate as a liquid/semisolid in a capsule rather than a conventional tablet or powder filled capsule

3.2.2.5 Selection of Engineering Controls

Once risk factors have been investigated, control measures can be considered. Factors include: cost, feasibility, ergonomics, ease of cleaning, and failure mode operation (what happens if the control measure fails).

3.2.2.6 Level of Exposure Risk

"Is the exposure risk still too high?"

Is it reasonable to add in a secondary system, such as an extract system? Is routine use of PPE acceptable for short-duration activities? Discuss with operating personnel, plant management, and HSE specialists. Operators may prefer PPE if a rigid barrier makes a process step ergonomically difficult.

3.2.2.7 Emergency Recovery

"Is emergency recovery required?"

Considerations: what will need to be recovered? What supporting infrastructure is required — PPE, change areas, showers, storage for PPE, separate air supply to service PPE?

3.2.2.8 Secondary PPE

"Is secondary control or PPE required?"

The rationale for secondary PPE should be well understood and documented: is it provided to supplement a primary control measure, or for emergency use only?

Commentary: IH Exposure Assessment and the Hierarchy of Controls

Industrial Hygiene (IH) Hierarchy of Controls

ISPE follows the traditional industrial hygiene order of control priority:

  1. Elimination / substitution (best): change the process to remove the source of exposure
  2. Engineering controls: closed systems, ventilation, isolation barriers
  3. Administrative controls: SOPs, training, work shifts
  4. Personal protective equipment (PPE) (last resort): masks, gloves, protective clothing

PPE should be regarded as a means of supplementing engineering controls and should not serve as the primary protective measure.

Key Point: The Critical Difference Between OEL and ADE

Even when derived from the same toxicological data, the OEL and ADE apply in different contexts:

  • OEL: protects healthy adult workers, based on an 8-hour inhaled exposure (µg/m³)
  • ADE: protects patients (including the elderly, children, and the immunocompromised), based on the daily oral or injected dose (mg/day)

As a result, the OEL and ADE values for the same compound can differ greatly; they should be assessed separately and must not be substituted for one another.

Analogy: Engineering Controls Are the Airbag, PPE Is the Seat Belt

The airbag (engineering control) is active protection, designed so that passengers ideally never need to rely on it; the seat belt (PPE) is passive protection that provides the last line of defense when the active protection fails. Neither can replace the other: the existence of PPE does not reduce the need to invest in engineering controls.

Three Elements of an Emergency Recovery Plan

If the primary containment system fails, the emergency response plan needs to cover:

  • What: what needs to be recovered, and who carries it out?
  • How: what infrastructure is needed (PPE storage, gowning area, showers)?
  • Air: is a separate air supply system needed to serve the protective PPE?

The emergency response plan should be completed before the product is introduced, not drawn up after an incident occurs.

3.2.3

Consequences of Control Strategy

p. 33 | Control Strategy Outcomes and the Feedback Loop

3.2.3 Consequences of Control Strategy

The consequences of adopting any particular exposure control strategy should be rechecked against the GxP/regulatory logic to ensure plant operability is not compromised.

"The consequences of adopting any particular exposure control strategy should be rechecked against the GxP/regulatory logic to ensure plant operability is not comprised."
— ISPE Baseline Guide Vol. 7, Section 3.2.3

This bidirectional check is critical: decisions made purely on IH grounds (e.g., adding rigid physical barriers for worker protection) may inadvertently compromise GMP operability (e.g., making cleaning access impossible or creating contamination traps). Similarly, GMP-driven decisions must be re-evaluated for their impact on worker safety.

Three Possible Outcomes of the Logic Diagram

Commentary: Control Strategy Outcomes and Their Business Significance for CDMOs

Business Implications of the Three Decision Outcomes

The Logic Diagram ultimately yields three facility strategies, each with a very different impact on the CDMO's business model:

Key Point: The Importance of the Two-Way Feedback Loop

The core message of Section 3.2.3: IH decisions and GMP decisions cannot each be optimized independently.

Typical conflict cases:

  • IH requires a closed isolator → GMP cleaning personnel cannot gain access → residue risk rises
  • GMP requires an open, cleanable design → operator dust exposure increases → OEL exceeded
  • IH recommends single-use equipment → GMP needs to validate the residue safety of the disposable equipment

Solution: a cross-functional assessment, with Engineering, QA, and IH participating together in the design review.

Recommended CDMO Business Decision Process

Integrate the Logic Diagram into the business process for new product introduction (NPI):

  1. At BD engagement: request the compound's ADE or toxicology summary and make a preliminary judgment of acceptability
  2. Technical feasibility assessment: run the full Logic Diagram and determine the facility strategy
  3. Quotation and contract: include dedicated equipment costs, validation fees, and changeover losses in pricing
  4. Technical agreement: state clearly whether a dedicated facility is a cross-contamination control requirement or a business decision
  5. Periodic review: repeat the assessment when the ADE value is updated or the process changes

Analogy: The Logic Diagram Is Like a Building Permit Review

Before a city building is constructed, it must pass a dual review of structural safety (GMP/patient safety) and fire code (IH/personnel safety). A developer cannot skip the fire protection facilities because they are expensive; likewise, a CDMO cannot ignore the impact on the process because IH controls are expensive. Both reviews must be passed at the same time, without contradicting each other.

Chapter 3 — Key Decision Points Summary

Commentary: Chapter Summary and Key Insights

Four Key Insights from Chapter 3

  1. The quality system is the backbone of cross-contamination management: technology transfer, change control, risk management, and CAPA must be linked together to form a complete cross-contamination control network.
  2. The Logic Diagram is an objective decision tool: used by a cross-functional team, it prevents subjective judgment and emotional decisions (such as "this compound sounds dangerous, so it must have a dedicated facility").
  3. The ADE is the core value in GMP logic: no ADE means no scientific risk assessment, which means the most conservative assumption (dedicated facility) must be made.
  4. IH and GMP must be coordinated in both directions: each constrains the other, and any control strategy must satisfy both patient protection and personnel safety without compromising either.

Key Takeaways for CDMOs

For a CDMO Operations Director, ISPE Vol. 7 Chapter 3 provides a defensible, science-based framework for product acceptance decisions.

When a customer or inspector asks, "Why can this compound be manufactured in a shared facility?", a fully documented Logic Diagram assessment is the strongest answer: not intuition, not historical habit, but a systematic risk assessment document.

This is also the most distinctive contribution of ISPE Vol. 7 compared with general GMP guidance: it gives manufacturers a scientific basis for saying "Yes, we can manufacture in a shared facility", rather than only telling you when to say "No".

Chapter 4-5: Risk Assessment & Identification

p37-p52

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture | p37–p52

Table of Contents
