Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the three steps of ICH Q9 risk assessment: risk identification, risk analysis, and risk evaluation.
  2. Distinguish the definitions and scope of application of "hazard" and "risk".
  3. Master the classification logic and prioritization tools of the Hazard Continuum.
  4. Understand the full derivation of an HBEL (health-based exposure limit): NOAEL → adjustment factors → ADE/PDE.
  5. Distinguish the purpose and calculation differences between the OEL (occupational exposure limit) and the HBEL.
  6. Understand the required content and qualification requirements for an HBEL assessment document.
4

Risk Assessment

ICH Q9 three-step framework — identification, analysis, evaluation | p37–38

Original Text — Chapter 4

Risk assessment as described in ICH Q9 encompasses three steps: risk identification, risk analysis, and risk evaluation. Each of the three steps helps to answer the three fundamental questions:

  • What might go wrong?
  • What is the likelihood (probability) it will go wrong?
  • What are the consequences (severity)?

The risk assessment steps may be considered the most important aspects of an overall quality risk management process. If the risks are not identified, analyzed, and evaluated properly, the decisions on how to control the risk cannot be made adequately.

The criteria for judging acceptability of risk for cross-contamination and operator exposures differ, although GxP and IH (Industrial Hygiene) considerations are linked because they share the same underlying toxicological data as the starting point from which they are both derived. In addition, the application and interpretation of the criteria in the risk assessment process will be different.

Part of the risk assessment process is to consider if there are realistically any plausible pathways that might give rise to an increased risk of exposure. For example, when considering airborne transfer, the following risk questions should be asked:

  • Can cross-contamination from an airborne pathway occur? Specifically, can product A be directly distributed via the air stream or resuspended back into the air (after being deposited) in a room/suite/facility, and then settle or get redeposited directly into product B or onto contact parts?
  • Can this occur in sufficient amount that the ADE for product A might be exceeded should a patient be administered the now contaminated product B?
  • Is it plausible for product that has been deposited on equipment or adjacent work surfaces to be redispersed into the air stream and present an exposure risk for operators via inhalation?

Similar questions should be asked to understand the risks associated with mix-up, retention, and mechanical transfer.

At the conclusion of the risk assessment, risk should be expressed in quantitative or qualitative terms depending on the data available from the three steps. This information should then be used in the decision making to accept or reduce the risks under the risk control steps.

Commentary

The Three Steps of ICH Q9 Risk Assessment

  • Risk Identification: confirm "What might go wrong?" by identifying all hazards and their possible consequences.
  • Risk Analysis: estimate the magnitude of the risk of the identified hazards, assessing the likelihood of exposure and the severity.
  • Risk Evaluation: compare against acceptance criteria to judge whether the risk needs to be controlled.

None of the three steps can be skipped: if hazard identification is incomplete, the subsequent analysis and decisions will inevitably be off.

GxP vs. IH: Same Source, Different Use

Common basis: both derive from the same toxicological data (NOAEL, LOAEL, dose-response).

The difference lies in application:

  • GxP (cross-contamination control): protects the patient; the question is "Do residues exceed the ADE?"
  • IH (industrial hygiene): protects the operator; the question is "Does the airborne concentration exceed the OEL?"

The acceptance criteria for risk differ, but the data sources are shared and the calculation logic is similar.

Worked Example: Assessing the Airborne Transfer Pathway

Dust from manufacturing Product A becomes airborne → settles into an open container of Product B → a patient takes Product B containing the residue.

The key question: "Does the amount that settles expose the patient above the ADE of Product A?" This is the core logic of cross-contamination risk assessment.

Four Exposure Pathways (Transfer Mechanisms)

  • Mix-up: errors in labeling or physical arrangement
  • Retention: residues left by incomplete cleaning
  • Mechanical transfer: residues carried away from equipment contact surfaces
  • Airborne transfer: dust and aerosols that become suspended and then settle

Each pathway must be assessed separately for feasibility, based on reasonable and credible scenarios (the Plausible Pathway principle).

5

Risk Identification — Introduction

Qualitative hazard identification as first step in risk assessment | p37

Original Text — Chapter 5 Intro

This Chapter describes risk identification, which starts with qualitative hazard identification, i.e., the process by which existing hazards are identified, initially without assigning magnitude. All available animal and human data should be reviewed to address the question: "What could go wrong?"

This first step in the risk assessment process also includes identifying possible consequences. It serves as a prelude to later steps of risk analysis and risk evaluation that ultimately lead to appropriate exposure control and management of risk.

Commentary

The Essence of Risk Identification

Qualitative, not quantitative: the first step only needs to identify whether a hazard exists, without immediately calculating numerical values.

Data basis: requires a systematic review of all animal and human study data.

Preparatory function: lays the groundwork for the subsequent "risk analysis (quantifying exposure)" and "risk evaluation (comparison with criteria)".

Analogy

It is like emergency room triage: first establish what is wrong with the patient (hazard identification), and only then can you set priorities (risk evaluation) and decide on a treatment plan (risk control). Prescribing medicine while skipping triage would have unthinkable consequences.

5.1

Definition of Hazard

Active pharmacological, toxicological, microbiological properties | p37–38

Original Text — Section 5.1

Hazard is defined in ICH Q9 as a potential source of harm. ICH Q9 also includes the severity of harm in the definition of risk. Within the context of this Guide, hazard describes the inherent property of a compound to produce adverse effects, e.g., in patients that may be exposed to the compound as a trace contaminant in another pharmaceutical product.

The compounds typically considered include:

  • APIs (Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients)
  • Process intermediates
  • Solvents
  • Excipients

Other compounds that may need to be considered include impurities, byproducts, and degradants. Each compound has its own inherent ability to cause adverse effects (i.e., toxicity) — effects that may be well documented in the case of the API, but may be unknown for other compounds.

Commentary

Definition of Hazard

ICH Q9 definition: a potential source of harm.

Specific meaning in this guide: the "inherent property" of a compound to cause adverse effects in humans, including:

  • Pharmacological: compounds that act at therapeutic doses, so even low-level exposure may affect patients.
  • Toxicological: causing organ toxicity, carcinogenicity, or reproductive toxicity above the safe dose.
  • Microbiological: although this chapter focuses on chemical hazards, microbial contamination is also a hazard in the broad sense.

Which Compounds Need to Be Assessed?

  • APIs: the most complete data, usually with a full toxicology data package.
  • Intermediates: compounds from the synthesis process, often with limited toxicological data.
  • Solvents: classified under ICH Q3C, with residue limits required.
  • Excipients: generally considered low in toxicity, but special populations (children, patients with kidney disease) still need attention.
  • Impurities/Degradants: the hardest to assess, often lacking complete toxicological data and needing to be estimated with the TTC approach.

Hazard vs. Risk: The Core Distinction

Hazard = the inherent ability of a compound to cause harm (inherent property).

Risk = Hazard × Probability of Exposure.

Even for a high-hazard compound (such as a potent cytotoxin), if the exposure pathway is completely blocked, the actual risk can approach zero. This is exactly the core logic of risk management in a multi-product facility.

5.2

Hazard Continuum and Prioritization

From no hazard to poorly characterised hazard — OEB/OEL estimation | p38–39

Original Text — Section 5.2

Depending on the hazardous properties of an individual compound, resulting from its mechanism-of-action and potency, health-based limits such as the ADE values can span several orders of magnitude. It is preferable to derive health-based values for all pharmaceutical compounds, regardless of potency, to ensure the protection of patients.

Similarly, there is a need to focus attention on those compounds with the lowest health-based exposure limits because these may be the compounds that are the most difficult to control, and the consequences of overexposure may be the greatest.

As part of an overall risk management approach, it is useful to have a qualitative tool to help determine the need for, and to prioritize, comprehensive quantitative risk assessments. This tool should ideally incorporate the severity of the effect/impact on quality of life, reversibility of the effect and likelihood of death.

Criteria for identifying hazardous drugs and prioritizing risk assessments include:

  1. Genotoxic compounds that are known to be or highly likely to be carcinogenic to humans
  2. Compounds that can produce reproductive and/or developmental effects at low dosages*
  3. Compounds that can produce serious target organ toxicity or other significant adverse effects at low dosages*

*For example clinical doses <1 to 10 mg/day or animal doses <0.1 to 1 mg/kg/day

The use of Occupational Exposure Limits (OELs) and Occupational Exposure Bands (OEBs) can be used to estimate ADE values for the purpose of prioritization in risk assessments. Extrapolating an OEL to an ADE value, in its simplest sense, is accomplished by:

ADE (µg/day) = OEL (µg/m³) × 10 m³

Many companies assign new compounds to an OEB, or equivalent designation corresponding to airborne concentration ranges, to provide guidance on safe handling before sufficient data are available to establish a numerical OEL.

Table 5.1: Estimating ADE Values Using OEBs

* Estimated ADE = Low End of Band × 10 m³

Commentary

The Hazard Continuum

The degree of hazard is not a dichotomy (dangerous/safe) but a continuous band that spans several orders of magnitude depending on the compound's mechanism of action and potency:

Hazard absent
No Hazard
ADE > 10 mg/day
Clearly characterised hazard
Well-Characterised
ADE 0.01–10 mg/day
Poorly understood hazard
Poorly Characterised
ADE < 0.01 mg/day

For compounds with a very low ADE (such as cytotoxic drugs and highly potent hormones), control costs are high and the consequences of an excursion are severe, so detailed risk assessment takes priority.

Criteria for Identifying Three Classes of High-Hazard Drugs

  • Genotoxic carcinogens: ICH M7 applies; the ADE must be calculated using TTC or linear extrapolation.
  • Reproductive/developmental toxicity: low doses may affect the fetus or fertility, and the adjustment factors must include the AFD.
  • Highly potent organ-toxic drugs: significant effects at clinical doses <1–10 mg/day, giving a very low ADE estimate.

Quick ADE Estimation from the OEB (for Prioritization)

Before a formal ADE document has been established, the OEB can serve as a provisional classification tool:

ADE (µg/day) = OEL_low_end (µg/m³) × 10 m³

Example: lower end of OEB 4 = 1 µg/m³
estimated ADE = 1 × 10 = 10 µg/day

Note: an OEB-derived estimate is for prioritization only and cannot replace an ADE established through a formal toxicological assessment. Once sufficient data are available, a formal ADE document should be established.

Practical Application for CDMOs

When taking on a new molecule for which the customer has not yet provided a formal ADE document:

  • First estimate the ADE using OEB classification to decide the cleaning validation targets and facility layout requirements.
  • Ask the customer for the toxicology data package; the CMO is responsible for ensuring that every compound it manufactures has a scientifically based ADE.
  • If no data exist at all, apply the TTC approach: 1 µg/day for potential carcinogens, 10 µg/day for highly toxic compounds, and 100 µg/day for general compounds.
5.3

Establishing Health-Based Exposure Limits (HBEL)

Foundation of risk-based manufacturing — hazard identification + dose-response + limit derivation | p40

Original Text — Section 5.3

The foundation of a risk-based approach for manufacturing pharmaceutical products comprises:

  • The identification of hazards
  • Assessment of dose-response relationships
  • Establishment of health-based limits

The identification of hazards and assessment of dose-response relationships are collectively referred to as hazard characterization.

Having an understanding of how a compound can cause harm, and at what dose, is crucial to evaluating risks and deciding on appropriate exposure controls.

The approach used to derive ADE values is consistent with a number of guidance documents on setting health-based exposure limits (e.g., WHO 2005). The EMA guidance document on setting Permitted Daily Exposure limits (PDEs) based on the ICH Q3C(R6) guidance for residual solvents utilizes a similar set of adjustment (assessment) factors. The PDE is essentially synonymous with the ADE.

In practice, the methods used to derive health-based exposure limits may differ slightly, but it is the responsibility of the organization to ensure that all relevant sources of uncertainty and variability are addressed, regardless of which adjustment factor covers them, and that their recommended limits are scientifically defensible and adequately protective.

Commentary

How the Three HBEL Abbreviations Relate

  • HBEL (Health-Based Exposure Limit): the umbrella term for all exposure limits derived from health data.
  • ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure): the term used in ISPE Risk-MaPP, applied to cleaning validation and cross-contamination assessment.
  • PDE (Permitted Daily Exposure): the term used in the EMA guideline and ICH Q3C.

ADE ≈ PDE; the values should be similar, with differences arising from the details of how the adjustment factors are selected.

Regulatory Drivers for the HBEL

EU GMP Annex 15 (2015) requires multi-product facilities to use the HBEL/ADE as the basis for setting cleaning validation limits, replacing the earlier 1/1000 therapeutic dose rule.

The HBEL approach is considered more scientific and more targeted because it takes compound-specific toxicological properties into account rather than applying a uniform safety factor.

Two Components of Hazard Characterization

  • Hazard identification: determine which toxicological endpoints the compound has (carcinogenicity, reproductive toxicity, organ toxicity, etc.).
  • Dose-response assessment: quantify at what dose, and how severe, the effects appear.

Together they define the "Critical Effect" and its corresponding NOAEL, from which the ADE is then calculated.

5.3.1

Data Collection and Analysis

Qualified toxicologist, regulatory data, literature sources | p40–41

Original Text — Section 5.3.1

For hazard identification, a formal review of all available relevant animal and human data should be performed for each compound. This review should be done by a qualified toxicologist. This is an individual that has many years of experience setting health-based limits such as ADE values and OELs, preferably in the pharmaceutical industry. Specific training in toxicology is necessary, whether at MS or PhD level, but degrees in related fields such as pharmacology or medicine may be sufficient when coupled with appropriate specific training and experience.

For the innovator companies that developed the API, the data used in the analysis should include the data submitted in the regulatory filing. Contract Manufacturing Organizations (CMOs) should request this information, and the rationale for the ADE that may have already been derived, from the innovator company asking them to manufacture the drug.

The reviewer should have access to the full range of preclinical and clinical data required for approval of the drug. For non-proprietary compounds, all relevant information on the potential hazards of the material should be obtained through reliable sources. Standard toxicology and pharmacology reference texts should be consulted and a literature search (e.g., on PubMed) should be performed.

Caution should be used when utilizing Safety Data Sheets (SDSs) as a reference source, since the credibility of the information may vary.

The toxicologist performing the hazard assessment and calculating the health-based exposure limit is responsible for assessing the gathered data from the publicly available sources with regard to relevancy, adequacy, and reliability of the collected information.

Commentary

Qualified Toxicologist

The ADE/HBEL must be established by a qualified toxicologist and may not be replaced by general QA or chemistry staff. Qualification requirements:

  • A master's or doctoral degree in toxicology, pharmacology, or a related discipline.
  • Practical experience in setting ADEs/OELs (a pharmaceutical industry background is preferred).
  • DABT (Diplomate of the American Board of Toxicology) or equivalent certification is an advantage but is not mandatory.

A CDMO without an in-house toxicologist should engage a qualified third-party toxicology consultancy.

Priority Order of Data Sources

  1. Highest priority: the regulatory submission documents of the innovator company (the toxicology data package in the NDA/MAA).
  2. Second choice: peer-reviewed literature in PubMed, and FDA/EMA public summaries.
  3. Supplementary: toxicology textbooks and pharmacology references.
  4. Use with caution: SDS (safety data sheets), whose quality varies and which should not be the main toxicological basis.

Special Responsibilities of CMOs/CDMOs

When acting as a contract manufacturer:

  • Proactively request the complete toxicology data package and the established ADE document from the client.
  • If the client cannot provide them, the CMO is responsible for outsourcing the establishment of the ADE itself.
  • EU GMP Annex 15 states explicitly: in a multi-product facility, every compound needs an HBEL, whether handled by an innovator company or a CMO.
5.3.2

Identification of the Critical Effect

First clinically significant adverse effect as dose increases | p41

Original Text — Section 5.3.2

The purpose of a hazard evaluation is to identify all possible hazards associated with a compound and to rank them according to their severity. When combined with a dose-response assessment, the critical effect can be defined. This is typically the first clinically significant adverse effect that is observed as the dose increases.

The critical effect should be used for the derivation of an ADE. Ideally, the critical effect should be derived from studies for the same route that the ADE value will be used to evaluate. Extrapolation from data for other routes may be necessary if relevant route specific data are not available.

Exposures below the NOAEL for the critical effect should not, by definition, lead to other effects, as these will only occur at higher doses.

The reviewing toxicologist should determine which of the effects reported at low dosages is the critical effect. It may be necessary to evaluate several endpoints, which may involve different sets of adjustment factors. A justification should be made considering the final ADE value, which is based on the most sensitive effect relevant to humans.

Due to differences in the route of exposure, the toxicological profile of the compound in question and the potential for local effects at the site of contact, the critical effect may be different for patients and operators.

Professional judgment is required to discern between biological effects that may be physiological or adaptive changes or subclinical pharmacological effects of the drug, which would have associated Lowest-Observed-Effect Levels (LOELs). These biological effects should be distinguished from clinically significant effects which are considered adverse effects.

Clinically significant effects should be considered adverse effects for anyone not under the supervision of a physician prescribing this drug for a specific medical condition. These would have associated Lowest-Observed-Adverse-Effect Level (LOAELs). Quite often, the LOAEL for these effects is used as the Point-of-Departure (PoD) for derivation of the ADE value.

Commentary

Definition of the Critical Effect

Definition: the first clinically significant adverse effect to appear as the dose increases.

Selection logic:

  • Among all documented toxicological effects, find the clinically meaningful effect that appears at the lowest dose.
  • Use the NOAEL of this effect as the starting point for deriving the ADE (Point-of-Departure).
  • In theory, if exposure is below the NOAEL of the critical effect, the other effects (which appear only at higher doses) will not occur either.

The Critical Effect May Differ for Patients and Operators

Oral drugs: the patient's exposure route is oral, and the critical effect may be systemic toxicity.

Manufacturing operators: the main exposure routes are inhalation or skin contact, and the critical effect may be local irritation or respiratory sensitization.

As a result, the OEL (protecting operators) and the ADE (protecting patients) for the same compound may be based on different critical effects and use different combinations of adjustment factors.

Analogy

Choosing the critical effect is like the "weakest member" principle in bridge design: the load-bearing capacity of the whole bridge is set by its most fragile beam. The ADE is set by the most sensitive toxicological endpoint (the NOAEL of the critical effect), not by the average of all effects.

5.3.3

Dose-Response Assessment

Threshold for systemic toxicity vs. no-threshold for genotoxic carcinogens | p41–42

Original Text — Section 5.3.3

During the hazard characterization process, all effects potentially attributed to a compound should be documented. Each effect, whether considered adverse or not, has a dose-response relationship. In general, as the dose increases, the incidence and severity of the effect increases.

For systemic toxicity endpoints, there is a dose below which no effects are expected. This is often referred to as the threshold for toxicity.

For some endpoints such as cancer, due to direct damage to DNA, it is often assumed that a threshold does not exist and some effects (e.g., mutations) are possible at low doses. In this case, it is necessary to determine the acceptable level of response for the effect (e.g., a 1/100,000 excess cancer risk). It should be noted that this may be viewed as a conservative approach given that there are DNA repair mechanisms that may reverse the mutations and immunosurveillance mechanisms that prevent cancers from developing.

Commentary

Two Models of the Dose-Response Relationship

Threshold Model: applies to most toxicological endpoints (hepatotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, reproductive toxicity). A NOAEL exists, below which there is no adverse effect. The ADE is calculated using the safety factor approach (adjustment factor method).

Linear No-Threshold Model: applies to genotoxic carcinogens. In theory, any dose carries some risk (a single DNA lesion may lead to a mutation). The limit is derived from an "acceptable risk level", such as an additional cancer risk of 1 in 100,000. ICH M7 uses this approach.

Analogy: Threshold vs. Non-Threshold

Threshold toxicity: like the capacity of a cup, where a small amount of drink will not overflow (below the NOAEL = no effect).

Non-threshold carcinogenicity: like dice, where every throw has some chance of showing a 6 (every DNA lesion has some chance of becoming a mutation). The lower the dose, the smaller the risk, but it is never zero.

Practical Significance

  • General APIs (with NOAEL data): calculate the ADE using the adjustment factor method and design the cleaning limits from it.
  • Genotoxic compounds (such as alkylating agents and certain API impurities): use linear extrapolation, with TDI = 1.5 µg/day as the lifetime exposure benchmark (ICH M7).
  • The existence of DNA repair mechanisms makes the no-threshold assumption conservative, but because each individual's repair capacity cannot be quantified accurately, regulators still default to the conservative value.
5.3.4

Establishment of the NOAEL or Benchmark Dose

NOAEL definition, LOAEL limitation, BMD as mathematical alternative | p42

Original Text — Section 5.3.4

For most toxicity endpoints, there is a clear dose-response relationship for the critical effect. The aim is to define the NOAEL for the critical effect, which may be used to derive the ADE. The application of adjustment factors (see Section 5.3.5) results in ADE values that are unlikely to produce any undesirable compound-related effects.

It may not be possible to define the NOAEL for some toxicology data sets, and therefore, it may be necessary to derive the health-based limit using a LOAEL. This occurs when statistically significant adverse effects are observed at the lowest dose used in a study. The Benchmark Dose (BMD) can be used in this situation.

The BMD is a value, equivalent to a NOAEL, that is derived mathematically by fitting a curve to the dose-response data for the critical effect. The BMD10 is defined as the dose that corresponds to a 10% response rate, the minimum level of response typically detected by standard toxicology studies. The statistical lower bound on the BMD10 (BMDL10) is often used in risk assessments as a surrogate for a NOAEL. Caution should be taken when extrapolating well below the observable range.

Commentary

The Relationship Between BMD and NOAEL

When statistically significant adverse effects already appear in the lowest dose group (that is, there is only a LOAEL and no NOAEL):

Option 1: use the LOAEL as the PoD and compensate by adding an AFL (LOAEL-to-NOAEL factor) = 3.

Option 2: use BMD software (US EPA BMDS) to fit a curve to the dose-response data and take the BMDL10 as the equivalent NOAEL.

BMDL10 ≈ NOAEL equivalent (no AFL needed)

The BMD method is mathematically more rigorous, but it needs enough dose-group data to fit the curve.

Why Is the NOAEL Limited?

The NOAEL depends on study design: if a study has only 3 dose groups with wide spacing, the NOAEL may overestimate the no-effect dose. The BMD method uses mathematical modeling to reflect the dose-response relationship more precisely, independent of the dose-group design.

5.3.5

Application of Adjustment Factors — ADE/PDE Calculation

Interspecies, intraspecies, duration, severity, LOAEL conversion, PK adjustments | p42–47

Original Text — Section 5.3.5

Health-based exposure limits, such as ADE values, are derived by dividing the NOAEL for the critical effect (after adjusting for body weight, e.g., 50 kg) by various adjustment factors (also termed assessment, safety, or uncertainty factors) to extrapolate to the "true" no effect level in the subpopulation of interest (e.g., elderly patients).

These are multiplied together to form a Composite Adjustment Factor (AFC) that is used in the denominator of the general equation used to set health-based limits.

ADE Calculation Formula (ISPE Risk-MaPP):
ADE (mg/day) = (PoD × BW) / (AFC × MF × PK)
PDE Calculation Formula (EMA):
PDE = (NOAEL × BW) / (F1 × F2 × F3 × F4 × F5)

Key adjustment factors:

  • AFA / F1 (Interspecies): Extrapolates from animal NOAEL to human. Allometric scaling, values 2–12 depending on species.
  • AFH / F2 (Intraspecies): Accounts for variability within the human population (kinetics + dynamics). Default = 10.
  • AFS / F3 (Study Duration): Adjusts when study duration is shorter than exposure scenario. Typically 3 (Risk-MaPP) or 10 (EMA, studies <4 weeks).
  • AFL / F5 (LOAEL-to-NOAEL): Applied when only LOAEL is available. Default = 3.
  • AFD (Database Completeness): Missing DART studies. Default = 3.
  • MF / F4 (Modifying Factor / Severity): Residual uncertainties, severity of effect, slope of dose-response. Range: <1 to 10.
  • PK (Pharmacokinetic): Bioavailability correction for route-to-route extrapolation.

Table 5.2: Adjustment Factors Used in EMA Guidance and Risk-MaPP

Commentary

Understanding the ADE/PDE Calculation Formula

ISPE Risk-MaPP (ADE)：

ADE = (PoD × BW) / (AFC × MF × PK)

EMA Guideline (PDE)：

PDE = (NOAEL × BW) / (F1 × F2 × F3 × F4 × F5)

Meaning of each parameter:

  • PoD / NOAEL: the no-observed-adverse-effect dose for the critical effect (mg/kg/day)
  • BW: standard patient body weight, usually 50 kg (taking the most vulnerable population into account)
  • AFC: composite adjustment factor = AFA × AFH × AFS × AFL × AFD
  • MF: modifying factor, addressing residual uncertainty and severity of effect
  • PK: pharmacokinetic adjustment, such as bioavailability correction

Calculation example (simplified):

NOAEL = 5 mg/kg/day (rat 90-day oral study)
BW = 50 kg
AFA = 5 (rat → human, allometric)
AFH = 10 (inter-individual human variability)
AFS = 3 (90 days → lifetime)
AFL = 1 (NOAEL available)
AFD = 1 (DART studies complete)
MF  = 1 (no additional uncertainty)
PK  = 1 (oral → oral, no correction needed)

AFC = 5 × 10 × 3 × 1 × 1 = 150
ADE = (5 × 50) / (150 × 1 × 1) = 250/150 ≈ 1.67 mg/day

Risk-MaPP vs. EMA: Main Differences

Study duration factor (F3/AFS): for studies shorter than 4 weeks, the EMA uses F3 = 10, while Risk-MaPP uses AFS = 3. Risk-MaPP draws on a broader body of empirical literature and considers 3 sufficiently protective.

Severity factor (F4/MF): the EMA handles this separately in F4 and F5, while Risk-MaPP handles residual uncertainty together in the MF. Care is needed to avoid double counting.

Result: for the same compound, the ADE/PDE from the two approaches should be similar, though the specific values may differ. Regulators generally accept either approach as long as there is adequate scientific justification.

Compound-Specific Adjustment Factor (CSAF)

When specific PK/PD data are available, a CSAF can replace the default 10-fold inter-individual variability factor (AFH).

The CSAF is calculated from the statistical distribution of measured data as (mean + 2 standard deviations) / mean.

It is particularly useful during new drug development (with abundant PK data), making the ADE more precise and avoiding over-engineered manufacturing controls.

A Practical Scenario for Bioavailability Correction

If the NOAEL comes from an oral study (oral bioavailability = 50%), but the cross-contamination risk for an intravenous product (100% bioavailability) must be assessed:

ADE_IV = ADE_oral × (oral BA / IV BA) = ADE_oral × (50% / 100%) = ADE_oral / 2

The ADE for the intravenous product must be halved, making the cleaning limit stricter. This is especially important when oral and intravenous products are made on a shared line (for example, a CDMO producing multiple dosage forms in the same facility).

5.4

Setting Occupational Exposure Limits (OEL)

Worker protection vs. patient protection — different targets, same toxicological data | p48–49

Original Text — Section 5.4

The primary purpose of this Chapter is to describe methods to set ADE values for patient protection; however, similar quantitative health-based assessments are performed to set OELs to protect workers handling these substances.

The safe level of exposure for patients may be different from that established for workers. This is because the critical effect used to derive the limits may be different due to differences in the anticipated route of exposure, and even where this is the same, the choice of adjustment factors may differ. Workers are generally healthy individuals, whereas patients may be children or elderly, and by definition, have at least one active medical condition.

Adjustments may be necessary to address route-to-route extrapolation, even if the same critical effect is used. For example, an OEL derived from the ADE may need to be adjusted for differences in bioavailability between the route used to identify the critical effect (e.g., oral) and the anticipated route of exposure (e.g., inhalation).

If the same assumptions apply regarding the critical effect, susceptible subpopulations, and bioavailability (e.g., 100%), an OEL can be calculated directly from the ADE by dividing the dose allowed by the volume of air breathed by a worker, typically 10 m³, engaged in light work for 8 hours:

OEL (µg/m³) = ADE (µg/day) / 10 m³

Surface target values (e.g., wipe limits), which are used for worker protection, can also be derived by dividing the dose permitted in 8 hours at the OEL (i.e., OEL × 10 m³) by a standard surface area (e.g., 100 cm²).

While it may be tempting to use wipe limits to assess the potential for cross-contamination, it is important to emphasize that they are developed for worker safety purposes. Any other use must be done within the context of a risk assessment for patient safety (cross-contamination) when considering specific plausible pathways of exposure to product.

Commentary

Converting Between OEL and ADE

Assumptions: same critical effect, same adjustment factors, inhalation bioavailability = 100%

OEL (µg/m³) = ADE (µg/day) ÷ 10 m³

Example: ADE = 100 µg/day

OEL = 100 ÷ 10 = 10 µg/m³

Surface wipe limit (Wipe Limit, used for workplace protection):

Wipe Limit = OEL × 10 m³ ÷ 100 cm² = ADE ÷ 100 cm²

Note: this wipe limit is intended for industrial hygiene and is not the same as the residue limit for cleaning validation.

Key Distinction: Wipe Limit ≠ Cleaning Validation Limit

Wipe limit (industrial hygiene Wipe Limit): evaluates the external surfaces of equipment to ensure that the operator's contact environment is not contaminated. Purpose: protect workers.

Cleaning validation residue limit (Cleaning Validation Limit): evaluates residues on product contact surfaces to ensure that subsequent batches do not expose patients above the ADE. Purpose: protect patients.

The two have similar origins but different contexts. Using an industrial hygiene wipe limit to judge whether cleaning validation passes is the wrong approach.

Why Might OEL and ADE Values Differ?

  • Different critical effects: operators are mainly exposed by inhalation, so the critical effect may be lung irritation; patients take the drug orally, so the critical effect may be hepatotoxicity.
  • Different target populations: the OEL protects healthy adults (with fewer adjustments), while the ADE protects patients, including sensitive populations (with more conservative adjustment factors).
  • Different bioavailability: oral absorption and inhalation absorption can differ greatly and each needs its own correction.
5.5

Documentation of the Health-Based Exposure Limit

Formal written document requirements for ADE/HBEL assessment | p49

Original Text — Section 5.5

The scientific rationale for the recommended health-based limit (e.g., ADE) should be summarized in a formal written document. The document should include a summary to facilitate review by stakeholders and the basis for the ADE should be clearly described.

The reader should be able to readily determine:

  • The health endpoint (critical effect) on which the ADE was established
  • The values chosen for adjustment factors
  • Which sources of variability and uncertainty they address
  • Any further adjustments (e.g., bioavailability correction)

The format of the documentation of the ADE does not need to be standardized; however, it should be used consistently within a given company. The documentation should include sections addressing the following:

  1. Identity information, physico-chemical properties and chemical structure
  2. Intended use and mechanism of action
  3. Pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics in animals and humans
  4. Animal data including acute toxicity, local effects, repeat dose toxicity, developmental and reproductive toxicity, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity
  5. Veterinary use if applicable
  6. Human data including clinical use, adverse reactions, susceptible subpopulations, pregnancy and nursing mothers
  7. Identification of the critical effect
  8. References
  9. Qualifications of the expert author (e.g., toxicologist)
  10. Appendices with detailed calculations of ADE value, chemical-specific adjustment factors, etc.

The level of detail in the documentation should be commensurate with the level of hazard and risk associated with the specific compound.

Commentary

Purpose of the HBEL Document

An ADE/HBEL document is not merely a calculation report but a toxicological scientific argument, used to:

  • Support the scientific basis for setting cleaning validation residue limits.
  • Be reviewed by regulators (required by EU GMP Annex 15).
  • Serve as the basis for facility classification and engineering control decisions.
  • Provide a transparent data trail during audits or customer technology transfers.

The 10 Required Sections of an ADE Document

  1. Compound identification: CAS number, molecular formula, structural formula, physicochemical properties (solubility, volatility, particle size distribution)
  2. Use and mechanism of action: indications, pharmacological mechanism, therapeutic dose range
  3. PK/PD data: absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and bioavailability in animals and humans
  4. Animal toxicology data: acute toxicity, repeat-dose toxicity (28-day/90-day/chronic), developmental and reproductive toxicity, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity
  5. Veterinary use: for veterinary APIs, explain the impact on human food safety
  6. Human data: clinical trials, post-marketing adverse reaction reports, data on sensitive populations
  7. Critical effect identification: rationale for selection, endpoint, NOAEL/LOAEL values
  8. References: all cited study sources
  9. Author qualifications: the toxicologist's education, certifications, and relevant experience
  10. Appendices: the full calculation, rationale for selecting adjustment factors, and compound-specific data

Level of Documentation Detail = Risk Level

The guide states explicitly that the level of detail in the document should be commensurate with the hazard and risk level of the compound.

  • Low-hazard compounds (ADE > 1 mg/day): a concise document is sufficient, highlighting the key data points and the ADE calculation.
  • High-hazard compounds (ADE < 10 µg/day): a full document, which must include detailed analysis of all toxicological endpoints and adequate scientific justification.
  • Genotoxic/carcinogenic compounds: must also include an explanation of the linear extrapolation method and the cancer risk calculation.

Practical Reflection: How Does Your Organization Manage ADEs Today?

  • Does the company have a qualified toxicologist in-house, or is ADE assessment entirely outsourced?
  • Does every compound manufactured on site have a written ADE document?
  • Is the ADE document updated regularly (for example, when new toxicology studies are published or indications are expanded)?
  • Can the cleaning validation residue limits be traced back to the ADE document?
  • When a customer asks you to manufacture a new compound, is the ADE document a deliverable in the commercial contract?

Putting the EU GMP Annex 15 Documentation Requirements into Practice

The 2015 edition of Annex 15 states explicitly: cleaning validation in a multi-product facility must be based on a toxicological assessment (that is, the ADE/HBEL document).

Inspection points: inspectors may ask to see:

  • The ADE document (signed by the toxicologist)
  • The calculation trail from the ADE to the residue limit
  • Proof of the toxicologist's qualifications
  • Version control and periodic review records of the document
∑

Section Summary

Chapters 4–5 key concepts consolidated | p37–52

Chapter 4–5 Key Concepts at a Glance
Key Takeaways

From Hazard to Limit: The Complete Derivation Chain

Hazard identification (which compounds have which toxicological endpoints)

↓ Dose-response assessment (identify the NOAEL / LOAEL)

↓ Select the critical effect (the most sensitive adverse effect appearing at the lowest dose)

↓ Apply adjustment factors (AFA × AFH × AFS × AFL × AFD × MF × PK)

↓ Calculate the ADE/PDE (PoD × BW / composite adjustment factor)

↓ Derive the cleaning validation residue limit (calculated from equipment surface area, batch size, and dose)

↓ Written documentation (signed by a qualified toxicologist, updated regularly)

The Parallel Relationship Between HBEL and OEL

Same basis: both derive from toxicological data (NOAEL, dose-response).

Different purposes: the HBEL protects patients (daily intake), while the OEL protects workers (airborne concentration).

Conversion relationship: OEL ≈ ADE / 10 m³ (a worker's 8-hour breathing volume), but differences in pathway and population must be taken into account.

Regulatory Expectations (EU GMP Annex 15)

  • Cleaning validation limits for all multi-product facilities must be based on the HBEL/ADE.
  • The ADE document is established and signed by a qualified toxicologist.
  • The document must be available for review and be periodically re-examined.
  • Facility design (containment strategy) should be consistent with the ADE/OEL level.

  • Understand the ICH Q9 risk analysis framework: probability × severity × detectability

  • Distinguish when to use qualitative versus quantitative risk analysis tools
