  • Understand airborne dustiness and the concept of the CPT
6

Chapter 6: Risk Analysis

ICH Q9 Framework: Probability × Severity × Detectability | p53–p54

6 Risk Analysis

ICH Q9 defines risk analysis as the estimation of the risk associated with the identified hazards. As the hazard is inherent to the API and other process chemicals, the exposure to the hazard is the variable that needs to be analyzed to determine the relative risks.

This Chapter describes risk analysis, which is closely related to exposure assessment, i.e., the process by which exposures are assessed, either qualitatively or quantitatively, to estimate the risk associated with the identified hazards.

The risk analysis looks at the modes of exposure such as:

  • Mix-up
  • Retention
  • Mechanical transfer
  • Airborne transfer

...for the product, as well as inhalation, dermal, ingestion, and mechanical transfer for workers, while considering the question "What could go wrong?"

The identified hazard also should be analyzed in order to answer the questions:

  • "What is the likelihood (probability) it will go wrong?"
  • "What are the consequences (severity)?"
  • "Can the failure be detected?"

Commentary

The Three Elements of ICH Q9 Risk Analysis

Risk analysis is the core step of the ICH Q9 risk management process. Three key questions form the assessment framework:

  • Probability: How likely is the failure to occur?
  • Severity: How serious is the impact of the failure on the patient?
  • Detectability: Can the failure be detected before harm occurs?

The combination of the three elements determines the final risk priority number (RPN) and guides the intensity of the control strategy.

Hazard vs. Exposure — the Core Distinction

An important concept in this guide: the hazard is inherent. It is determined by the toxicological properties of the API and cannot be changed. But exposure is a controllable variable. Risk analysis focuses on understanding and managing exposure routes, not just on the size of the hazard.

Example: a highly toxic compound (high hazard) handled in a closed system has very low exposure, so the overall risk remains controllable.

Analogy: a weapon vs. the path to the target

Hazard is like the sharpness of a knife: a fixed property. What really determines the risk of injury is whether the knife can reach the body (the exposure route). Risk analysis is the search for, and blocking of, every "path by which the knife reaches the body".

The Four Cross-Contamination Routes (Key Framework)

ISPE Vol.7 divides cross-contamination into four categories, ranked from highest to lowest importance:

  1. Mix-up — the most common reason for regulatory action
  2. Retention — the route directly addressed by cleaning validation
  3. Mechanical Transfer — physical contact via non-product-contact surfaces
  4. Airborne Transfer — migration of airborne particles
6.1

6.1 Holistic Approach to Risk Analysis

Multidisciplinary teams, competing requirements, GxP + IH balance | p54–p55

6.1 Holistic Approach to Risk Analysis

While the focus of ICH Q9 is understood, appropriate risk analysis should recognize that the production of medicines requires that a number of potentially conflicting requirements need to be met, which may impact each other, e.g.:

  • Cross-contamination (focus on patient safety)
  • Industrial Hygiene (IH) (focus on worker safety)
  • Process safety
  • Production demands

Therefore, effective risk analysis requires a shared understanding to achieve the balance between these different requirements.

Multidisciplinary Team

A multidisciplinary approach is beneficial when drawing on expertise from several specialist areas and provides a holistic and balanced approach. This should include:

  • Toxicologists
  • Industrial hygienists
  • Engineers
  • Quality managers
  • Users or operating personnel
  • Independent facilitators

GxP vs. Industrial Hygiene

Although GxP and IH exposure assessments are linked, the application and interpretation of the criteria in the assessment process are different.

High Hazard ≠ High Risk

It should be noted that more hazardous compounds will not necessarily lead to higher potential for exposure. Some reasons that more hazardous compounds are not necessarily high risk of cross-contamination:

  • Higher hazard compounds tend to be a smaller percentage of the final product
  • Processed in more closed systems than lower hazard products
  • Controls tend to be more robust

The exposure potential may not be the same for manufacture of the same compound and the same process in a different location as there are other variables such as worker behavior.

Commentary

The Core Spirit of the Holistic Approach

Risk analysis cannot look at only one dimension, "cross-contamination → patient safety". It must also consider:

  • Patient safety (GxP): control of cross-contamination risk
  • Employee safety (IH): occupational exposure limits (OEL/TLV)
  • Process safety: explosion protection, corrosion, high pressure, etc.
  • Production efficiency: controls cannot be tightened indefinitely at the expense of efficiency

These four dimensions sometimes conflict and need to be balanced. This is what "holistic" means.

Why a Cross-Functional Risk Team Is Needed

ISPE stresses that risk analysis needs a multidisciplinary team, because:

  • Toxicologist: sets the ADE/OEL and determines hazard severity
  • Industrial hygienist: assesses occupational exposure and performs air sampling
  • Engineer: equipment design, containment, cleanability
  • Quality manager: GMP compliance requirements
  • Operator: knows front-line reality best
  • Independent facilitator: avoids departmental blind spots and asks challenging questions

Key Concept: High Hazard ≠ High Risk

This is a counter-intuitive but critical concept in this guide:

  • Highly toxic compounds (such as antineoplastics) are usually dosed very low and make up a very small share of the final product
  • They are often manufactured in highly closed systems
  • Because they receive so much attention, the controls are often stricter

By contrast, a low-toxicity excipient handled in an open system with unvalidated cleaning may carry a higher cross-contamination risk.

Conclusion: risk = hazard × exposure; you cannot look at hazard alone.

Analogy: GxP and IH — the same ruler with different scales

A GxP assessment is concerned with "the amount of residue-containing drug product that enters the patient"; an IH assessment is concerned with "the amount employees inhale or absorb through the skin at work". The two use similar methodologies (ADE derivation), but the acceptance criteria and assessment scenarios are completely different, so they must not be mixed.

Practical Application in Taiwanese Pharma Plants

In most small and mid-sized plants, risk assessments are done by QA alone, without cross-functional input. We recommend forming a risk assessment team with fixed members from production management, engineering, QA and EHS (environment, health and safety), and periodically inviting an external consultant to review. This is exactly the Holistic Approach that ISPE advocates.

6.2

6.2 Tools for Risk Analysis

Qualitative & Quantitative Methods, Air Sampling | p55–p57

6.2 Tools

Fundamentally, risk analysis should incorporate both qualitative and quantitative processes to link the likelihood of occurrence with the severity of harm. The tools should be applied in a manner that is commensurate with the risks.

In order to have a robust assessment, it is important to use actual data from the facility such as incident reports, cleaning and cleaning validation results, results from protocol studies, and regulatory action, to assist in categorizing the risk being assessed.

The analysis will typically focus on a defined part of the process, e.g., charging a vessel or a particular unit operation such as compression. The entire process should be reviewed (including unexpected operation situations such as breach of containment, or a spill).

6.2.1 Qualitative Risk Analysis and Prioritization

Qualitative risk analysis is a process that can be used to assess the risk of exposure and prioritize subsequent quantitative activities. Formats include, but are not limited to:

  • Flowcharts
  • Check sheets
  • Process mapping
  • Risk ranking and filtering
  • Cause and effect diagrams

A robust qualitative risk analysis process should incorporate:

  • Hazard characterization using ADE/OEL/TLV® data
  • Physical characteristics of the pharmaceutical compound
  • Definition of unit operations to be assessed including cleaning
  • Observation of operations and discussions with personnel
  • Review of similar existing unit operations, vendor input, benchmarking
  • Exposure profile including identification of plausible pathways
  • Checks that existing control measures are functioning properly

6.2.2 Quantitative Risk Analysis

Based on the results of the qualitative risk analysis, it may be necessary to perform a quantitative risk analysis involving the collection and evaluation of data. The quantitative analysis process provides an objective and empirical basis regarding risk potential.

Statistical indices such as Process Capability Index (Cpk) or Process Performance Index (Ppk) can be used to evaluate cleaning residue data against upper limits. For cleaning data, which is typically non-normally distributed, the Process Performance Index (Ppk) is a preferred technique.

6.2.2.1 Air Sampling

Workplace air sampling is normally conducted by industrial hygienists as part of an ongoing worker health protection monitoring program. Similar air sampling techniques may also be useful as part of an initial risk assessment for Quality Assurance purposes to help demonstrate that material is being adequately contained.

There is no regulatory requirement to perform air sampling as part of GxP. Containment should routinely be proven through qualified and measurable physical parameters, such as pressure differentials and airflow volumes and direction.

Commentary

Cpk vs. Ppk — Application in Cleaning Validation

Cpk (Process Capability Index): based on short-term process data, assumes a normal distribution.

Ppk (Process Performance Index): based on long-term actual data; better suited to non-normal distributions.

Cleaning residue data are usually skewed (right-skewed), because most cleaning results are very low (close to the LOD) with occasional high values. ISPE therefore recommends using Ppk, or first applying a non-normal transformation (such as Weibull or log transformation) before calculating.

The Dual Role and Limits of Air Sampling

Air sampling in a pharma plant serves two completely different purposes:

  • IH purpose (led by the industrial hygienist): ensure personal exposure of employees is < OEL; sampling points are near the personnel breathing zone
  • GxP/QA purpose (led by quality assurance): confirm that material is contained within the process/room; sampling points are outside the room or facility

ISPE states explicitly that IH monitoring data should not be used directly for quantitative assessment of product cross-contamination risk, because the sampling location and purpose are entirely different.

Analogy: supporting qualitative analysis with real data

Qualitative risk analysis is like a doctor taking a history: a quick way to find likely problems. But if it relies only on "feel" or "theory", without citing the plant's real cleaning validation data, deviation records and inspection results, the conclusions stay superficial. ISPE emphasizes: a good qualitative analysis must be rooted in real in-plant data.

Practical Advice: Focusing the Risk Analysis

ISPE cautions against trying to assess the whole process at once, which makes the analysis too complex and loses focus. The right approach:

  • Work by unit operation (UO) (e.g. filling, tablet compression, cleaning)
  • Assess one at a time, with each UO producing clear conclusions and actions
  • Do not overlook abnormal situations, such as leaks, spills and equipment failure

Qualitative vs. Quantitative Risk Analysis — When to Use Each

The two analyses complement each other; usually the qualitative analysis is done first to decide whether a quantitative one is needed

6.3

6.3 Routes for Cross-Contamination — Overview

Four routes ordered by importance | p57

6.3 Routes for Cross-Contamination

The routes for cross-contamination within pharmaceutical processing activities are presented in order of importance. Figure 6.2🔍 contrasts the risk of cross-contamination and worker exposure risk.

The four routes are:

  1. Mix-up (§6.3.1)
  2. Retention (§6.3.2)
  3. Mechanical Transfer (§6.3.3)
  4. Airborne Transfer (§6.3.4)

Note that the ranking of risk to the product from cross-contamination is essentially the inverse of the ranking of risk to the worker: airborne transfer poses the highest risk to workers (inhalation), while mix-up and retention are the predominant concerns for product cross-contamination.

Commentary

Key Insight: Why the Two Risk Rankings Are Opposite

This inverse relationship comes from physical principles:

  • Mix-up and retention involve bulk, direct material transfer, which affects the product more than particles diluted in air
  • Airborne transfer poses the greatest hazard to workers, who inhale dust for long periods near the source; but its effect on product in distant closed containers is limited
  • This also explains why Mix-up is the most common cause of regulatory action: it is a direct contamination event involving a large quantity

Risk Ranking Matrix of the Four Routes

The ranking of product cross-contamination risk and the ranking of worker exposure risk are almost opposite

6.3.1

6.3.1 Mix-up

Most common cause of regulatory action / product recall | p55–p57

Route 1 — Mix-up

6.3.1 Mix-up

Mix-up is frequently cited by regulators as one of the most common causes for regulatory action/product recall. For the purpose of this Guide, mix-up refers to the contamination at unsafe levels of one product with another.

The highest risks from mix-up normally stem from low-tech GxP failures due to:

  • Basic human error (e.g., failure to follow procedure)
  • System weakness (e.g., poor material labeling)

Obviously, there is an increased potential risk of cross-contamination if two or more products are being handled in the same area, even with appropriate batch to batch segregation.

GxP Review Areas for Mix-up Prevention

The risk analysis to identify potential cross-contamination routes should include a review of basic GxP to confirm that appropriate systems provide a high level of assurance that mix-up will not occur, such as:

  • Identification and status labeling/controls of materials and products
  • Status labeling/controls of equipment and facilities
  • Receipt and controls of materials and products
  • Physical segregation, process flow, and security during material and product handling, storage, and staging

Typical Examples of Mix-up

  • Inadequate line clearance between products
  • Accidental use of dirty equipment
  • Introduction of another product during sampling
  • Incorporation of the wrong starting material or excipient
  • Mislabeling of equipment and/or materials
  • Unintended transfer of materials from one vessel to another containing different product/materials

Human Error Related Factors

  • Operator training and understanding of process requirements
  • Ergonomic requirements of activities against operator capabilities
  • Local environment (lights, noise, and heat)
  • Operator workload (too busy, bored)
  • Level of routine involved in an activity
  • Local culture, e.g., is procedural innovation discouraged?
  • Overly complex procedures
  • Ease of error, e.g., a function of design, can an error be made and how easily?

6.3.1.1 Factors Affecting Exposure by Mix-up

The presence of multiple processes within a common facility with shared or overlapping process flows may give rise to an increased risk of mix-up through increased potential for mistakes in materials management processes.

Factors to be considered when assessing the risk of mix-up within the process include:

  • Overlapping process flows and transit routes
  • Common dispensary areas
  • Common storage areas for change parts

Commentary

Why Is Mix-up the Most Common Reason for Regulatory Action?

Mix-up needs no complex scientific mechanism: it is a direct, bulk admixture of material. When it happens, the amount of contamination often far exceeds the ADE limit, and it is usually found late in the process (or even after release), with serious consequences. Typical cases include:

  • Raw materials of similar color used by mistake
  • Containers with detached labels or illegible writing
  • Incomplete line clearance
  • Materials of different products mixed together in the warehouse

Two Root Causes of Mix-up: Human Error vs. System Weakness

The 8 influencing factors of human error deserve careful thought:

  • Training: does the operator truly understand the process requirements?
  • Human factors engineering: are the movements ergonomic?
  • Environment: poor lighting and loud noise increase the error rate
  • Workload: being too busy (high pressure) or too idle (lapsing attention) are both risks
  • Degree of routine: the more routine an operation, the more likely it is run on autopilot with details overlooked
  • Culture: if employees fear punishment and do not report errors, errors accumulate
  • Procedural complexity: an overly complex SOP actually increases the chance of error
  • Fault tolerance of the design: good design prevents error (error-proofing)

Line Clearance

Line clearance is the most critical GxP control for preventing mix-up. Standard practice includes:

  • Remove all materials, labels and documents from the previous batch
  • Two-person verification (four-eyes principle)
  • Complete and record the Line Clearance Checklist
  • Update the equipment status label to "Cleaned" + next product information

Common inspection finding: line clearance is a formality, or signatures are meaningless.

Analogy: Shared Space and the Odds of a "Collision"

Picture several products moving through the plant like traffic in a city. Shared warehouses, shared weighing rooms and crossing logistics routes are like adding more intersections: each crossing point is a potential collision (mix-up) point. The most effective prevention is to design dedicated flow paths rather than rely on every driver (operator) staying highly alert at all times.

Exercise: Where Are the High-Risk Mix-up Points in Your Plant?

Review the scenarios below; every "shared" item is a potential mix-up point:

  • Shared weighing room (several products weighed at the same time)
  • Shared buffer corridor (materials of different batches moved at the same time)
  • Shared gowning room (gowning, then entry into different process areas)
  • Shared parts storage area (change parts not cleaned thoroughly)
6.3.2

6.3.2 Retention

Cleaning validation, ADE-based limits, cleaning process lifecycle | p57–p65

Route 2 — Retention

6.3.2 Retention

Retention is defined as residual material on product contact surfaces after cleaning that can carryover from one product to another product in the same equipment, having been used in a sequential or campaign manner.

Retention is the mode of cross-contamination directly addressed by the cleaning program which includes cleaning validation.

Development and validation of cleaning procedures that consistently reduce residuals, both:

  • Intrinsic (product/process materials)
  • Non-intrinsic (such as cleaning agents)

...on product contact equipment to acceptable levels is critical to the success of a cross-contamination control program.

6.3.2.1 Health-Based Acceptance Limits for Cleaning

Basing the acceptance criteria on a health-based limit such as the ADE (or PDE) value has many advantages over traditional methods (e.g., 1/1000th of the low clinical dose).

The most important is the fact that the ADE value is toxicologically derived.

Calculation of Health-Based Cleaning Limits:

Swab Residue Level (mg/swab) =

(ADE × SB × TA × RF) / (MDD × SSA)

Rinse Residue Level (mg/L) =

(ADE × SB × RA × RF) / (V × MDD × SSA)

Where: ADE = Acceptable Daily Exposure, MDD = Maximum Daily Dose, SB = Smallest batch size, SSA = Shared Surface Area, TA = Test Area, RA = Rinsed Area, RF = Recovery Factor, V = Volume of Rinse

6.3.2.3 Use of 1/1000th and 10 ppm

The use of traditional acceptance limits is not the preferred strategy. Ideally, compound specific, scientifically derived, health-based values should be established whenever possible.

  • 1/1000th method is often overprotective for most APIs → lowers apparent margin of safety
  • For highly hazardous drugs (e.g., antineoplastic), 1/1000th method may not be sufficiently protective
  • 10 ppm is arbitrary and not applicable to all compounds

The health-based limit should not be seen as a limit in the true sense, but as a reference point for determining the level of risk. Equipment must always be visually clean.

6.3.2.11 Factors Affecting Exposure by Retention

Equipment-related factors:

  • Large surface areas for potential product contact
  • Frequency of changeover and cleaning
  • Use of disposable equipment/product contact parts
  • Crevices or ledges where product can accumulate (occluded areas)
  • Rough or unpolished product contact surfaces
  • Drains
  • Material compatibility with preferred cleaning agents

Material-related factors increasing residue risk:

  • Sticky or caked-on solids
  • Poor solubility or wettability with available cleaning materials
  • Materials that dry with hard surfaces
  • Highly viscous or shear thickening materials
  • Materials that react with common cleaning solutions to form solid or sticky gel-like materials

Commentary

The Precise Definition of Retention

Retention refers specifically to material that remains on equipment surfaces after cleaning and is carried into the next product in subsequent batches. Key characteristics:

  • Involves only product contact surfaces
  • Occurs in sequential/campaign production
  • The cleaning procedure is the direct means of control
  • Covers both "inherent" residues (API, excipients) and "non-inherent" residues (cleaning agents)

Interpreting the ADE-Based Cleaning Limit Formula

Swab residue limit (mg/swab) =
  ADE × minimum batch size × sampling area × recovery
  ───────────────────────────────────────
      maximum daily dose × shared contact area

Rinse residue limit (mg/L) =
  ADE × minimum batch size × rinse area × recovery
  ───────────────────────────────────────
    rinse volume × maximum daily dose × shared contact area

Key logic: the calculation is based on "the amount of previous-product ADE allowed to carry over into the maximum daily dose of the next batch product", ensuring that even a patient taking the maximum dose ingests previous-product residue within the safe range.

"Visually Clean" Is the Minimum Standard, Not the Only Standard

Important clarification: the residue limit calculated from the ADE is sometimes higher than the visible amount. This does not mean it is acceptable for equipment to "look unclean".

Rule: visually clean is the minimum requirement in all cases.

Visual inspection can be the sole criterion for routine release only when the ADE limit is significantly higher than the visual detection limit (a sufficient safety margin). This requires a prior study to confirm the Visual Detection Limit (swab screening method).

Hard-to-Clean Equipment Locations (Worst-Case Locations)

The core of cleaning validation is to find the hardest-to-clean locations (worst case):

  • CIP systems: flow shadow zones and shielded areas
  • Manual cleaning: hard-to-reach locations (such as the inside of a ball valve)
  • Equipment design flaws: dead legs, crevices, unpolished surfaces, drains

If the hardest locations can be cleaned, all other locations will necessarily be clean. This is the basic principle of small-scale cleanability models.

Analogy: Cleaning Validation ≈ the Process Validation Lifecycle

ISPE draws an explicit analogy: cleaning validation runs completely parallel to the three-stage FDA process validation lifecycle:

  • Stage 1 (Process Design) = cleaning process development, risk assessment
  • Stage 2 (Process Qualification) = CIP equipment qualification + 3 cleaning validation runs
  • Stage 3 (Continued Process Verification) = routine monitoring, trending (Ppk), investigation of abnormal results

The notion that "validation is complete after 3 PQ runs" is outdated; an ongoing monitoring program must be established.

6.3.3

6.3.3 Mechanical Transfer

Contaminated non-product contact surfaces, operator apparel, equipment routing | p65–p66

Route 3 — Mechanical Transfer

6.3.3 Mechanical Transfer

Mechanical transfer includes all routes by which material can be transferred from contaminated non-product contact surfaces into or onto the product. This includes:

  • Product contact surfaces contaminated by contact with contaminated surfaces (e.g., in cleaning areas)
  • Inadvertent or transient contact with other contaminated non-designated product contact areas
  • Direct contact of the product with operator apparel and gloves

The major issue with such routes is the lack of control of contaminated items.

The factors which affect the risk of mechanical transfer are made up of those which influence the:

  • Transfer of the material to the surface
  • Association of the surface with the product at risk
  • Release of the material from the contaminated surface to the product

6.3.3.1 Factors Affecting Exposure by Mechanical Transfer

The risk of mechanical transfer can occur with the colocation of a process with contaminated equipment or items, e.g., operators wearing contaminated clothing or contact between process equipment contaminated with different materials.

Factors include:

  • The use of common areas where segregation of processes and equipment is poor, e.g., common manual cleaning areas where dirty and clean equipment from different processes may be present at the same time
  • Use of common gowning regimes across areas where multiple products may be present simultaneously
  • Surface to surface contact, e.g., scraping, smearing, or wiping of process equipment
  • Use of common equipment, e.g., hand tools or change parts between different processes

The use of certain equipment may cause operators or mobile equipment to become contaminated and potentially transit to other process areas without undergoing decontamination procedures. Issues to be considered:

  • The presence of highly manual, uncontained/open processes
  • The specification and location of decontamination procedures for users and equipment
  • Routing of equipment and users around facilities, especially where there is common routing of products

Commentary

The Three-Step Mechanism of Mechanical Transfer

The mechanical transfer contamination pathway can be broken down into three necessary conditions (all three must be present):

  1. Contaminant transfers to a vector surface (e.g. employee gloves or the outside of equipment touch contaminated material)
  2. The vector contacts the exposed product at risk (e.g. a contaminated glove touches the contact surface of another product)
  3. The contaminant is released from the vector to the product (e.g. wiping or pressure contact transfers the residue)

The control strategy is to place barriers at these three steps and break any one link.

High-Risk Scenario: Shared Cleaning Areas

A common cleaning area is a high-risk point for mechanical transfer, because:

  • "Dirty" equipment and "clean" equipment from different processes are present at the same time
  • Splashes and contact during washing can contaminate already cleaned equipment
  • The movement routes of equipment between the cleaning room and the production area may cross

Best practice: set up a dedicated cleaning room for high-risk products, or establish a strict procedure of "bagging in protective covers immediately after cleaning before transfer".

Analogy: Employees Are "Mobile Contamination Vectors"

In open processes, employees' garments, gloves and shoes can pick up powder and become "contamination ferries" between process areas. This is why:

  • High-risk product areas need a dedicated gowning procedure
  • On leaving the area, decontamination is needed, not just a change of garments
  • Tools (hand tools, parts) need a clear dedicated area assignment or must be cleaned before use in another area

In Practice: Mechanical Transfer Risk Assessment Checklist

  • Are there personnel or equipment flow routes across process areas?
  • Does a shared gowning room lead to mixing of operators from different products?
  • Do hand tools (wrenches, scrapers) carry product-specific labels or cleaning records?
  • Does the cleaning area have "dirty" and "clean" zones and material flow control?
  • Is there a decontamination station before and after open processing?
6.3.4

6.3.4 Airborne Transfer

Aerosol generation, dustiness, CPT, physical barriers | p66–p68

Route 4 — Airborne Transfer

6.3.4 Airborne Transfer

The direct airborne transfer route assumes the generation of a stable aerosol, which moves to another area where it deposits in significant quantities on another exposed product.

An aerosol is a suspension of fine solid or liquid particles in air. The operator has a greater risk of exposure than another product because of the inhalation of dust by the operator in relatively close proximity to the product compared to the passive deposition of dust into or onto another product distant from the emission source.

Products typically are separated by physical barriers, airlocks, and corridors.

"Industrial hygiene monitoring data should not be used to quantitatively evaluate potential product cross-contamination due to these differences. However, high levels of airborne dust identified by monitoring may be qualitatively indicative of a poorly controlled process."

Chemical contaminants do not have the potential to proliferate as is the case with microbial contamination. In addition, there is generally less potential for airborne contaminants to present a serious risk of cross-contamination than from other routes, such as mix-up and retention.

However, chemical contamination can increase due to continued deposition from an extended time emission source onto a continuously exposed at-risk product. Such deposition may also give rise to increased risk of subsequent mechanical transfer.

Three Key Questions for Airborne Pathway Assessment

  1. Can the contaminating process form a stable aerosol?
  2. Can the stable aerosol be emitted and move to a position where it may deposit on the at-risk product in significant quantities?
  3. Is the at-risk product sufficiently exposed to the aerosol for significant cross-contamination (i.e., above the ADE value)?

6.3.4.1 Factors Affecting Exposure by Airborne Transfer

Risk analysis should consider the following for any significant risk of cross-contamination:

  • Open processing activities at both source and receiving points
  • Poorly sealed process equipment with pressure driving forces (positive pressure at source and/or negative pressure at receiving material positions)
  • The repeated transfer of material through breakable or poorly contained couplings
  • Relative locality of different process streams
  • Provision of rooms and other building features between source and receiving positions

Physical Properties for Stable Aerosol Formation

Key issues include:

  • Moisture content of solids
  • Liquid viscosity and surface tension
  • Particle size distribution
  • Particle shape
  • Particle density
  • Particle charge (related to relative humidity)

Such properties may be considered as a combined factor commonly known as dustiness — a measure of the propensity of the material to remain airborne for extended periods when in an aerosol.

6.3.4.2 Containment Performance Target (CPT)

The Containment Performance Target (CPT) (expressed in µg/m³) is a target value that is used as the basis for plant design and selection of risk control strategies (i.e., containment systems) for protection of the workforce.

Commentary

Conditions for Aerosol Formation

Not all dust can become a meaningful cross-contamination vector. All of the following must be met at the same time:

  1. Formation of a stable aerosol: the process must generate particles fine enough to stay suspended
  2. A transport path exists: the aerosol must be able to travel to the receiving product
  3. Sufficient deposition: the deposited amount must exceed the ADE to constitute a significant risk

If any one condition is not met, the airborne route can be ruled out. This "three-question method" is a quick screening tool.

Dustiness — an Indicator of a Material's Airborne Potential

Dustiness is a composite indicator of a material's tendency to become airborne. Influencing factors include:

Materials with high dustiness need stricter containment measures

Airborne Transfer vs. the Other Routes: Why Is It Ranked Last?

Airborne transfer is ranked last in product risk, because:

  • Chemical contaminants do not multiply like microorganisms; the amount deposited is limited by physical laws
  • Product is usually in closed containers or rooms, where physical barriers isolate it effectively
  • Airborne powder is heavily diluted before it reaches the receiving product
  • Compared with mix-up (direct bulk admixture), the amount deposited is usually small

Exception: with long exposure time, a continuously open process and poor ventilation, accumulated deposition may exceed the ADE, and it can then also become a secondary source for mechanical transfer.

IH Air Sampling Data Cannot Be Used for Quantitative Assessment of Product Cross-Contamination

ISPE's explicit position: industrial hygiene air sampling (used to protect workers) must not be used directly to assess product cross-contamination risk, because:

  • Different sampling locations: IH samples in the employee breathing zone; GxP sampling points should be around the periphery of the process room
  • Different assessment goals: IH is concerned with the amount workers inhale; GxP is concerned with the amount deposited on product
  • Different sampling volumes: GxP air sampling may need a larger volume to detect very low leakage

The two departments must communicate closely to avoid misuse of data.

Containment Performance Target (CPT) — a Design-Oriented Indicator

The CPT (Containment Performance Target) is expressed in µg/m³ and is the basis for equipment selection and facility design:

  • The CPT is derived from the material's OEL
  • The CPT determines the containment equipment class (such as glove boxes, isolators, RABS)
  • The lower the CPT, the higher the containment required and the higher the equipment cost
  • Common bands: <1 µg/m³ (extremely high toxicity), 1-10 µg/m³ (high toxicity), >100 µg/m³ (general)

Analogy: Pressure Differential Management = a One-Way Valve for Airflow

Plants use pressure differentials to control airflow direction: high-toxicity areas are kept at negative pressure so air only flows in, never out. It is like a pump that makes water flow in only one direction, stopping dust from "flowing back" into clean areas. This is the core engineering measure for controlling airborne transfer and must be designed together with the CPT requirement.

Exercise: Which Processes in Your Plant Are Most Likely to Generate High Dustiness?

Common high-risk processes:

  • Milling: generates large quantities of fine particles
  • Fluid bed drying (FBD): large amounts of powder suspended in hot air
  • Manual charging: open pouring of powder
  • Compression: powder splashing

Every high-dustiness process should be fitted with local exhaust ventilation (LEV) or closed containment, and assessed for whether a CPT-based design is needed.

Comparison of the Four Routes — Chapter 6 Summary Matrix

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture | p69–p78

Key Points of This Section (p69–p78)

  • Understand the systematic framework and trigger conditions for probability of occurrence analysis
  • Master how production operations (weighing, blending, compression, filling, granulation) influence cross-contamination risk
  • Identify potential cross-contamination routes in non-production activities (cleaning, maintenance, changeover)
  • Assess the effect of facility and equipment design (HVAC, closed/open systems, drainage) on risk
  • Understand the definition of non-product contact areas and the assessment of residues there
  • Understand the role of detection methods in risk scoring, including adequacy of analytical methods and comparison with compendia

Contents of This Section

6.4 Probability of Occurrence 6.4.1 Production Process Activities 6.4.2 Non-Processing Activities 6.4.3 Facility and Equipment Design 6.5 Product Exposure Risk in Non-Product Contact Areas 6.6 Detection
6.4

Probability of Occurrence

p69–p70 | Systematically identify potential cross-contamination routes under all operating conditions

Original Text (English)

6.4 Probability of Occurrence

A risk analysis designed to identify the probability of cross-contamination should consider all potential routes of cross-contamination under all operational conditions in a systematic and detailed manner. The analysis should be reviewed:

  • At the design stage of any new or refurbished facility/equipment
  • At the introduction of new products or changes to any process or equipment
  • As part of any relevant change control
  • As part of a regular risk management review

The following are possible examples where the probability of failure or error may be enhanced, and these also should be considered as part of any review:

  • Manual processes
  • Historically non-robust processes or equipment
  • Specific elements of a process (batch start up, batch changeovers, shift changeovers, post maintenance)
  • New, untrained, or changed personnel
Commentary

Core Concept: Probability of Occurrence

Probability of occurrence is one of the three dimensions of cross-contamination risk assessment (the other two are severity and detectability). This section requires a systematic, detailed review of potential contamination routes under all operating conditions, not only normal production.

The assessment is not performed only at the design stage of a new facility; any product change, equipment modification or process adjustment must trigger a reassessment.

Identifying High-Risk Scenarios — Inspection Focus

The following scenarios significantly raise the probability of failure and are common focus areas for regulatory inspections:

  • Manual operations: depend on personnel skill; low consistency
  • Historically unstable processes or equipment: with a record of past failures
  • Specific operating nodes: batch start-up, batch changeover, shift handover, restart after maintenance
  • New, untrained or replaced personnel: increase the risk of operating deviations

Inspectors often ask the company to provide a dedicated risk assessment or SOP controls for these nodes.

Analogy: Aircraft Safety Checks

Probability assessment is like safety assessment in aviation: it reviews not only normal flight but also takeoff, landing, ground handling and crew changes, every handover node. In a pharma plant, "batch changeover" and "shift handover" are the nodes where things most easily go wrong.

6.4.1

Production Process Activities

p70–p71 | Comprehensive assessment of operating states, process characteristics and exposure factors

Original Text (English)

6.4.1 Production Process Activities

It should be noted that the correct application of GxP generally ensures that most processes are reasonably well contained and controlled with respect to cross-contamination. The production process needs to be fully reviewed to ensure that all potential exposures are identified and evaluated.

6.4.1.1 Operational Status: Set Up, Operation, Cleaning, Upset

A system that controls product and worker exposure should perform at all times. Many processes are evaluated for containment performance during optimal operation, such as at Factory Acceptance Testing (FAT) and do not take account of the following conditions:

  • Set up
  • Operation
  • Intervention for correction, heel recovery, end point determination, scrape down for yield/uniformity, sample recovery
  • Upset, such as equipment failure
  • Disassembly
  • Cleaning

Each process step should be evaluated to ensure that it does not provide a significant potential for exposure. The review of each of these items should ensure that the necessary actions can be performed and transfers of tools, materials, and components to perform the tasks can be done without significant or unacceptable exposure or carry over.

6.4.1.2 Process and Exposures: A Review of Potential Exposures Created within Processes

A specific process will have a number of features, all of which may have a greater or lesser impact on the risk of cross-contamination by one or more of the routes. The major factors to consider include:

  • The degree of open handling of the hazardous material (e.g., in bulk manual dispensing)
  • Energy in the form of velocity, pressure, and temperature inherent to the process, which may create transmissible aerosols, e.g.:
    • Milling
    • Micronizing
    • Blending
    • Atomization
    • Fluidization
    • Agitated mixing
    • Compression
  • Transfer: the repeated charging and discharging of unit processes through breakable couplings provides a possible route of release — the more transfers required, the greater the potential of risk
  • Worker behavior or compliance with SOPs: systems that are highly dependent on operator technique or allow wide scope for variations in operator performance are more susceptible to failure
  • Frequency: the more often a process is performed the greater the risk of material release or serial releases
  • Scale: the impact of scale may not be linear. The factors affected by scale include worker care (manually charging 100 kg requires a different approach to charging 100 g), and the volume of material available for release
  • Knowledge of the equipment used, operability, and performance
Figure 6.6🔍: Summary of Process Characteristic Factors that Affect the Risk of Cross-Contamination
Commentary

The Blind Spot of FAT — a Common Inspection Deficiency

Many plants assess containment during the factory acceptance test (FAT), but the FAT is usually performed under optimal operating conditions and does not cover the following high-risk nodes:

  • Equipment installation and set up
  • Manual interventions (scraping, sampling, end-point determination)
  • Equipment failure (upset)
  • Disassembly and cleaning

Inspectors will ask: "Does your risk assessment cover cross-contamination under failure scenarios?" This is a common source of 483 observations.

Analogy: The Non-Linear Relationship Between Scale and Risk

The guide points out that "the effect of scale may be non-linear". When charging 100 g, an operator can be very careful, but when charging 100 kg the absolute amount of material spilled is far greater, and operator fatigue and the area over which dust spreads increase disproportionately. This is often underestimated when a new product is introduced at large scale.

ISPE Key Reminder: GxP Compliance Is Not a Sufficient Condition

The guide states that correct execution of GxP "will normally ensure reasonable containment for most processes", but this is only a baseline, not a sufficient condition. A comprehensive exposure assessment for each process is still necessary; "we comply with GMP" cannot replace specific risk identification and assessment.

Process Characteristics Risk Factor Matrix

The following process characteristics all have a direct effect on the probability of cross-contamination:

6.4.2

Non-Processing Activities

p71–p72 | Waste handling, planned/unplanned maintenance, changeover, cleaning operations

Original Text (English)

6.4.2 Non-Processing Activities

6.4.2.1 Waste Handling

The risk analysis should cover all aspects of waste handling to ensure that items such as soiled clothing, disposable items (e.g., masks, gloves, and filters), and material/product waste streams are appropriately handled to avoid the potential for cross-contamination. Shared areas, e.g., common cleaning rooms, potentially containing simultaneously both dirty and clean equipment from different process streams should be carefully considered.

6.4.2.2 Planned and Unplanned Maintenance

All planned maintenance activities should be reviewed in sufficient detail to identify potential routes for cross-contamination. Some planned maintenance may take place outside of normal operating hours and by personnel other than those involved with the batch manufacture.

The planned maintenance review should consider the following aspects, as a minimum:

  • Nature of the maintenance (e.g., air handling shutdown)
  • Frequency of the maintenance (e.g., daily, weekly, monthly, annual)
  • Status of production when the planned maintenance occurs (e.g., area clean or dirty)
  • The availability of approved procedures for planned maintenance
  • Necessary qualifications of personnel performing the maintenance activities
  • Appropriate training for those performing planned maintenance
  • The likelihood of powder spillage during maintenance (e.g., emptying local dust extraction and vacuum units)

It is more difficult to predict how unplanned maintenance may affect the probability of cross-contamination. A proactive review of potential unplanned events should be performed, to predict how to handle many of them in advance to help to avoid the risk of cross-contamination. This could lead to specific failure mode procedures for some major events (e.g., HVAC failure).

6.4.2.3 Changeovers

The changeover between one product and the next is an important aspect to be reviewed, especially regarding the order of such changeover activities. The changeover activities should be appropriately documented as part of a procedure and clearly indicate the sequence of the changeover work.

Changeover between products may require that equipment change parts have to be removed from the line for one product and be replaced with change parts for the second product. The handling, cleaning, transport, and storage of such change parts (e.g., tablet punches) should be carefully considered.

6.4.2.4 Cleaning

Cleaning can in itself create a potential risk of cross-contamination if not performed properly. The cleaning procedures should be reviewed to ensure that they are sufficiently detailed for personnel to be able to follow them consistently and correctly.

The cleaning processes which are developed should incorporate a risk-based approach to assist in the determination of critical controls and/or critical parameters. For example, a Design of Experiment (DoE) could be performed to determine what parameters have the greatest or least impact on the cleaning process.

Automatic cleaning procedures such as CIP should, in theory, provide much more consistent cleaning than manual cleaning procedures. The CPPs associated with any CIP system (e.g., times, temperatures, and pressures) and how these are monitored, controlled, and alarmed should be understood. There also should be a clear understanding of what happens if these CPPs go outside of limits (e.g., low pressure), as well as the degree of safeguards against manual intervention (e.g., password control).

Commentary

Cross-Contamination Blind Spots in Waste Management

Waste handling is often overlooked, but the contamination routes are real:

  • Product-contaminated gloves, masks and filters can transfer contamination if not disposed of according to procedure
  • A common cleaning room is the highest-risk area: dirty equipment and clean equipment from different products are present at the same time, making cross-contamination very easy
  • Waste route design should be completely separated from the production flow

Seven Minimum Review Elements for Planned Maintenance (PM)

ISPE explicitly lists the minimum requirements for assessing planned maintenance; this is a risk assessment item that inspectors often review:

  1. Nature of the maintenance (e.g. HVAC shutdown)
  2. Frequency of maintenance (daily, weekly, monthly, yearly)
  3. Production status when the maintenance occurs (area clean or contaminated?)
  4. Whether an approved PM procedure exists
  5. Qualification requirements for the personnel performing it
  6. Whether personnel training is in place
  7. Potential for powder release (e.g. when emptying dust collectors or vacuum units)

Key question: are the maintenance personnel from an external contractor? Do they understand the cross-contamination control requirements?

FMEA Thinking for Unplanned Maintenance

The guide requires a proactive review of unplanned maintenance: before an incident occurs, think ahead, "If the HVAC suddenly stops, what is our response SOP? How is cross-contamination risk controlled?" This is exactly the FMEA (failure mode and effects analysis) approach used in manufacturing: predict failures and establish failure mode procedures, rather than improvising after something goes wrong.

Key Points for Changeover Management

Changeover is the highest-risk node in a multi-product plant. Control priorities include:

  • Sequence of changeover activities: there must be a written procedure with a clearly defined operating sequence
  • Tooling/parts management: taking tablet punches as an example, removal, cleaning, transport and storage during changeover are all potential cross-contamination points
  • The changeover procedure should include a checklist, with final release by QA personnel

The Risk of Cleaning Itself — Application of DoE

The guide points out that cleaning itself can also create cross-contamination, a view that is often overlooked. Control directions:

  • The cleaning procedure should be detailed enough for personnel to perform it consistently and correctly
  • Use DoE (design of experiments) to identify the critical process parameters (CPPs) of the cleaning process
  • CIP system CPPs (time, temperature, pressure) must be monitored, controlled and alarmed
  • The response process when a CPP is out of limits must be clearly defined, with safeguards (such as password locks) to prevent unauthorized intervention

CIP is more consistent than manual cleaning, but only if mechanical reliability has been verified.

6.4.3

Facility and Equipment Design

p72–p73 | Definition of closed/open systems, HVAC and facility design factors

Original Text (English)

6.4.3 Facility and Equipment Design

The design of the facility and equipment should be reviewed when considering the potential risks of cross-contamination. The review should include consideration of risk elements such as:

  • Surfaces and finishes (e.g., smoothness, accessibility to clean)
  • Air handling philosophy (e.g., filtration levels, fresh air or recirculation, pressure differential regimes, airflows, monitoring, recording, alarms, back up). The analysis also should review the need for once through air. If air can be recirculated without undue risk to workers and cross-contamination, this can have a large impact on the facility design as well as the operating costs. See Section 9.6 for more information.
  • Airlocks
  • Equipment layout (e.g., space, segregation)
  • Material and process flows including common areas for processes and personnel
  • Cleaning philosophy including provision of effective automated cleaning systems, such as CIP and Wash In Place (WIP)
  • Process equipment design (e.g., closed versus open systems, charging ports)

Actual exposure varies between individual process cycles due to operator performance differences, and the degree of this variability is often reflected in the method of exposure control. Closed systems have far lower variability than open systems, and open systems with engineering controls are better controlled than unprotected open processes.

ADV TOPIC: The selection of the specific process equipment may have a major impact on the scope of exposure through careful design of the processing machine to minimize leaks. This may already be an inherent requirement of the equipment, e.g., with API reaction pressure vessels during reaction steps or fluid bed dryers during drying. As a result, process equipment or processes associated with them can be considered to be either "open" or "closed."

6.4.3.1 Closed Process Definition

A process step (or system) is considered closed where the product is not exposed to the immediate room environment (and vice versa). It is the manufacturer's responsibility to define and demonstrate closure for a process step.

An example might include a pressurized vessel operation or a process enclosed in an effective "high containment" process equipment item, e.g., a sealed process system filled and discharged through split butterfly valves.

6.4.3.2 Open Process Definition

Any process step that does not meet the definition of "closed process" is considered open. This might include open material handling during dispensing or where the process system is opened for a particular step, e.g., granulator bowl scraping or bowl removal from a fluid bed dryer for product recovery. It should be noted that the amount of time the process is open (whether it is brief or longer) should be considered during the risk assessment.

Commentary

Definition of a Closed Process and the Burden of Proof

A closed process is defined as one in which the product is not exposed to the direct room environment (and vice versa). Key principles:

  • The burden of proof is on the manufacturer: the company must define what "closed" means and provide validation evidence of containment
  • Typical examples: pressurized vessels, and sealed systems using split butterfly valves for charging and discharging
  • The variability of exposure in a closed system is far lower than in an open system

Open Processes — Considering the Time Factor

Any process that does not meet the definition of closed is considered an open process, including:

  • Open material handling during dispensing
  • Granulator bowl scraping operations
  • Bowl removal at discharge of a fluid bed dryer

The guide stresses that the length of the opening (brief or longer) should be considered in the risk assessment. Even a brief opening can still be a significant risk point if the material's ADE is very low.

Decision Logic for Once-Through Air

Whether the HVAC uses recirculated air is an important design-stage decision:

  • Once-through air (OTA): the strictest cross-contamination control, but with very high energy consumption (heating and cooling energy is not recovered)
  • Recirculated air: significantly lowers facility design and operating costs, but it must be confirmed that aerosols in the return air are effectively filtered

The decision is based on risk assessment, not a preset position: if recirculation does not add unacceptable risk to workers or of cross-contamination, recirculation is an acceptable option. See Section 9.6 for details.

Seven Dimensions of the Facility Design Risk Review

The influence of facility and equipment design on the probability of cross-contamination is fundamental: design sets the "floor" of the risk, and later operational controls are only a management layer on top of the design. Scope of the review:

6.5

Product Exposure Risk in Non-Product Contact Areas

p73–p75 | Area classification, residue assessment, plausibility analysis, exposure assessment considerations

Original Text (English)

6.5 Product Exposure Risk in Non-Product Contact Areas

In general, the risk of cross-contamination due to mechanical and/or airborne transfer is considered to be low relative to other possible pathways (e.g., retention or mix-up) although in these areas it may be critical for worker safety. If the risk assessment indicates that airborne contamination is a potential concern, then further analysis may be necessary using quantitative assessment tools based on the acceptance limits established reflecting the ADE of Product A and the batch size and maximum daily dose of Product B.

6.5.1 Non-Product Contact Area

Figure 6.7🔍 illustrates the main classifications of non-product contact areas:

  • Barriers: physically segregate the product contact surface from the surrounding product room area as well as from workers. Types of barriers can be closed processing equipment or enclosures such as a pressured vessel, isolator, or ventilated enclosures such as safety cabinets and fume cupboards.
  • Product room/suite: where one product is processed on a dedicated, batch, or campaign basis where the non-product contact surfaces include exterior surfaces of enclosed process equipment or barriers and interior finishes such as walls, floor, ceiling, and doors.
  • Supporting areas: adjacent to the product rooms/suites, where workers from other product rooms may be present.
  • GxP area: the common area where the worker is not in direct contact with the product as in the secondary packaging, shipping/receiving, and warehousing areas.
  • HVAC system: includes ducting, filters, Air Handling Units (AHUs), and control devices for a product room installed in the GxP area.

6.5.2 Residue on Non-Product Contact Surfaces

It is important that non-product contact surfaces are kept visually clean. Visual monitoring enables a wide area to be observed quickly. Swab sampling data may be available to permit assessment of potential cross-contamination of products from these non-product contact surfaces.

Particularly where the exposure potential is high, swab testing against preset limits may be part of a more detailed review/action plan. These preset limits should take into consideration plausible, worst-case, assumptions of potential exposure pathways, the amount of Product A that could cross contaminate Product B, and the ADE value for Product A.

Some companies may use wipe samples/limits to assess the potential dermal risk from systematic exposure of employees and this should not be confused with potential cross-contamination risks from non-product contact surfaces.

6.5.3 Plausibility

Non-product contact parts represent elements within the process boundary which are not in product contact. Typical elements are the internal surfaces of an isolator or the surfaces of an engineering control device. These surfaces are far less critical than product contact surfaces and should be cleaned between products.

Surfaces outside this zone are of even less significance, particularly where closed systems are used. Though the surface concentration taken over the whole surface area may seem a significant number, the risk of all or even a small fraction of this material being disengaged from the surface and entering the product is relatively insignificant.

Example calculation (Plausibility): A room 5 m × 5 m × 3 m has a surface area of 110 m². Where the LOD is 5 µg/swab, where the swab is 25 cm² and the samples taken are below the level of detection; taking a very conservative approach, the total available on the surface is:
110 m² × 10,000 cm²/m² × 5 µg/25 cm² = 220,000 µg or 220 milligrams.
Supposing the potential contamination is to a batch with 100,000 maximum daily dose units and the ADE is 50 µg/day, the total that could be present and be within the limit is 50 µg × 100,000 = 5,000,000 µg or 5 grams. The margin of safety (5 g vs 220 mg) is significant.
Second example (scoop transfer): A stainless steel scoop with surface area 2,900 cm² at visual detection limit of 4 µg/cm²:
2,900 cm² × 4 µg/cm² = 11,600 µg or 11.6 milligrams.
With a safe carryover limit of 5 grams, the margin of safety = 5,000 mg / 11.6 mg ≈ 431. This represents low cross-contamination risk.

6.5.4 Considerations for Exposure Assessment

The mode of exposure for non-product contact areas is by either mechanical or airborne transfer. The following should be considered when conducting an exposure assessment:

  • Open processes during operation, cleaning, upset, and maintenance
  • Retained or accumulated product inside the barrier or the product room/suite
  • Recirculated HVAC systems for the product room — is the air stream effectively protected from recirculated aerosols?
  • Retained and accumulated product in the ductwork, including airborne transfer due to pressure differentials
  • Leakage of product to areas above the ceiling or interstitial space or into the cavities of the building
  • Retained or accumulated product during maintenance or repair of the HVAC or utility systems
  • Transfer of product compound from the worker's clothing, hands, or feet
  • Transfer of product compound from dirty equipment transfer and their wheels
  • Transfer of product compound on the handling media
  • Manual cleaning of equipment and/or barrier
  • Emergency or upset situations in the pharmaceutical area
  • Shared HVAC systems serving several rooms or common areas where several different products are handled — should be evaluated in relation to each specific hazard
Commentary

Dermal Exposure Risk vs. Cross-Contamination Risk — Do Not Confuse Them

The guide specifically clarifies that some companies use swab sampling/limits to assess employees' dermal risk, and this should not be conflated with cross-contamination risk assessment. The purposes differ:

  • Dermal exposure assessment: protects workers (occupational health, IH)
  • Cross-contamination assessment: protects the quality of the next batch of product (GxP)

The basis for setting sampling limits differs, so the two cannot be used in place of each other.

Quantitative Method for Plausibility Assessment (Plausibility Calculation)

ISPE provides an important quantitative thinking tool: even if the residue on room surfaces looks large, the amount that actually enters the product is often vastly different from the ADE limit.

Calculation logic:
Total surface residue in the room under the most conservative estimate (mg)
        vs.
Maximum amount allowed to carry over into Product B = ADE_A × maximum number of daily dose units (mg)

Margin of Safety (MoS) = allowable amount / estimated residue

Example 1 (room surfaces):
  Estimated maximum residue = 110 m² × 10,000 cm²/m² × 5 µg/25 cm²
               = 220,000 µg = 220 mg
  ADE allowable amount = 50 µg × 100,000 dose units = 5,000 mg = 5 g
  MoS = 5,000 mg / 220 mg ≈ 22.7  → low risk

Example 2 (metal scoop):
  Maximum residue = 2,900 cm² × 4 µg/cm² = 11,600 µg = 11.6 mg
  ADE allowable amount = 5 g = 5,000 mg
  MoS = 5,000 mg / 11.6 mg ≈ 431  → low risk

This calculation rests on the most conservative assumptions (all residues are at the LOD value and all can transfer to the product), so the actual risk is lower.

12 Considerations for Exposure Assessment — Practical Checklist

The 12 considerations listed in 6.5.4 should each be recorded in the actual risk assessment document:

  • Open process activities during operation, cleaning, abnormal events and maintenance
  • Product accumulation and residue in isolator barriers or product rooms
  • Whether aerosol protection in the HVAC return air system is adequate
  • Dust accumulation in ducts and pressure-driven airborne transfer
  • Leakage above ceilings, in interstitial spaces and in wall cavities
  • Residues during HVAC/utility system maintenance
  • Carry-over on personnel garments, hands and shoe soles
  • Contamination carried on the wheels of mobile equipment
  • Transfer by material handling vectors (pallets, containers)
  • Manual cleaning of equipment/isolator barriers
  • Emergency or abnormal situations
  • Scenarios where shared HVAC serves multiple rooms

Five-Tier Classification Framework for Non-Product Contact Areas

ISPE establishes a clear hierarchy of spatial classification, corresponding to different risk levels and monitoring requirements:

6.6

Detection

p75–p76 | Analytical methods, PAT, compendia comparison, adequacy of controls

Original Text (English)

6.6 Detection

An essential element in accurately assigning a risk level to a hazard arising from a compound is the degree of difficulty by which the compound can be detected. A hazard rating may be high, but if the method of detection is sufficient, linked to health-based limits and to appropriate control measures, then the subsequent risk arising from that hazard can be managed and low.

The application of methodologies which possess the degree of selectivity and sensitivity necessary to detect a compound of interest is key to any detection-based control of risk from a hazard. Such controls, when optimally embedded into those points of the process which present the highest level of potential concern can effectively control the risk.

In situations where the method of detection is visual only, it is important to understand the visual acuity of the staff and what level of residue is considered safe. If the safe level is below the visual acuity of the staff then the risk of failure not being detected may be considered high whereas if the safe level is well above (several orders of magnitude) the visual acuity of the staff the risk of failure not being detected may be considered low.

The degree of variability in product contact equipment cleaning processes should be understood and considered as a primary factor in ongoing monitoring decisions. As an example, automated CIP processes present less risk from a variability perspective than manual cleaning methods, and may therefore justify less ongoing monitoring (once mechanical reliability is proven) than a manual process.

6.6.1 Developmental Controls Providing Process Knowledge

During the course of pharmaceutical discovery and development activities, one focus is on the development of analytical methodology aimed at accurate identification and assessment of both drug substance and potential synthesis and degradation impurities. The drug substance itself and the potential preliminary toxicological profiles are established for each impurity as well as for the drug substance.

Traditionally, once analytics have been established, efforts have turned to the optimization of test methods, an exercise which culminates in a "validation exercise."

Process Analytical Technology (PAT) is increasingly being applied to processes for the purpose of acquiring information to facilitate process understanding and allowing risk control strategies to be established. Once established, these should be maintained and continually improved under the Pharmaceutical Quality System (ICH Q10).

As such, the development, convergence, and subsequent integration of analytical technologies such as Near Infra-Red (NIR) Raman Spectroscopy and endpoint monitoring/control tools should be considered essential developmental activities. Complementary controls, such as barrier systems, also may be seen as suitable mechanisms to control a risk arising from any particular hazard.

Subsequent scale up activities should embed continued refinement of PAT enablers. Such continual improvement initiatives should become institutional requirements within organizations, much akin to historical efforts of yield optimization and process train mapping.

ADVANCED TOPIC: A potential challenge to the holistic approach presented may be encountered where traditional demarcation exists between research and commercial functions within the industry. This should not prevent existing methodologies being either discounted or replaced by new techniques or a technology developed by units supporting the progression of the compound throughout its life cycle.

6.6.2 Compendia Comparison

Prior to starting the development of complementary analytics, units concerned should assess the basis of current referee methodology utilized by regulatory agencies. The methods, both microbiologically and analytically derived, typically assist the agencies in the evaluation of a variety of sample collection analyses: to include challenging materials for the presence of compounds, such as penicillins and beta-lactams.

The caveat for any internally developed analytical or microbial method is that it should formally demonstrate at least an equivalent level of sensitivity as the compendial (e.g., USP, EP, JP) or referee methodology. Reasons for developing an alternative methodology may include improved sensitivity, robustness, application to PAT technologies, or ease of use.

6.6.3 Adequacy of Controls

Throughout the product life cycle, the containment technologies, equipment, and analytical testing which are utilized should continue to be evaluated for applicability, currency, reproducibility, and robustness. Without consistency of the controls which are employed, multiple variables will exist which will complicate root cause investigations should a detection technique identify a breach to the acceptable threshold of control which has been established.

Commentary

The Role of Detection in Risk Assessment

In the FMEA risk assessment framework, detectability is one of the three dimensions of the risk priority number (RPN):

  • Severity × Probability × Detection (difficulty of detection) = RPN

An important ISPE insight: even when the Hazard Rating is high, if the detection method is sensitive and directly linked to the health-based limit, the overall risk can still be effectively controlled and kept low. This is the correct understanding of "risk is controllable", not "risk does not exist".

Limits of Visual Inspection — the Importance of the Analytical Method

When the detection method is only visual inspection, the key questions are:

  • What is the employee's visual acuity?
  • How does the safe residue level relate to the lower visual detection limit?

Concluding logic:

  • If the safe level < the lower visual detection limit → a failure cannot be seen by eye → high detection risk
  • If the safe level >> the lower visual detection limit (several orders of magnitude apart) → low detection risk

This logic is directly linked to setting the visual detection limit (VDL) in cleaning validation.

Process Analytical Technology (PAT) as a Detection Tool

PAT (Process Analytical Technology) is increasingly being integrated into process detection:

  • NIR (near-infrared spectroscopy) and Raman spectroscopy: non-destructive, real-time composition analysis tools
  • End-point monitoring: automatically determines the process end point, reducing reliance on the operator's subjective judgment

ICH Q10 requires that once a PAT system is established, it should be continuously maintained and improved within the pharmaceutical quality system (PQS), rather than left in a static state once validated.

The guide also points to the problem of organizational barriers: the traditional divide between R&D and commercial manufacturing should not become an obstacle to technology transfer.

Compendia Comparison — the Regulatory Baseline

When developing in-house or alternative analytical methods, there is a non-negotiable baseline:

An in-house method must formally demonstrate sensitivity at least equivalent to the compendial method (USP, EP, JP) or a reference method recognized by the regulatory authority.

Valid reasons for developing an alternative method include:

  • Higher sensitivity
  • Better robustness
  • Suitability for integration into PAT technology
  • Operational convenience

Penicillins and beta-lactams are the typical products of greatest concern to regulators, and the sensitivity requirements for their analytical methods are especially strict.

Practical Reflection: Does Your HVAC Maintenance Procedure Trigger a Cross-Contamination Risk Assessment?

Reflect on the following questions in light of this section:

  • Does your plant's HVAC planned maintenance (PM) have a written procedure listing all 7 minimum elements?
  • Is there a predefined failure mode procedure for HVAC failure?
  • Is the sensitivity of the cleaning residue analytical method you use better than or equivalent to the USP/EP method? Is there a formal comparison record?
  • Does the changeover SOP clearly define the operating sequence and include a QA release confirmation step?

Four Criteria for Assessing the Adequacy of Controls

The guide requires continuous assessment, throughout the product lifecycle, of all control technologies, equipment and analytical tests in use:

If controls are inconsistent, once an out-of-limit result is detected, root cause investigation becomes extremely difficult, because there are too many variables that cannot be ruled out. This is the core reason the guide stresses consistency of controls.

Section Summary (Section 3b: Key Points of 6.4–6.6)

  • 6.4 Probability of Occurrence: the risk assessment must cover four trigger points: design, product introduction, change control and periodic review; manual operations, unstable processes, transitions between operating nodes and personnel changes are indicator factors of high probability of occurrence.
  • 6.4.1 Production Activities: FAT tests only the optimal state, so the risk assessment must extend to all operating states: equipment set up, manual intervention, failure, disassembly and cleaning; energy input (milling, fluidization, compression), transfer frequency and scale effects are the core considerations.
  • 6.4.2 Non-Processing Activities: shared waste areas, review of cross-contamination routes in PM/EM programs, the documented sequence of the changeover procedure, and CIP CPP monitoring and alarm design are all key compliance points.
  • 6.4.3 Facility Design: a closed process has a clear definition and the burden of proof is on the manufacturer; whether the HVAC uses once-through air requires a risk assessment decision, not a default choice.
  • 6.5 Non-Product Contact Areas: five-tier spatial classification (Barriers → Product Room → Supporting → GxP → HVAC); the quantitative plausibility calculation shows that the actual risk to product from room surface residue is often far below the ADE limit; the exposure assessment must cover 12 dimensions.
  • 6.6 Detection: detectability is one of the three dimensions of the RPN; visual inspection methods must be compared against the safe limit; PAT (NIR/Raman) is an advanced detection tool; in-house methods must be at least as sensitive as compendial methods; controls must be continuously assessed for suitability, timeliness, reproducibility and robustness.
