Chapter 7-8: Risk Evaluation & Control

p79-p84

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products | p79–p84

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand where Risk Evaluation sits in the ICH Q9 framework and what form its output takes
  • Master how acceptable risk is defined: the three-zone action framework (green / yellow / red)
  • Understand how ADE / HBEL / OEL serve as the numerical basis for acceptable risk
  • Understand the holistically balanced approach to Risk Control
  • Be familiar with the three control strategy criteria: Operability, Impact, Minimize Over-Engineering
  • Be able to apply the principles of these chapters to CDMO decisions on accepting multi-product work and investing in facilities

Section Outline

Ch.7 Risk Evaluation 7.1 Defining Acceptable Risk Ch.8 Risk Control 8.1 Holistically Balanced Approach 8.1.1 Operability 8.1.2 Impact Assessment 8.1.3 Minimize Over-Engineering CDMO Practical Implications
7

Risk Evaluation

The final step of the three-step ICH Q9 risk assessment: compare against acceptance criteria and decide whether action is needed

Original Text (English)

ICH Q9 places risk evaluation at the conclusion of risk assessment (risk identification / risk analysis / risk evaluation). Risk evaluation compares the identified and analyzed risk against given risk criteria. This step is designed to provide an estimation of risks to patients via product cross-contamination or to workers based on the hazard identification, dose-response assessment, and exposure assessment, along with consideration of the assumptions, limitations, and sources of uncertainty and variability in the analysis.

All available information is evaluated in order to determine the robustness of the data sets used to assess the risk. Based on this evaluation, a conclusion can be made as to whether potential exposures are above or below acceptable levels.

Incomplete knowledge about the hazard of a compound, the potential routes for mechanical and airborne transfer, and resulting variation in exposures brought about by design of process or equipment, are all sources of uncertainty whose potential impact should be considered in assessing both risk to patients by cross-contamination and risk to workers by direct exposure to the compound.

If the data sets do not provide sufficient information, it may be determined during this step that additional data gathering is necessary. The output of the risk evaluation is either a quantitative estimate (e.g., distance between acceptance criteria and data gathered — true margin of safety) or a qualitative description (e.g., high, medium, a numerical probability, or low) of a range of risk.

All aspects of risk assessment, not just the risk evaluation, should be evaluated and documented in a manner proportionate to the risk (the higher the risk, the more in-depth the evaluation and documentation process). The evaluation should be completely transparent so that the user fully understands the application and limitation(s) of the conclusions drawn. A fully transparent risk assessment can be used in the ongoing risk review process.

Commentary and Practical Application

Where Risk Evaluation Sits in the Three Steps

ICH Q9 defines "Risk Evaluation" as the third and final step of Risk Assessment. The first two steps are:

  • Risk Identification: finding out what the hazard is
  • Risk Analysis: analyzing the likelihood and severity of the hazard
  • Risk Evaluation: comparing against acceptance criteria to decide whether the risk is within the acceptable range

Many sites stop their risk assessments after the first two steps and never establish clear "acceptance criteria", so the assessment becomes a paper exercise rather than a management tool.

Analogy: A Traffic Signal System

The three steps of risk assessment are like installing signals at an intersection:

  • Risk Identification = finding what hazards the intersection has (vehicles, pedestrians, bends)
  • Risk Analysis = analyzing how often each hazard occurs and how severe the consequences are
  • Risk Evaluation = deciding whether the intersection needs a "red light", a "yellow warning", or "no control at all"

Without the third step, the earlier analysis leads to no action.

Key Point: The Form of the Output

The output of a risk evaluation can be:

  • Quantitative: for example, how far the measured exposure is from the ADE (the safety margin) - more scientific and defensible
  • Qualitative: for example, a high / medium / low risk classification - easy to apply but needs careful interpretation

The guide specifically warns that a qualitative description is only an "indication of potential risk", not a definitive risk outcome.

Proportionate to Risk

The depth of documentation should be proportionate to the level of risk. This is a common point of regulatory scrutiny:

  • High-risk products (highly potent APIs, carcinogens) → detailed quantitative analysis and a complete documentation chain
  • Low-risk products (conventional dosage forms, low toxicity) → qualitative analysis with simple records is sufficient

Over-documenting low-risk items not only wastes resources but can also divert attention from the genuinely high risks.

7.1

Defining Acceptable Risk

A three-zone action framework based on HBEL / ADE / TTC

Original Text (English)

It is important for senior management to set guidelines on how to determine acceptable risk, and more importantly, unacceptable risk. Unacceptable risk requires that processing should cease until the risk is reduced to acceptable levels. Typically, there will be three action ranges:

  • Acceptable risk: no action required / monitor only
  • Unacceptable risk: cease actions until risk is reduced to acceptable levels / monitor to confirm effectiveness of risk reduction
  • Reduce risk if possible: between acceptable risk and unacceptable risk, there is a range where risk reduction measures should be employed but actions can continue to be monitored to ensure risk is not increased to unacceptable

Risk can be categorized in qualitative terms such as high, medium or low risk, red, yellow, green, or in quantitative terms where the data is compared to acceptance criteria consisting of numerical limits.

Caution should be exercised in the use of qualitative risk terms as these are indicative of the potential for risk and not a definitive risk outcome. All identified risks, whether qualitative or quantitative, high or low, should be reviewed periodically, based on the level of risk, to ensure that the risk profile has not changed.

Commentary and Practical Application

The Numerical Basis for Acceptable Risk

Defining what is "acceptable" requires a numerical anchor point. The guide recommends health-based limits:

Patient protection (cross-contamination):
  Acceptable limit = ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure)
                   = HBEL (Health-Based Exposure Limit)
  → calculated by a toxicologist from the NOEL / safety factors

Worker protection (occupational exposure):
  Acceptable limit = OEL (Occupational Exposure Limit)
                   = OEB (Occupational Exposure Band)

Early development (insufficient toxicological data):
  TTC (Threshold of Toxicological Concern) may be used
  → usually 1.5 µg/day (Kroes et al.) as an interim substitute

Where a specific ADE cannot be established, the TTC provides a conservative default limit and prevents risk from being released on the grounds of "we do not know".

The Pitfalls of Qualitative Terms

The guide specifically warns that qualitative labels such as "high / medium / low risk" are only an "indication of potential risk" and not a final conclusion.

Practical pitfall: many QRM documents use red / yellow / green colors without explaining the numerical basis for the scoring. This is easily challenged in an inspection: "On what criteria is your 'high risk' based?"

Recommended practice: qualitative labels must be linked to a quantitative trigger to be defensible.

Analogy: A Bank Credit Score

Defining acceptable risk is like a bank setting its lending thresholds:

  • Credit score > 750 → automatic approval (green zone)
  • 600-750 → additional collateral required (yellow zone)
  • < 600 → declined (red zone)

Without thresholds set in advance, every review becomes a "gut-feel decision", which is neither fair nor compliant. The HBEL is the pharmaceutical industry's "credit threshold".

Responsibility of Senior Management

This section explicitly requires senior management to set these guidelines - this is not something the technical department decides alone.

This matters especially for a CDMO: before accepting a project, senior management must clearly answer "What level of risk are we willing to accept?" Only then can the CDMO make well-founded commitments to clients and, when necessary, have a basis for declining a high-risk product.

The Three-Zone Action Framework (Traffic-Light Model)

The chapter explicitly requires senior management to define three zones in advance; this is one of the core GMP compliance requirements:

8

Risk Control

The ICH Q9 decision process: reduce and/or accept risk, with integrated consideration of IH and GxP requirements

Original Text (English)

ICH Q9 states that risk control is the process of decision making to reduce and/or accept risks. Risk acceptance and risk reduction should be considered as iterative steps and be subject to constant review, feedback, and communication. If the risk is still unacceptable, further risk reduction steps should be identified.

A Risk Priority Number (RPN), from an FMEA is an example of a qualitative risk evaluation output and any action taken as a consequence is part of the risk control steps.

It is important to consider control strategies that will address concerns in both IH (Industrial Hygiene) and GxP settings, and any control strategy used to maintain an acceptable level of risk for one setting should not add risk to the other.

For example, a strategy that focuses on reducing exposure in the immediate workplace may have a negative, and perhaps unforeseen, impact on GxP issues. Worker exposure might be successfully reduced by the use of a totally closed system, close coupling of unit operations, or by the addition of redundancy in the form of gloveboxes. However, this may increase the risk of product retention and cross-contamination of subsequent products if the processing train is more difficult to clean.

Once a strategy has been selected for one setting, a risk analysis of the other should be performed before accepting the control strategy. Similarly, before embarking on the implementation of risk reduction strategies, a qualitative risk assessment should be undertaken using risk ranking and FMEA tools or other appropriate tools or techniques.

Once control strategies have been implemented, quantitative risk assessments should be made to demonstrate the performance capability of the system.

Commentary and Practical Application

Risk Control Is Not a One-Time Decision

The guide emphasizes "iterative steps" - risk control is a continuing cycle, not a one-time tick-box exercise.

A typical cycle:

  • Implement controls → confirm the effect quantitatively → if still insufficient → identify the next reduction step → confirm again

This is exactly the purpose of ICH Q9 Risk Communication and Risk Review: the control strategy must be re-evaluated periodically, especially when the process, equipment or product mix changes.

IH vs GxP: The Potential Conflict Between Two Objectives

The most important warning of this chapter: controls adopted to protect workers (IH) may worsen product safety (GxP), and vice versa.

Classic example: introducing a closed system or glovebox to reduce worker exposure:

  • IH benefit: worker exposure is greatly reduced
  • GxP cost: the system is harder to clean → product residue → higher cross-contamination risk

Correct approach: each time a control strategy is designed for one side, an independent risk analysis must be performed for the other side to ensure that no new risk has been introduced.

Analogy: The Fire Door Dilemma

For fire safety (the analogue of IH), a building is fitted with self-closing fire doors. But if a door is too heavy or too hard to operate, workers may prop it open with a brick (bypassing the control), creating a greater danger.

This is a realistic picture of why "controls must take operability into account". If a design considers only theoretical effectiveness and ignores user behavior, the control strategy will fail.

Sequence for Verifying a Control Strategy

The standard process suggested by the guide:

  1. Before implementation: qualitative evaluation using FMEA / risk ranking
  2. After implementation: verify system performance with quantitative data (such as exposure testing and cleaning validation data)

Neither step may be omitted. If only a qualitative evaluation is done and the actual effect is never verified with data, it will be hard to justify in a regulatory inspection.

8.1

Holistically Balanced Approach to Risk Control

A control philosophy that considers all stakeholders, from formulation and equipment to facility

Original Text (English)

The fundamental objective for a risk management program is to ensure that exposure both of the patient through contamination of one product with another, and for a worker via direct contact/inhalation is maintained below the ADE value or other acceptable health-based limit, e.g., OEL for worker exposure and is visually clean, where appropriate.

When working in multiproduct facilities, zero risk of cross-contamination of one product with another is neither realistic nor scientifically achievable. The degree to which such cross-contamination is controlled within the normal bounds of GxP should be commensurate with the risk identified to the patient, considering hazard, probability, and severity.

Similar considerations should be applied within either multipurpose or dedicated facilities toward the potential exposure of workers to the compound concerned. In either case, the controls selected should be appropriate to the hazard as well as the nature and scale of the activity. These controls must ensure that safe levels are not exceeded and should be economically and technically feasible, and applied in both a holistic and balanced manner.

In this context, holistic means that every aspect of product and process is considered, including formulation, equipment, and facility design and operation. All stakeholders should be considered when determining which risks exist and what measures are necessary to ensure they are adequately controlled. Each control measure implemented and the degree and way in which it acts as a control should be understood.

Risk control measures should be determined by a multidisciplinary team and care should be taken to ensure that the risk to one set of stakeholders is not mitigated at the cost of exposing another stakeholder to an unacceptable risk. To achieve balance, understanding the individual aspects of control and the interactions between controls should be evaluated to meet multiple needs.

Commentary and Practical Application

The Precise Meaning of "Holistic"

The guide specifically explains what "holistic" means: every aspect of the product and process must be considered, including:

  • Formulation: do the physicochemical properties of the drug affect how difficult it is to clean?
  • Equipment Design: open or closed? How accessible is it for cleaning?
  • Facility Design: personnel and material flow, airflow, segregation method
  • Operation: SOPs, personnel behavior, training

Optimizing only one of these while ignoring the others cannot truly achieve the overall control objective.

The Myth of Zero Risk

The guide states explicitly that in a multi-product facility, "zero risk of cross-contamination" is neither realistic nor scientifically achievable.

This statement has a double meaning:

  • For regulators: manufacturers should not be required to claim zero risk; they should be required to quantify and manage risk to an acceptable level
  • For manufacturers: do not strive for "no contamination at all"; strive for contamination levels well below the ADE / HBEL

This is a science-based regulatory philosophy and the core spirit of the EMA 2015 Guideline on Setting Health Based Exposure Limits.

The Need for a Multidisciplinary Team

The guide requires control measures to be decided by a multidisciplinary team, which in practice should include:

  • Toxicologist (sets the ADE / HBEL)
  • Industrial hygienist (sets the OEL / OEB)
  • Process engineer (assesses equipment cleanability)
  • Facility engineer (assesses HVAC and flows)
  • QA (ensures documentation compliance)
  • Operators (assess operability)

In a CDMO, this team usually has to be in place at the product Feasibility Assessment stage, so that a well-founded decision can be made before the project is accepted.

Analogy: City Traffic Planning

A holistically balanced approach is like city traffic planning:

  • Widening the roads only (equipment upgrades) without changing the signals (operating procedures) → the congestion remains
  • Changing the signals only (SOPs) while the roads are too narrow (inadequate facility) → limited effect
  • Considering only cars (worker safety) and ignoring pedestrians (patient safety) → unbalanced

Only by considering all factors together can a truly effective system be designed.

8.1.1–3

The Three Control Strategy Criteria: Operability / Impact / Minimize Over-Engineering

Operability · Impact · Minimize Over-Engineering

1

8.1.1 Operability

Functionality and human factors engineering
Ensure controls are workable in actual operation and do not create additional risk

2

8.1.2 Impact

Cost, time, process disruption
Select the best option using whole-life-cycle cost-benefit analysis

3

8.1.3 Minimize Over-Engineering

Avoid over-specification
Complex systems tend to create more gaps; staying simple is what makes a control real

Original Text (English)

8.1.1 Operability (Function and Ergonomics)

A holistic approach should take account of all the operational parameters involved when designing control strategies. Operability assessments should be conducted with the participation of workers and a full review of the operations required in setup, operation, intervention, disassembly, upset recovery, and cleaning.

An ergonomic assessment should be conducted and should include evaluations, such as reach and force required to perform tasks. Ergonomics is the science of fitting the task to the person rather than the person to the task.

8.1.2 Impact (Cost, Time, Disruption to Process)

A cost benefit analysis should be undertaken to ensure that the benefits of a well-designed system are maximized. A lifetime cost analysis should be used to select the most appropriate solution. The time and disruption caused by the implementation should be taken into account in the evaluation.

For example, a closed system may meet all the requirements for product and worker safety without dedication, but may have a very long time between campaigns due to the cleaning process. An evaluation on the impact may lead some manufacturers to dedicate some equipment for purely operational reasons.

8.1.3 Minimize Over Engineering of Solutions

One of the largest costs, and a significant impact on operability, is the over-design and over-specification of a solution. Complex engineering is much easier to achieve than a simple elegant, operational solution. The unnecessary complication of controls, features, etc., is common and can lead to increased product or worker exposure if tasks are too complicated.

Workers may bypass the complex system and others, such as the cleaning personnel, may have an increased exposure potential. With proper use of risk management tools, the solutions should identify the areas requiring risk reduction, while indicating areas that are already within the acceptable risk range, and therefore, do not require further attention.

Care should be taken in accepting vendor statements of performance such as "Equipment X performs to Y OEL", as the statement is incomplete without additional data such as duration of task. The "keep it simple principle" should be applied.

Commentary and Practical Application

8.1.1 Operability: People Are at the Core of the System

"Ergonomics is the science of fitting the task to the person rather than the person to the task" - this sentence expresses a design philosophy that is often overlooked:

  • Wrong thinking: this process is too complicated, so operators need more training
  • Right thinking: this process is too complicated for operators, so the design needs to be improved

The operability assessment must cover the whole equipment life cycle: setup, operation, intervention, disassembly, upset recovery and cleaning.

Cleaning is often the aspect most easily overlooked, yet how easy it is to clean directly determines the actual cross-contamination risk.

8.1.2 Impact Assessment: Whole-Life-Cycle Cost

The guide requires the use of "lifetime cost analysis" rather than looking only at the initial investment cost (CAPEX).

Typical example: closed system vs dedicated equipment:

  • Closed system (shared): lower CAPEX, but each cleaning requires a long campaign interval, which reduces capacity utilization → high OPEX
  • Dedicated equipment: high CAPEX, but no campaign cleaning is needed and capacity utilization improves → low OPEX, and the cross-contamination risk is also lower

Deciding on equipment sharing purely on "whether the cleaning standard can be met", without considering the impact of the campaign interval on the production schedule, often leads to suboptimal decisions.

8.1.3 Minimize Over-Engineering: Complexity Is a Risk

The guide's counter-intuitive insight: a complex control system is itself a source of risk.

Three typical failure modes:

  1. Worker bypass: the system is too hard to operate → workers find shortcuts → the control becomes a dead letter
  2. Cleaning dead spots: the more complex the engineering design, the more spots are hard to clean → residue risk increases
  3. Maintenance exposure: complex systems need more frequent maintenance → more opportunities for maintenance staff to be exposed

"Complex engineering is much easier to achieve than a simple elegant, operational solution" - this sentence is a warning to engineers: designing a complex system is relatively easy; designing a simple and effective one is the real challenge.

Analogy: The Backfire of Password Rules

Requiring passwords to contain upper and lower case, digits and special characters, and to be changed every 30 days (over-engineering):

  • Result 1: employees write passwords on sticky notes and stick them on the monitor
  • Result 2: passwords increasingly look like P@ssw0rd1, P@ssw0rd2...

The more complex the system, the more people tend to bypass it. The same applies to the design of GMP controls: over-design not only wastes money but may leave the overall risk higher than a simple solution would.

Reading Vendor Claims Carefully

The guide specifically warns that a vendor statement such as "equipment X can achieve OEL Y" is incomplete without the following information:

  • Duration of the task
  • The specific actions of the operator
  • Cleaning frequency and method
  • Environmental conditions (temperature, humidity, airflow)

When purchasing closed systems or engineering control equipment, ask the vendor for the specific test conditions and data rather than accepting an isolated OEL claim.

CDMO

CDMO Practical Implications: A Framework for Accept / Decline Decisions

Integrating the principles of Ch.7 + Ch.8 to establish criteria for CDMO decisions on accepting highly potent products and investing in facilities

Core Question Framework

Below is a checklist of questions a CDMO should answer systematically when evaluating whether to accept a highly potent or high-risk product:

Risk Evaluation (Ch.7)

  • Can the client provide ADE / HBEL values? If not, can the TTC be used in the interim?
  • Are the facility's existing exposure data within the ADE safety margin? (Green zone? Yellow zone? Red zone?)
  • Are we using qualitative or quantitative risk assessment? Is there a numerical anchor point?
  • Has senior management set a clear acceptable-risk threshold for accepting projects?

Risk Control (Ch.8)

  • Do the proposed controls consider both IH (workers) and GxP (patients)?
  • Has an Operability assessment been done? Can operators complete all tasks without bypassing the system?
  • Has a whole-life-cycle cost analysis been done? What is the impact of the campaign cleaning interval on scheduling?
  • Is the control solution simple enough? Are there signs of over-design?
  • Is there a multidisciplinary team (toxicology, IH, engineering, operations, QA) evaluating it together?
Decision Tree and Practical Recommendations (Commentary)

Three Paths for the CDMO Accept / Decline Decision

Based on the risk evaluation results, a CDMO facing a highly potent product usually has three paths:

  1. Accept as-is: existing facility exposure is already within the green zone of the ADE; only monitoring and records need strengthening, with no additional investment
  2. Accept with investment: exposure is in the yellow zone; specific engineering controls (such as closed systems, high-efficiency ventilation, dedicated equipment) must be invested in, and the project is accepted once the cost-benefit analysis is passed
  3. Decline: exposure is in the red zone, or the investment cost exceeds the commercial return, or compliance cannot be reached within an acceptable time

Key point: declining is not a failure but a responsible business decision. Accepting a product that cannot be managed safely damages a CDMO's reputation and regulatory compliance far more than losing one client.

Avoiding the "Knee-Jerk Response"

The guide does not explicitly use the term "knee-jerk", but the spirit runs through the whole chapter: faced with a high-risk product, one should not reflexively commit to heavy engineering controls, but should instead:

  • First quantify the risk (compare with the ADE / OEL) to confirm that the problem really exists
  • Evaluate whether existing controls are already sufficient (avoid over-engineering)
  • Invest only where confirmed necessary
  • After investment, verify that the effect meets expectations (quantitative confirmation)

Every additional control has a cost: financial cost, complexity cost and operator workload cost. Adding cost out of proportion, without a corresponding risk reduction, is the essence of over-engineering.

Trigger Conditions for Facility Investment Decisions

Clear trigger conditions that warrant investing in facility improvements:

  • Measured exposure > ADE × 0.5 (approaching a 50% safety margin)
  • Cleaning validation data show insufficient consistency
  • Highly potent products from several clients operating in the facility at the same time
  • Equipment complexity leads to unexpected procedural deviations by operators

Situations where investment is not warranted: the risk evaluation result is already in the green zone and investment is made only because a new product "feels dangerous" - "preventive" over-design with no data to support it.

✓

Section Key Takeaways

Ch.7 Risk Evaluation + Ch.8 Risk Control - Review of Core Principles

Ch.7 Risk Evaluation - Five Key Points

  • 1. Evaluation is comparison: Risk Evaluation = comparing the analysis results with standards such as the ADE / HBEL / OEL
  • 2. Three-zone framework: acceptable (green) / reduction needed (yellow) / unacceptable (red), which must be clearly defined in advance
  • 3. Numerical anchors: ADE = patient standard; OEL/OEB = worker standard; TTC = safe lower limit when no toxicological data exist
  • 4. Be careful with qualitative terms: high / medium / low must be linked to numerical triggers, otherwise they are not defensible
  • 5. Proportionality: documentation depth is proportionate to the level of risk; avoid over-documenting low-risk items

Ch.8 Risk Control - Five Key Points

  • 1. Dual balance: control strategies for IH (workers) and GxP (patients) must be considered together; do not favor one at the expense of the other
  • 2. Holistic thinking: formulation, equipment, facility, operation - none can be left out, and any single change requires the overall impact to be re-evaluated
  • 3. Three criteria: Operability / Impact / Minimize Over-Engineering
  • 4. Human factors first: the task fits the person, not the person the task - a system so complex that workers bypass it is not a good control system
  • 5. Simplicity: KISS (Keep It Simple) is not a compromise but precise design based on a real understanding of the risk
9

Chapter 9: Risk Reduction

p85-p108

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture | p85–p98

Contents

9. Original Text — English

9   Risk Reduction

ICH Q9 states that risk reduction is the implementation of control strategies to mitigate or avoid the risk. Risk reduction can be achieved by addressing the severity and/or probability of harm as well as provide improvements in the detection of the hazards.

Where the results of a risk assessment have identified the need for control strategies to reduce the risk of exposure to a safe level, a hierarchy for risk reduction should be applied to prioritize all significant risks. The hierarchy is listed in priority order and should be considered and applied in this order.

Each level of the hierarchy should be applied in a way that is appropriate to the activity and consistent with the risk assessment, and each should be implemented only after consideration of the preceding level. A holistic view of the product, process, and facilities may lead to a strategy that includes partial mitigation at several levels in the hierarchy before overall risk is reduced to acceptable levels.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Core Definition: Risk Reduction

ICH Q9 defines risk reduction as "actions taken to mitigate or avoid risk" through the implementation of a control strategy. Risk can be reduced along three dimensions:

  • Reduce the severity of harm (Severity)
  • Reduce the probability of harm occurring (Probability)
  • Improve the ability to detect the hazard (Detectability)

Key Principle: A Holistic View

In practice, there is no need to insist that a single level achieves complete risk elimination. Instead, each of several levels achieves partial mitigation, and together they bring the overall risk down to an acceptable level. This layered defense approach is very common in pharmaceutical manufacturing.

Analogy: The Occupational Hygiene Hierarchy of Controls

This risk reduction hierarchy maps directly onto the Hierarchy of Controls used in Industrial Hygiene: elimination > substitution > engineering controls > administrative controls > personal protective equipment (PPE). The highest level is the most fundamental and effective form of control; PPE is regarded as the last line of defense and should not be relied on as the primary means of protection.

Original Text — English

9.1   Hierarchy of Risk Reduction

Figure 9.2🔍: Applied Hierarchy of Risk Reduction — priority order from most to least preferred control strategy.

9.1.1   Elimination

Completely eliminates the risk. Since the hazard is a constant based on the compound, completely eliminating the risk is not practical.

Elimination of potential points of exposure (process steps and/or transfers) should be considered as a method to eliminate or reduce the risk. If an exposure point can be eliminated, then the risk associated with it is removed.

9.1.2   Substitution

Substituting the hazardous material with a less hazardous material is also not practical since the hazardous material is the compound. Substitution of the process or formulation should be considered as a method to eliminate or reduce risk.

In the pharmaceutical industry, existing product registrations may limit the feasibility of substitution options. Addressing these issues early in development prior to fixing formulations or processes is recommended.

9.1.3   Reduction

Limit material handling and reduce the potential for exposure through the use of engineering controls.

In this context, engineering controls can include the use of high integrity closed processing equipment, as well as additional containment devices such as extract-based systems and isolators. When employing these technologies, the primary concern — either worker protection or cross-contamination — should be made clear. When holistically applied, it is possible to address both aspects.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Risk Reduction Hierarchy (highest to lowest priority)

Elimination
↓
Substitution
↓
Reduction / Engineering Controls
↓
Administrative Controls
↓
PPE (last line of defense)

The higher the level = the more fundamental, the more effective, and the first to be considered

9.1.1 Elimination — Practical Meaning

The toxicity of an active pharmaceutical ingredient (API) is an inherent property, so the hazardous substance itself cannot simply be "eliminated". The focus of elimination is therefore on eliminating exposure points, that is, reducing the number of process steps and material transfers. Examples: combining process steps (one-pot processor) and reducing open weighing operations.

9.1.2 Substitution — Formulation and Process Substitution

The API itself cannot be substituted, but the following can be:

  • Process substitution: adding a liquid instead of a powder
  • Formulation substitution: direct compression instead of wet granulation; selecting low-dust excipients
  • Dosage form substitution: filled hard/soft capsules instead of uncoated tablets

Key reminder: once a product has obtained marketing authorization (Registration), changes to the formulation and process carry major regulatory cost, so these options must be considered at the very start of development.

9.1.3 Engineering Controls — A Dual Purpose

Closed systems and isolators protect in two directions at the same time:

  • Worker protection (IH): preventing API exposure from affecting employee health
  • GxP cross-contamination protection: preventing the API from contaminating other products or the environment

The two directions sometimes conflict (for example, positive pressure benefits GxP but is unfavorable for IH), so they must be evaluated together (holistic approach).

Original Text — English

9.1.4   Administrative (Procedural) Controls

The proper use of risk controls should be described in SOPs. Administrative procedures should ensure that personnel understand the practical implications of the risks set out in the SOPs. Failure mode procedures that clearly indicate the actions to be completed, and the communication required in case of failures, also should be prepared.

Since success with the procedures is dependent upon adherence to the procedures (compliance), attention should be paid to behavior management and tracking activities, such as cleaning validation or purity testing.

A key element of a quality management system is to ensure the compliance of personnel with policies, work, processes, and procedures. The quality culture of an organization generally fits one of the progressive descriptions in Table 9.1.

9.1.4.1   The Impact of Culture

The quality culture progresses through the stages described in Table 9.1, and is proportionate to the perception of management commitment to "Quality in the Workplace" by personnel. Engaged and capable personnel, coupled with well written risk control policies and procedures (incorporating worker input) form the foundation of effective risk management.

Staff turnover or the use of temporary staff especially in critical areas, is also an important consideration. As such, reliance on manual controls will increase risk and may require additional or more frequent use of detection methods.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Core Requirements for Administrative Controls

Administrative controls are the key supplement that follows engineering controls in the risk reduction hierarchy, and they must include:

  • Clear SOPs: documenting how risk control measures are to be used correctly
  • Failure Mode Procedures: the actions to take on failure and the reporting chain
  • Behavior management and tracking: monitoring activities such as cleaning validation and purity testing

David's Practical View — Culture Matters More Than SOPs

On the aseptic filling floor, Stage 3 is the basic requirement: operators keep behaving correctly even when working alone. The hallmark of Stage 4 is "peer correction", which is the highest level of quality assurance for aseptic technique. Management's "visible commitment" is the key lever for driving the culture upgrade.

Special Risk from Temporary Staff

For temporary staff, or positions with frequent staff turnover, the continuity of quality culture can be broken, which is equivalent to dropping back to a lower stage. Controls that depend on manual operation (manual controls) inherently increase risk; replace them with engineering controls wherever possible, or increase the detection frequency to compensate.

Table 9.1 — Four Stages of Quality Culture Maturity

Original Text — English

9.2   Risk Reduction: Routes of Exposure

Risk reduction strategies for each of the routes for cross-contamination are discussed.

9.2.1   Mix-up

Procedures, protocols, physical controls, and redundant monitoring can help reduce mix-up. Methods that ensure detection of mix-ups are necessary, as it is unlikely that mix-up can be eliminated totally. Techniques to reduce the risk of mix-up include:

  • Provision of redundant electronic verification of materials
  • Color coding
  • Bar coding and reading of materials, equipment, etc.
  • Clear labeling of room status (in use/product present) as well as of used equipment (cleanliness, contaminant present etc.)
  • Removal of unused API and other materials from plant local stores at the end of a campaign
  • Using locked cages to store APIs with careful chain of custodian, control of the key
  • Radio Frequency Identification (RFID)
  • Separate redundant manual checks
  • Selecting transfer and utility connect technologies that are unique to the process
  • PAT and NIR verifications
  • Label creation at point of application
  • Physical separation of high risk products/materials

It is the manufacturer's responsibility to develop and demonstrate a well thought out and executed strategy for the prevention of mix-ups that takes full account of human fallibility.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

9.2.1 Mix-up Prevention — Key Principles

Mix-up is a risk that "can never be completely eliminated", so the emphasis is on redundant design in the detection layer:

  • Electronic redundant verification: barcodes, RFID, electronic batch records
  • Visual identification: color coding and room status labeling
  • PAT / NIR: real-time material identity confirmation, stronger than conventional laboratory testing
  • End-of-campaign clearance: removing all API and materials after the campaign ends to prevent misuse in subsequent batches
  • Human factors engineering: accepting that "human error is unavoidable", so the system design must account for human fallibility

Analogy: The Material Verification Process in Aseptic Filling

In aseptic filling operations, the double check of API weighing and preparation is the administrative control layer against mix-up, while the electronic batch record (eBMR) automatically locking the material ID is the engineering control layer. Only with the two layers stacked together can the probability distribution of human error be covered.

Original Text — English

9.2.2   Retention

The development of a comprehensive cleaning program based on science, risk assessment, and statistical analysis will provide the greatest level of safety for the patient. Equipment should be designed for ease of cleaning and for the confirmation of the effectiveness of cleaning. Techniques to reduce the risk of retention include:

  • Clean to set limits consistently
  • Cleaning process design (avoid damaging highly polished product contact surfaces)
  • Certified visual inspection program
  • Overall cleaning program risk assessment
  • PAT (Process Analytical Technology)
  • On-line and at-line monitoring
  • Process capability evaluations of swab sample data
  • Use single use disposable product contact parts
  • Dedicated product contact parts

9.2.2.1   Cleaning Validation Program

A site's cleaning validation program is often the most visible and audited part of its overall cross-contamination control strategy. In order to be both effective and compliant, a cleaning process validation must:

  • Be based on a science and risk-based understanding of the hazards
  • Be executed using already qualified equipment systems (water or CIP)
  • Be performed following Quality Unit preapproved protocols
  • Include multiple test repetitions to demonstrate consistency

Note: unlike manufacturing process validation, there is no current expectation to formally justify the number of validation runs — a three-run cleaning validation is still typical and generally acceptable. Science and process knowledge should always be the first consideration.

9.2.2.2   Continued Process Verification (CPV) for Cleaning

CPV plans for cleaning processes should include both aspects of acceptability: analytical measurement of residuals against a predetermined health-based safety limit and the visual observations of personnel inspecting cleaned equipment for any visible residue.

Analytical Criteria: where acceptance limits are well above visual detection and the cleaning process is robust, analytical sampling may be reduced as justified by risk assessments. Equally where the acceptance limits are well below visual detection — especially for manual cleaning processes — analytical sampling may be increased.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

9.2.2 Retention Risk Reduction — Three Pillars

  • Equipment design: easy-to-clean design (CIP-able), minimized contact surface area, elimination of dead legs
  • Cleaning validation program: science-based, setting and maintaining cleaning limits
  • Materials strategy: single-use or dedicated product contact parts

Cleaning Validation — Key Differences from Process Validation

Process validation: ICH Q7/Q10 require the number of validation batches (PPQ) to be justified (typically 3 batches, but this needs scientific support).

Cleaning validation: 3 batches is still widely accepted as the standard, and a formal justification of the number of batches is not required, but the number should still be decided on the basis of science and an evaluation of batch-to-batch variability.

Important: scientific understanding comes first; 3 batches is a starting point, not an end point.

CPV: Continued Process Verification for Cleaning

After validation is complete and commercial production begins, the CPV monitoring plan needs to integrate two aspects:

  • Analytical monitoring: residue level vs. HBEL (health-based exposure limit)
  • Visual inspection: visually clean as a supplementary criterion

Principles for risk-based adjustment of monitoring frequency:

  • Acceptance limit >> visual detection limit → analytical sampling frequency can be reduced
  • Acceptance limit << visual detection limit (for example, highly potent drugs) → analytical sampling frequency must be increased

Application of PAT in Cleaning Monitoring

Online TOC (Total Organic Carbon) monitoring can be linked directly, or by correlation, to the residue level. If this correlation has been validated, the questions of sampling time points and sampling locations are greatly simplified. This is one of the most mature applications of PAT in cleaning CPV.

Original Text — English

9.2.3   Mechanical Transfer

Techniques to reduce the risk of mechanical transfer include:

  • Providing a boundary so that mechanical transfer is minimized (see Section 9.5)
  • Gown/glove change at the product boundary; use of material and personnel airlocks and decontamination procedures
  • Workers should be gowned to protect the person, personal clothing, and the product from potential contamination
  • Gloves should be worn at all times when in the product boundary; a second pair should be worn during critical operations such as connecting/disconnecting transfer systems and cleaning
  • Removing critical gowning items at a defined point using a technique which avoids cross-contamination
  • Providing flow routes that keep clean and dirty equipment, people, materials, separate
  • Using airlocks for personnel, materials, or both to segregate work areas
  • Wiping or cleaning of the exterior of material containers when removing from production area
  • Physically separate rooms to separate products and processes
  • Using "clean" construction to facilitate removal of potential contaminants from surfaces
  • Telephones and similar devices requiring manual activation should be replaced by hands-free or touch pad devices
  • Providing electronic means to pass information and data between the room/suite and the GxP area

9.2.4   Airborne Transfer

Techniques to reduce the risk of airborne transfer include:

  • Closed process systems
  • Engineering controls to reduce the degree of openness of a process enclosure
  • HVAC arrangement to flush critical zones with clean air and move airborne product away from room entry, operator space, or other potential vectors for cross-contamination
  • Airlocks, single and double, with or without positive pressure cascades
  • Gowning/decontamination of people and materials passing in and out
  • Use of HVAC and pressure regimens to minimize airborne transfer to protect workers and product
  • Use of appropriate filtration to capture airborne particulate and prevent recirculation

Note: many of the techniques are similar to those listed to reduce mechanical transfer.

Wipe samples taken outside the GxP area may show contamination due to failures of gowning procedures or even mechanical transfer on paperwork. The presence of drug substance at or near the limits for non-product contact is an indication of a failed system and careful assessment of all aspects of the hierarchy of risk control is essential to remediate.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Mechanical Transfer — Key Points for Gowning Management

  • Changing gowns/gloves at the product boundary is the most basic protection against mechanical transfer
  • Critical operations (connecting and disconnecting lines, cleaning) require double gloves (outer gloves)
  • The gown removal (doffing) technique must be standardized to avoid the "contaminated side" touching clean items
  • Manual devices such as telephones should be replaced with hands-free equipment to reduce opportunities for mechanical transfer via contact surfaces

Airborne Transfer — Core Logic of HVAC Pressure Differential Design

The goal of pressure cascade design is to control the direction of airflow, ensuring that air flows from the "clean area to the potentially contaminated area" and preventing API particles from escaping outward. For highly potent compounds, special attention is also needed to the following:

  • GxP requirement: positive pressure (protect the product from the environment)
  • IH requirement: negative pressure (contain the hazardous API inside)
  • The two conflict → a case-by-case evaluation is needed, usually resolved with containment + HEPA exhaust

Positive Swab Result Outside the GxP Area — A Warning of System Failure

If API is detected in a swab sample taken outside the GxP boundary (for example, an office corridor or outside the change room), this indicates that the entire risk control system has failed (possible causes include a gown removal error, mechanical transfer of documents, or loss of HVAC pressure). The entire control hierarchy must then be re-examined; it is not enough to remedy only the surface symptom.

Original Text — English

9.3   The Role of Pharmaceutical R&D in Risk Reduction

ICH Q8(R2): "The aim of pharmaceutical development is to design a quality product and its manufacturing process to consistently deliver the intended performance of the product… quality should be built in by design."

Opportunities to reduce or eliminate exposure should be considered as early as possible in the development of the product and the process.

The toxicological properties of a compound can be assessed early in the development process. An Occupational Exposure Band (OEB) is likely to have been assigned and it is possible to estimate an ADE from the OEB. This information may help influence formulation design at an early phase of development.

Priorities for a formulation scientist include: Bioavailability, Stability, Ability to manufacture. Physical form and particle size of the active drug substance together with the choice of excipients are considered.

Direct Compression / Granulation

Direct compression formulations can be employed to minimize cross-contamination and worker exposure by combining the active ingredient with suitable excipients. The formulator may consider selecting excipients that generate very little dust, such as silicified microcrystalline cellulose or mannitol. Processing steps can be significantly reduced — dispensing, blending, and compression — eliminating fluid bed drying and dry milling.

A well-designed granulation process should offer similar benefits. A granulating solvent can dampen the API and reduce the risk of aerosols. Single pot granulation systems allow granulation and drying in the same vessel, reducing transfer exposure potential.

Coating and Encapsulation

Coating the solid dosage form can significantly reduce the risk of cross-contamination. A film or sugar coating provides a significant barrier, protecting the active drug and reducing exposure risk in downstream operations such as packaging. It minimizes risks to downstream handlers — pharmacists, nurses, and patients.

Encapsulation allows powders to be filled directly into a shell. Capsules can be sealed, over-encapsulated, or film coated. Encapsulation can be a dusty process and close attention should be given to dedusting.

Liquid / Semi-solid Fill

Formulating the active substance in a liquid or semi-solid vehicle and filling into a capsule (hard or soft shell) is a particularly useful approach for high hazard compounds, as it removes any powder handling stages once the compound is solubilized.

Overall, early containment of an active drug substance via formulation — especially for more hazardous materials — may bring long-term handling advantages and risk reduction. There may also be cost benefit from reducing the need for specialized containment technology, dedicated equipment, or dedicated rooms.

Once formulations and processes are registered, it can be very costly and time consuming to make changes. Formulation scientists should pay close attention to opportunities to reduce risk throughout the entire manufacturing and supply chain, as early as possible in development.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Linking Quality by Design (QbD) and Risk Reduction

The ICH Q8(R2) principle of "quality by design": designing a "low-risk" formulation and process already at the development stage is more effective, and costs less, than remedying problems later with engineering controls.

The R&D scientist's risk reduction toolbox:

  • Formulation Design
  • Process Selection
  • Salt Selection → affects aqueous solubility, stability, and particle size
  • Particle Size Control → the smaller the particle size, the greater the dust exposure risk

The Regulatory Reality of "Early Formulation Lock = Late-Stage Cost"

Once the formulation and process have been approved in the marketing authorization, any change may require a supplemental filing (Variation/Supplement), consuming time and resources. This makes it critically important to apply risk thinking in the development stage: the impact of each formulation strategy on long-term manufacturing risk must be fully evaluated before the formulation is "locked".

Particle Size Control — A Double-Edged Sword

API particle size affects two directions:
Therapeutic perspective: smaller particle size → higher dissolution rate → better bioavailability (especially important for poorly soluble drugs)
Safety perspective: smaller particle size (<10 µm) → more likely to become respirable particles → higher IH exposure risk
Particle size design must therefore consider both the PK target and operational safety, a typical example of the risk balancing R&D has to do.

Encapsulation as the Best Risk Reduction Measure

For highly potent compounds in OEB 4/5, liquid-fill capsules are the most thorough way to eliminate powder handling steps: once the API is dissolved in a liquid medium, all subsequent steps are liquid operations, the dust exposure risk falls to almost zero, and the reliance on dedicated equipment and high-grade containment facilities is greatly reduced.

Original Text — English

9.4   Process and Related Technologies

Properly designed and operated processes, equipment, and other related technologies can reduce the risk of cross-contamination from mix-up, retention, mechanical transfer, and airborne transfer. During process and equipment design, the reduction of one set of risks (e.g., IH) should not negatively impact the risks to the other areas (e.g., GxP).

For most formulations, the only stage of the process in which the API is handled in its pure form is during dispensing, API sampling, and API addition to the first process; therefore, these operations require close attention. After blend uniformity or final mix, the risk increases because the acceptable limit at single dosage can be much lower than the ADE.

API addition in liquid or slurry form should result in far lower exposure potential than dry powder addition, unless there is a danger of aerosol formation on addition of liquid under pressure, or the solvent base is conducive to transdermal migration.

Integration of unit processes: systems that integrate a number of process steps, such as "one-pot" processors (granulator/dryers), reduce the need for transfers between unit operations.

Use of closed process: equipment vendors may claim a process is "closed," but the detail of such claims should be fully evaluated. Will there be a need for any process intervention?

  • Manual scrape down
  • Performing process corrections
  • Changing contaminated or blocked parts such as mill screens
  • Extract samples
  • Determine process end points
  • Perform cleaning
  • Upset recovery

The impact of interventions on the potential for exposure should be evaluated. In-line measuring devices or automated devices to scrape down or clear blinded mill screens should be considered.

Selection of process equipment that reduces exposure potential: hammer mills can result in far higher exposure potential than cone mills. Charging a bin blender can provide lower risk through close coupling than charging a V blender.

Selection of process equipment to reduce retention: minimize surface area; eliminate ledges and crevices; reduce changeover frequency; use polished product contact surfaces.

9.4.1   Redundancy

Redundancy is the provision of more than one element performing the function so that if one fails, the next takes up the required duty and detection devices alarm the failure of the primary system.

If a system is dependent on a single critical interface, it is likely to fail; when redundancy is introduced, the likelihood of failure diminishes. A robust system can be designed based on a series of non-optimal redundancies.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Peak API Risk Points in the Process

The exposure risk from neat (pure) API is concentrated at these points in the process:

  • Dispensing
  • API Sampling
  • First Addition

Once the API is mixed into the excipients, the exposure risk falls because of dilution. However, after the final blend / final mix (blend uniformity / final mix), the API content per unit dose approaches the final dose, so if the formulation were ingested the risk rises again; at this point the permitted residue based on the ADE is actually stricter.

A "Closed Process" Does Not Mean Zero Exposure — Intervention Analysis

A supplier may claim a "closed system", but all necessary interventions must be evaluated:

  • Manual scrape down
  • Mill screen change
  • Sampling
  • Cleaning
  • Upset recovery

Every intervention is a potential exposure event, and the exposure level and the adequacy of the control measures must be evaluated for each one. PAT (such as online endpoint analysis) is the best engineering control for eliminating the exposure from sampling interventions.

Effect of Equipment Selection on Exposure — A Mill Case

Hammer Mill: high energy, high speed → generates a large amount of fine powder (<10 µm) → high exposure potential

Cone Mill: low-energy shear → coarser particle size → significantly lower exposure potential

For the same functional requirement, choosing equipment with "lower exposure potential" is itself risk reduction at the engineering control level, with no need for additional containment facilities.

9.4.1 Redundancy — A System Reliability Principle

The concept of redundancy: multiple components are provided to perform the same function; when the primary component fails, the secondary component takes over automatically and an alarm is triggered.

In risk control, a single critical interface is a point of weakness in the system:

  • A single seal → if it fails, contamination leaks out
  • A single differential pressure sensor → if it fails, monitoring is lost
  • A single filter → if it fails, the environment goes out of control

Redundant design can achieve high reliability for the overall system through a combination of "several sub-optimal components", without every component having to meet the highest specification.

Application of Redundancy in Aseptic Filling Facilities

Isolator HVAC systems are usually designed with double HEPA filtration (primary + secondary), together with differential pressure monitoring alarms. If the primary filter triggers an alarm because of excessive differential pressure (clogging), the operator has enough time to respond, rather than the environment going out of control immediately. This is a typical application of redundant design in a GxP environment.

Original Text — English

9.5   The Role of Facility Engineering in Risk Reduction

A properly designed and operated facility can reduce the risk of cross-contamination from mix-up, mechanical transfer, and airborne transfer. Proper flow of workers, product, equipment, materials, and wastes within a facility can have a significant impact on maintaining risk of cross-contamination at or below acceptable limits.

9.5.1   Spatial Configuration

In planning a facility, the relationship of areas should be carefully considered to minimize the risk of cross-contamination. Individual ISPE Baseline® Guides address this subject specifically for the type of facility being reviewed, such as those considering API and Oral Solid Dosage (OSD).

9.5.2   The Control Concept

Regardless of the hazard of a compound, it should be handled in such a way that the compound remains within the room or suite in which it is processed. The compound should not be detectable above acceptable limits outside the areas directly involved in processing the product.

9.5.2.1   Hierarchy of Boundaries

There are a number of boundaries that contain the pharmaceutical compound (Figure 9.4🔍):

  • Process Boundary: the process equipment and the interface through which it is charged, discharged, sampled, intervened, and recovered. Can vary from closed to open.
  • Room or Suite Boundary: the room or suite in which one product is processed on a batch or campaign basis. May be product dedicated. Must contain the product at all times so that the common GxP corridor is never exposed to unsafe concentrations. May have material and personnel airlocks.
  • GxP Boundary: the boundary between the outside world and the pharmaceutical environment — keeps outside contamination out and pharmaceutical materials in.

Facility Zones (Figure 9.5🔍):

  1. Office, administrative, cafeteria, corridors — outside scope; product should never be in these areas; street clothes worn.
  2. Warehousing for product-related goods (GxP areas). Raw API and intermediates should be segregated.
  3. Controlled areas for gowning/material transfer — transitioning between outside and core GxP zones.
  4. GxP corridors — flow routes; should not be product contaminated. Visible residues are a cause for concern.
  5. Product rooms — single product campaign; process rooms are the final product boundary. Fully closed systems may allow more than one product per room.
  6. Laboratories — restrict access; prevent contamination and worker exposure.
  7. Technical space — utilities; adequate safeguards for dust collection, air handling filter changes.

Alternate Arrangements:

  • Single door to corridor: positive or negative pressure; positive pressure rooms place corridor at risk.
  • Single stage airlock: significant improvement in containment; may be over or under engineered.
  • Double stage airlocks: allows clean gowning and de-gowning in separate rooms; overcomes airborne and mechanical transfer.
Analysis and Application — Commentary

Core Control Philosophy of Facility Engineering

The ultimate goal of facility design: to ensure the compound "stays where it is supposed to be". This containment concept is achieved layer by layer through three boundaries:

  • Process Boundary: equipment level — the first line of defense, closest to the API
  • Room/Suite Boundary: facility level — protects the GxP corridor
  • GxP Boundary: site level — isolates the outside world

The three boundaries form defense in depth, so that when one layer fails the outer layers still provide protection.

Logic for Choosing Among Three Airlock Options

Single Door corridor: lowest cost, but a positive-pressure room may contaminate the corridor; suitable for low-hazard compounds.

Single Airlock: significantly improves containment and suits moderate-hazard compounds; however, avoid the unnecessary cost that comes with over-engineering.

Double Airlock: separate "clean side" gowning and "dirty side" de-gowning, controlling both mechanical transfer and airborne transfer; suitable for high-hazard compounds (OEB 4/5). It may be over-design in low-hazard situations.

Analogy: The GxP Corridor Is a "Sacred, Must-Not-Be-Contaminated" Passage

In a multi-product facility, the GxP corridor is the "shared highway" for all product rooms. Once the corridor is contaminated with visible residue or with API above the acceptance limit, it means that the cross-contamination risk of the entire facility is out of control, because from the corridor one can enter the manufacturing area of every other product. The corridor must therefore be strictly maintained in a "zero product" state.

Original Text — English

9.5.3   Material and People Flows

The design of facilities to provide logical flow routes is complex. One common design is to segregate "clean" and "dirty" corridors. In this ideal scenario, only "clean" personnel, equipment, and product that is robustly contained and has acceptable surface concentrations can pass down the "clean" corridor, and only potentially contaminated personnel, equipment, and product pass down the "dirty" corridor.

Application of the product boundary decontamination concept for personnel, equipment, and product removes the need for segregated corridors.

Minimizing personnel and material flows is considered good practice, and for reasons outside the scope of this Guide, segregation of people and material movement can be required for safety and security reasons.

9.5.3.1   Material and Personnel Air Locks (MAL / PAL)

Airlocks may have several purposes, including:

  • Prevent mechanical transfer of product from the product boundary into the GxP corridor
  • Provide a means to maintain pressure cascades
  • Demonstrate entry into a controlled area
  • Prevent contamination of the facility and product by external contaminants
  • Provide an area for gowning/de-gowning
  • Provide an area for operator to don controlled-use gowning to prevent mechanical transfer

A key element of any MAL or PAL is that there is a clean side and a dirty side with a physical barrier where the two sides meet.

A MAL requires three zones to minimize cross-contamination:

  1. Clean
  2. Transit
  3. Dirty

Clean side personnel place material in the designated transit zone. Dirty side personnel collect materials without entering the transit zone. Decontamination takes place in the dirty zone. Personnel should not transit through a MAL because of the risk of mechanical transfer from contaminated clothing.

Airlocks are frequently operated with a positive differential pressure to adjacent rooms or corridors to prevent the transit of airborne contamination between these areas. Sticky mats should be used to further prevent mechanical transfer out of the airlocks.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

Pros and Cons of the "Clean/Dirty Dual Corridor" Design

Ideal state: only clean personnel/equipment pass through the clean corridor; only contaminated items pass through the dirty corridor → complete segregation.

Practical challenges:

  • Floor space requirement increases substantially (each corridor needs twice the space)
  • Operational complexity increases, and inconsistent execution easily causes the concept to fail
  • May be over-engineering for some compounds

Alternative: implement a "decontamination concept" at each product boundary, in which personnel, equipment, and materials are decontaminated before leaving the product boundary; this can replace the dual corridor design.

MAL (Material Airlock) Three-Zone Model

Clean Side
Place materials into the transit zone
↔
Transit Zone
Temporary material holding, decontamination operations
↔
Dirty Side
Remove materials, decontaminate

Key rule: personnel on the dirty side must not enter the transit zone, and personnel must not pass through the MAL (because their clothing may carry contamination).

PAL Design Practice in Aseptic Filling Facilities

In an aseptic filling facility, the PAL serves two purposes at once: GxP sterility protection (against contamination coming in from outside) and IH protection (against API being carried out). In the pressure design, the change room is usually set at positive pressure relative to the corridor (to prevent microorganisms from entering), but for a facility handling highly potent API, a negative-pressure decontamination chamber may need to be added after the gowning steps. A sticky mat is a low-cost engineering control against mechanical transfer from the soles of shoes and should be included as standard PAL equipment.

Original Text — English

9.5.4   System Failure or Upset

However good the system, occasional failure can occur as a result of human, mechanical, or electrical failure, and these can lead to exposure. As part of the risk reduction process, the outcome of failure should be addressed to minimize risk.

In a well-regulated closed processing environment, the only justification for airlocks is to provide redundancy. Since failures of closed systems consistently show lower exposures than the same events in open operation, and because monitoring systems will indicate that failure has occurred, it may be possible to make the boundary interfaces much simpler.

Where open processing of high hazard compounds occurs, this does not apply.

9.5.5   Finishes

All GxP areas should have hard, washable, coved, and crevice-free finishes that are sufficiently leak tight to enable pressure cascades to be created and maintained.

Analysis and Application — Commentary

9.5.4 System Failure Management — Failure Mode Design

A "good system also fails occasionally" is not an excuse but a design premise. Risk control design must consider the consequences of failure in advance:

  • What is the "fail-safe" default state on failure?
  • Can the monitoring system detect the failure in time and trigger an alarm?
  • Is the exposure after failure still within an acceptable range?

Consequences of failure: closed systems vs. open systems: even when a closed system fails, the resulting exposure is usually still far below the normal exposure of an open system operating in the same scenario. High-hazard compounds should therefore be given priority for closed systems, since even in the failed state there is an additional safety margin.

9.5.5 Building Materials and Surface Finishes — GxP Area Standards

Four core requirements for GxP area building materials:

  • Hard: able to withstand chemical attack from cleaning agents and disinfectants
  • Washable: surfaces can withstand repeated wet cleaning without degrading
  • Coved: the floor-to-wall junction has a coved (radiused) design, eliminating right-angle corners where dust accumulates
  • Crevice-free: the absence of crevices ensures thorough cleaning can be achieved, and the sealing is sufficient to maintain the pressure differential

These four characteristics serve two purposes at once: GxP cleaning validation compliance and pressure differential maintenance (HVAC containment).

Engineering Significance of the Cove

Right-angle joints are blind spots for cleaning: mops and cleaning brushes cannot reach deep into the corner, and API powder can accumulate there over a long time. A cove makes the floor and wall form a continuous curved surface, so cleaning liquid flows through naturally, greatly reducing the retention risk. This is the most intuitive example of "Design for Cleanability" in facility building materials.

Original Text — English

9.5.6   Dedicated Principles

One route to prevent retention is to dedicate equipment; this can vary from the dedication of an entire process train to individual pieces of equipment that may be dedicated because of size and complexity.

Another route is to use disposable product contact parts on a batch or campaign basis. This is a very practical solution in some cases such as aseptic formulation, but impractical with the current state of technology for many other processes such as fluid bed drying.

Both dedication and disposable principles can be limited to just the product contact parts so long as the drives and controls can be located outside the product boundary.

9.5.7   Segregation Principles

If risk cannot be reduced to acceptable levels by dedicating equipment or change parts, it may be necessary to dedicate or segregate the facility. For the purpose of this Guide, segregation means a facility in which:

  • GxP areas are physically separate
  • HVAC units are separate or arranged to prevent cross-contamination
  • Critical utilities are protected by backflow prevention and/or filtration at point of use
  • Access and corridors are separate
Analysis and Application — Commentary

9.5.6 Flexible Application of Dedication

Dedication does not have to mean an entire set of equipment or an entire facility; it can apply only to product contact parts:

  • Drives and controls can be located outside the product boundary and need not be dedicated
  • Contact parts such as mill screens, line fittings, and mixing blades can be dedicated individually or replaced with disposable alternatives
  • Single-use systems for aseptic preparation are the most mature application of this principle

A single-use fluid path is technically not feasible in some situations, such as fluid bed drying, but it has become the industry standard in liquid preparation and aseptic dispensing.

9.5.7 Four Elements of Facility Segregation

Facility-level segregation (Facility Segregation) must cover four aspects, none of which can be omitted:

  • Physical segregation: GxP areas are completely separated physically
  • HVAC segregation: independent air handling systems or special arrangements to prevent air cross-over
  • Utility protection: backflow prevention + point-of-use filtration, so that utility systems do not become a transfer pathway
  • Access segregation: separate entrances/exits and corridors, preventing personnel from carrying contamination in or out

Analogy: Utilities Are the "Invisible Cross-Contamination Pathway"

Utilities such as water for injection (WFI) piping, compressed air, and vacuum lines connect the entire facility. Without a backflow preventer or point-of-use filter, API from one product room could flow back through the utility piping and contaminate other rooms. This is a hidden cross-contamination route that is easily overlooked. The "integrity" requirement for facility segregation must include point-to-point protection of the utilities.

Reflection Exercise: Which Level Is Your Facility At?

Evaluate the risk reduction strategy of your existing facility:

  • What is the OEB level of the highest-hazard compound currently handled?
  • Is campaign manufacturing used? Does the cleaning validation strategy match the hazard level?
  • Which equipment is fully dedicated? Which has only dedicated contact parts? Which still needs improvement?
  • Does the HVAC system share return air between different products? Is there a risk?
  • Are the utilities fitted with point-of-use filters or backflow prevention devices?
Section Summary — Key Takeaways

Chapter 9 (Part A) — Core Messages

  • Risk reduction follows a hierarchy: Elimination > Substitution > Engineering Controls > Administrative Controls > PPE
  • Each route of cross-contamination (mix-up, retention, mechanical, airborne) has specific tailored control strategies
  • R&D decisions on formulation, dosage form, and particle size have long-term implications for manufacturing risk
  • Facility design must consider spatial configuration, boundary hierarchy, and flow routes
  • Dedicated principles (full or partial), segregation, and facility finishes are the engineering bedrock of contamination control
  • Administrative controls and quality culture are essential but cannot substitute for engineering controls
Section Summary — Commentary

Key Messages Consolidated from Chapter 9 (First Half)

  • Hierarchy of controls: engineering controls are always preferable to administrative controls, and PPE is the last resort
  • Development decisions determine manufacturing risk: the choice of formulation, dosage form, and particle size made during development already determines the future manufacturing risk profile
  • Each contamination pathway has its own control logic: mix-up is controlled by identification and verification; retention by cleaning design and validation; mechanical transfer by boundary management; airborne transfer by closed systems and HVAC
  • The facility is the physical foundation of risk control: boundary design, space layout, material flow, and building materials and surface finishes are all engineering means of risk reduction
  • Dedication/segregation decisions should be based on hazard assessment: not "always dedicate" or "always share", but decided on the basis of OEB, ADE, and cleaning validation feasibility
  • Quality culture (Stage 4) is the highest goal of administrative control: peer correction is the hallmark of a mature quality culture
