Learning Objectives

  • Understand the applicable scenarios and regulatory requirements for once-through and recirculation HVAC systems
  • Master the quantitative method for calculating airborne cross-contamination risk, including the relationship between particle size, mass, and filtration efficiency
  • Understand the design principles of directional airflow and pressure gradients: the application of positive pressure, negative pressure, and airlock types
  • Understand the hierarchy of containment system selection: from open operation to LEV, glovebox, and isolator
  • Master the role and limitations of PPE / RPE in the manufacture of hazardous compounds
9.6

HVAC and Environmental Control — Overview

Three levels of proximity: Room → Local → Equipment Enclosure | p98-p99

9.6 HVAC and Environmental Control

The key requirement of life science HVAC is to assure a suitable environment for the product and process with regard to temperature, humidity, and most importantly, airborne particulate, molecular, and viable cleanliness. This requirement is in addition to the usual industrial HVAC requirements of making workers comfortable and protecting them from hazardous airborne fumes, vapors, aerosols, and dust.

Often, the need to control hazardous compounds complicates environmental control as cleanliness and containment may require conflicting pressurization schemes.

As product potency increases, the need for control of airborne product/API also increases. The effectiveness of HVAC related contamination controls improve as proximity to the product increases. Three levels of increasing proximity are considered:

  • Room Level HVAC — dilutes airborne contaminants by entrainment in air streams, extracted from the room. Susceptible to behavior, technique, and operational failures.
  • Local Ventilation — "near field" effect by placing exhaust and/or supply close to emission sources. Higher particulate concentration at source enables more efficient filtration or exhaust disposal.
  • Equipment Level Ventilation — e.g., isolator, RABS, vented balance enclosure, or closed equipment. Combines physical barriers with HVAC to control particulate buildup within the enclosure. Closing a process reduces the size of the critical environment, making the surrounding room a support area.

This triumvirate of control technologies can provide significant safety and product protection benefits, individually and in concert. They can safely handle highly hazardous materials with very low OELs.

At HVAC equipment level, removal of undesirable airborne contaminants is achieved through exhaust (disposal) or depth filtration. Filters also serve as an enhanced boundary protecting against unintended backflow or migration of API dust through duct systems upon loss of HVAC. Terminal filters at air inlets and outlets of processing rooms are therefore common.

Commentary: The Three-Level HVAC Control Concept

The Three-Level Control Distance Principle

HVAC control effectiveness is inversely proportional to distance. The closer to the contamination source, the more effective the control:

  • Room Level: dilution by high air change rates; effectiveness depends on personnel behavior and operating technique, making this the most fragile level.
  • Local Ventilation: LEV (local exhaust ventilation) captures directly near the contamination source, which is more efficient than room air changes because it captures high-concentration air.
  • Equipment Level: isolators, RABS, and similar systems fully enclose the process, shrinking the "clean zone" to the smallest possible area and reducing the surrounding environment to a support area.

Analogy: The Kitchen Range Hood

Think of stir-fry cooking fumes as API dust.
Room air changes = opening a window to let the wind blow it away → poor result
LEV = a range hood pulling directly above the wok → good result
Containment equipment = cooking inside a sealed steamer → almost zero escape

Life sciences HVAC design makes each level complement the others, forming defense in depth.

Key Point: The Conflict Between Cleanliness and Containment

A common conflict in highly potent drug facilities:
- Product protection requires positive pressure (to keep external contamination out)
- Personnel and environmental protection require negative pressure (to keep API in)
At the design stage, decide which takes priority according to product toxicity (OEL/HBEL), and use airlocks to separate the two requirements.

Cross-Reference

For detailed HVAC design requirements, see:
- ISPE Good Practice Guide: HVAC (dedicated to HVAC system design)
- ISPE Baseline® Guide Vol. 3 (Sterile Manufacturing Facilities)

9.6.1

Once-Through versus Recirculation

When is 100% exhaust required for hazardous compounds? | p99-p100

9.6.1 Once-Through versus Recirculation

Recirculated HVAC systems offer energy savings and operational cost benefits over once-through systems. The air recirculated from manufacturing areas is often cleaner and much closer to the desired temperature and humidity conditions than fresh air.

However, recirculation of air from powder handling spaces, especially in multiproduct concurrent manufacturing facilities, can convey airborne product particles to other areas and may maintain aerosols in suspension, depending on particle size and room air patterns.

Regulations suggest that if air is to be recirculated, when potentially contaminated with airborne product particles, it should be adequately treated. The definition of adequate treatment is amenable to a risk-based approach, as described in Section 9.6.2.

Three Levels of HVAC Control (recap)

Commentary: Decision Logic for Once-Through vs. Recirculation Systems

Core Concepts

Once-Through system (100% Exhaust): all supply air is fresh outside air and all exhaust air is discharged outdoors (after treatment). There is no recirculation, so the cross-contamination risk is lowest, but energy consumption is very high.

Recirculation system: part or all of the return air is reused, which saves energy, but filtration is needed to remove API particles.

Core Regulatory Requirements

EU GMP Annex 15 and the GMP rules of many countries: in multi-product facilities, if air is recirculated it must be "adequately treated". What counts as "adequate" is determined by risk assessment (see 9.6.2).

For highly toxic compounds (low OEL / HBEL), "adequately treated" usually means:
→ double HEPA filtration, or
→ 100% exhaust (no recirculation at all)

Analogy: Office Air Conditioning vs. Chemical Laboratory Exhaust

It is reasonable for ordinary office air conditioning to recirculate and reuse its air (no toxic gases). But the exhaust from a chemical laboratory fume hood must be 100% discharged outdoors and must never be recirculated back into the office. The logic for handling API dust is the same: the higher the toxicity, the more strongly 100% exhaust is favored.

9.6.2

Quantitating the Risk of Airborne Cross-Contamination

Calculation method, filter efficiency tables, particle mass relationships | p99-p104 (Advanced Topic)

9.6.2 Quantitating the Risk of Airborne Cross-Contamination

(Advanced Topic)

Control of cross-contamination is primarily a matter of understanding the mass of "product A" which might be carried into "product B". For contaminants spread through the air, the process involves:

  • Understanding the potential maximum mass of airborne product that may be produced
  • Determining how much of this mass could be delivered to other spaces
  • Evaluating upset cases (e.g., spills) and potential failures of abatement equipment (failure sensitivity evaluation)

The method is similar to the approach used by Industrial Hygienists for worker safety: the actual quantity of product aerosolized during a working day is measured, and correction factors for PPE are applied.

A quantitative evaluation provides:

  • More robust understanding of contamination risks and appropriate controls
  • Verification measures through facility life
  • Data for investigations, event response planning, and critical management tasks
  • Assurance that resources are commensurate with risk — neither inadequate nor wasteful

Contamination Sources and Potency

The potency of the product and the potential maximum concentration of airborne product drive the need for more rigorous abatement. The highest particle concentration air streams — such as from dust collectors, especially when handling potent products — require the most stringent abatement and may not be candidates for recirculation.

Results from Environmental Health and Safety studies are generally expressed in µg/m³, which couples well with the quantitative method outlined here. To determine appropriate filtration, fractional removal efficiencies across twelve particle size ranges from standards such as ANSI/ASHRAE 52.2, EN 1822, EN 779, or ISO 29463-1 are used.

Filter Efficiency Examples

Table 9.2/9.3/9.4 (source tables) demonstrate mass reduction through filtration stages:

A properly installed and maintained MERV 15 (~95% ASHRAE) filter can provide a 3 log reduction in contaminants. A MERV 11 (~50% ASHRAE) gives a 1.5 log (20:1) reduction.

Large Particles — Greater Cross-Contamination Risk

While small particles (<10 µm) are of interest in worker safety (greater respirability), large particles (>10 µm) pose greater cross-contamination risk for two reasons:

  1. In any heterogeneous particle dispersion, the greatest mass resides in large particles. A 10 µ particle is ~10,000 times heavier than a 0.5 µ particle and 1,000,000 times heavier than a 0.1 µ particle. At example specific gravity, it takes 15,000,000 of 0.5 µ particles to deliver 1 µg; only 2,000 of 10 µ particles are needed.
  2. Lower buoyancy of large particles (higher settling rate) makes them more likely to contaminate product by falling out of an airstream as velocity decreases in a production room.

This is good news for filtration: even moderate efficiency filters are quite effective at removing large particles.

Failure Analysis Considerations

Analysis must always consider failure causes including:

  1. Filter and/or frame leakage
  2. Spills and other upsets which cause an excess of airborne API

Commentary: Quantification Method for Airborne Cross-Contamination

Core Calculation: Assessment of Airborne Contamination Mass

The core logic of the quantification method has three steps:

Step 1: Determine the airborne API concentration at the contamination source
  Source: EHS monitoring data, in µg/m³
  → Apply the particle size distribution (mass fraction at each particle size)

Step 2: Calculate the protection factor from filtration efficiency
  Residual mass at each particle size = total mass × (1 - filtration efficiency %)
  → Refer to ANSI/ASHRAE 52.2 or EN 1822 fractional efficiency data
  → Calculate for each of the 12 particle size bins
  → Sum to obtain the total residual mass in the filtered air

Step 3: Verify that the residual concentration is ≤ the acceptable limit
  Acceptance limit = HBEL / ADE (health-based exposure limit)
  → If exceeded: upgrade the filtration or switch to 100% exhaust

Key formula (particle mass):

Particle volume = (4/3) × π × r³
Particle mass = particle volume × density
  (typical density: 0.6–1.0 g/cm³)

Effect of particle size:
  10 µm particle mass ≈ 5.2×10⁻⁴ µg
  0.5 µm particle mass ≈ 6.5×10⁻⁸ µg
  Mass ratio = 10 µm / 0.5 µm ≈ 8,000 times

Explanation of Key Variables

  • Total Mass (total mass at each particle size): from EHS monitoring, in µg/m³
  • Fractional Filter Efficiency: the filtration efficiency at each particle size, from MERV or EN standard testing
  • Fractional Mass After Filtration：= Total Mass × (1 - Efficiency)
  • MERV (Minimum Efficiency Reporting Value): the US ASHRAE filter rating standard
  • EN 1822: the European HEPA/ULPA filter standard (corresponding to H13/H14)

Key Insight: Large Particles Are the Main Cross-Contamination Threat

Intuitively one might assume that small particles (which can drift farther) carry higher risk, but in fact:

  • Large particles (>10 µm) carry far more mass than small particles (volume is proportional to the cube of the radius)
  • Large particles settle quickly and are more likely to fall into open containers or the process
  • Medium-efficiency filters (MERV 11-13) already capture large particles very well

This means: cross-contamination risk can be managed effectively with medium-efficiency filtration, without fitting HEPA everywhere (unless the API is extremely toxic).

Analogy: Sand Dust vs. Pollen

Sand dust particles are large but heavy; they do not drift far before landing, but once they land on food they bring a large mass of contamination. Pollen is fine but light; it drifts far, but each grain carries very little contamination. The logic for API dust is the same: large particles are the main source of mass in cross-contamination.

Link to the Industrial Hygiene Method

This method derives from the assessment approach industrial hygienists use for worker safety, with the following differences:

  • Industrial hygiene: protects workers, focusing on inhalation of small particles (<10 µm)
  • Cross-contamination control: protects the product, focusing on mass transfer of large particles (>10 µm)
  • Both express results in µg/m³, so the data can be shared

Reflection Exercise

Suppose EHS monitoring data show an airborne API concentration of 50 µg/m³ in a powder handling room, the product HBEL = 0.5 µg/day, and the daily inhaled air volume of an employee is assumed to be 10 m³:

  • Daily inhaled API mass = 50 × 10 = 500 µg/day → exceeds the HBEL by 1000 times
  • A filtration efficiency of 99.9% (3 log) or higher is needed to bring it down to an acceptable level
  • → Recommendation: MERV 15 or HEPA, or 100% exhaust
9.6.3

Directional Airflow and Pressure Regimens

Positive vs negative pressure, pressure cascades, airlock configurations | p103-p105

9.6.3 Directional Airflow and Pressure Regimens

In order to control the migration of product aerosols from one room or area to another, it is important to manage the airflow between spaces. Two approaches are possible:

Approach 1 — High Mass Airflow Entrainment: Entrain the aerosol in a large mass of air, typically moving at 0.2 m/s (40 fpm) or greater. Typically used in open downflow booths and similar devices, but not preferred for rooms due to large and rapid changes in airflow when doors are opened or closed (typically 400 m³/h (800 cfm)).

Approach 2 — Controlled Direction with Adequate Velocity (preferred): Control the direction of airflow and assure adequate velocity to transport particles, typically in the range of 0.5 to 1.0 m/s (100 to 200 fpm). Two sub-approaches:

  • Maintain a fixed offset between air entering and leaving via the HVAC system (fixed balance or airflow tracking)
  • Measure and control room pressurization, typically in steps of 10 to 15 Pa (0.4 to 0.6 in. W.G.), but no lower than 5 Pa (0.02 in. W.G.). High-pressure difference across doors results in high velocities at leak points, on the order of 3 to 3.5 m/s (600 to 700 fpm).

Pressure Cascade Design Principles

The pressure cascade and direction of airflow should be designed to be protective of product, personnel, and environment:

  • To prevent environmental contaminants from entering: production area positive with respect to surrounding environment
  • However, a positive manufacturing space can spread product aerosols to other products and personnel; therefore, open product handling areas should be negative with respect to other GxP areas (e.g., the GxP corridor)

Airlock Configurations

Single door configurations between production room and corridor are challenged when the door is open. For greater protection, airlocks with interlocked doors are used. Three configurations (Figure 9.7🔍):

  • Simple Pressure Cascade: Corridor (positive) → Production (negative). Effective for API containment.
  • Sink-type Airlock: Process room positive to airlock AND corridor positive to airlock. Airlock is the lowest pressure (sink).
  • Bubble-type Airlock: Airlock is positive with respect to both corridor and process room. Testing has shown greater emission potential with this configuration — generally not preferred for hazardous compounds.

Commentary: Directional Airflow and Pressure Gradient Design

Basic Principles of Pressure Gradients

Air flows from high pressure to low pressure. The goal of pressure gradient design is to make "clean air" always flow toward "areas that may be contaminated", with the contamination source area kept at negative pressure to prevent escape.

  • Positive Pressure: prevents external contamination from entering (protects the product)
  • Negative Pressure: prevents API from escaping (protects personnel and other products)

For the manufacture of highly toxic API, negative pressure is usually the main approach to prevent hazardous substances from spreading outward.

Pressure Differential Design Values

Recommended pressure differential (adjacent rooms): 10–15 Pa
Minimum acceptable pressure differential: 5 Pa (below this it is difficult to maintain directional airflow)

Airflow velocity through door gaps (under the pressure differential):
  3.0–3.5 m/s (600–700 fpm)
  → sufficient to carry and stop particles from moving in the reverse direction

Air velocity (sweeping a large area):
  ≥ 0.2 m/s (40 fpm): for downflow booths
  0.5–1.0 m/s (100–200 fpm): for directional control

The Dilemma: Positive vs. Negative Pressure

A design conflict manufacturers often face:

  • Product protection → positive pressure in the manufacturing room (to keep out external microbial contamination)
  • Personnel / environmental protection → negative pressure in the manufacturing room (to prevent API dust leakage)

Solution: insert an airlock between the manufacturing room and the corridor to form a pressure buffer zone; the corridor stays at positive pressure and the manufacturing room at negative pressure, with the two not directly connected.

Analogy: The Hospital Isolation Room

An infectious disease isolation room uses negative pressure (to prevent the virus from leaking out); a cleanroom uses positive pressure (to prevent external particles from entering). An API manufacturing room has both needs, and the airlock is the "buffer zone" that lets the two needs coexist.

Cross-Reference

For airlock design details, see: ISPE Good Practice Guide: HVAC and ISPE Baseline® Vol. 3 (Sterile Manufacturing Facilities). For specific pressure differential monitoring and validation methods, refer to the ISPE C&Q guide.

9.7

Containment Related Technologies

Selection of containment systems matched to compound hazard level | p105-p106

9.7 Containment Related Technologies

Containment related technologies are typically used to make a process step, or a process a closed system, keeping the product and the room environment separate. Employing containment related technologies can reduce the risk of cross-contamination by airborne or mechanical transfer routes.

9.7.1 Selection of Appropriate Containment Systems

The equipment selection process should result in individual equipment items or systems identified which give high confidence that the design target/performance expectation will be met. Success depends on an early understanding of the full range of activities, including normal operation, sampling, and cleaning, shared with the full team including supplier(s) and integrators.

Planned and breakdown maintenance activities should be discussed at an early stage. Requirements for activities such as sampling should be questioned, as they may present significant difficulties in effective containment design and may present an exposure risk that outweighs other benefits.

Information Required for Successful Equipment Selection

  • Detail of all activities to be undertaken including normal operation, sampling, and cleaning
  • Appreciation of the rate of movement of particles or the energy with which they may be released
  • Variety of containers in and out of the equipment
  • Way waste and contaminated equipment will be removed
  • Need for aids for heavy, awkward, or restricted space lifting
  • Operators' understanding of risks and how behavior affects containment performance — behavior has the greatest impact on local extract and enclosures and least impact on closed systems and isolators
  • Ergonomics of activities, especially repetitive actions, access requirements, working position, load characteristics, and operator stature range
  • Knowledge of containment or emission performance of equipment in similar applications
  • How containment performance may change with time due to ineffective cleaning and/or wear
  • Understanding of the problem by the design team
  • Potential for powder to ignite or support a dust cloud explosion — contained systems have smaller volumes and low air change rates creating risk of explosive concentrations; nitrogen inerting may be required
  • To dissipate electrostatic buildup, all metal parts should be grounded (earthed), including elements docked and insulated by plastic wear elements

Commentary: Hierarchy of Containment System Selection

Hierarchy of Containment System Selection (graded by OEL/HBEL)

The choice of containment technology follows the principle of the hierarchy of controls: consider engineering controls (containment) first, rather than relying on administrative controls (SOPs) or PPE. The containment levels from low to high are as follows:

Open Processing
OEL > 100 µg/m³ | suitable for low-toxicity compounds
↓ Increasing toxicity
Local Exhaust Ventilation (LEV / Downflow Booth)
OEL 10–100 µg/m³ | near-field capture, semi-open
↓
Closed Vessels / Systems
OEL 1–10 µg/m³ | reduced escape, manual intervention points controlled
↓
Glovebox / Isolator
OEL < 1 µg/m³ | complete physical separation, highest level

Effect of Personnel Behavior on Different Containment Technologies

Key insight: the lower the degree of containment, the greater the influence of operator behavior on the actual containment achieved.

  • Open operation / LEV: operating technique, standing position, and movement speed have the greatest effect on containment performance
  • Semi-closed (glovebox attached externally): moderate dependence on personnel behavior
  • Isolator / fully closed: personnel behavior has the least effect; the equipment itself provides the assurance

→ Highly toxic compounds should not rely on personnel behavior as the main means of control

Analogy: Face Mask vs. Protective Cabin

Open operation + PPE is like wearing a face mask against a virus: the result depends on how it is worn. An isolator is like handling the virus inside a completely sealed protective cabin: the result barely depends on personnel skill. The more toxic the API, the more a solution toward the "protective cabin" end is needed.

Explosion Risk: A Commonly Overlooked Design Consideration

A side effect of containment systems: small volume and low air change rate may create explosive dust concentrations (Dust Explosion Limit). The design must:

  • Evaluate the explosion risk of the API dust (MEC: Minimum Explosive Concentration)
  • If there is a risk: use nitrogen inerting (Nitrogen Inerting) to lower the oxygen concentration
  • Ground all metal parts to prevent static sparks from igniting dust

Sampling Design Challenge

One of the most difficult aspects of containment system design is sampling. The document stresses actively questioning whether sampling is necessary: sometimes the exposure risk of sampling far outweighs the value of the quality information obtained, and this should be discussed at the start of plant design rather than remedied afterwards.

9.8

Personal Protective Equipment and Gowning

PPE vs RPE distinction, roles, and cross-contamination risk from mechanical transfer | p106-p108

9.8 Personal Protective Equipment and Gowning

Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) refers to gowns, gloves, booties, hair covers, hoods, respiratory protection, face masks, etc.

Respiratory Protective Equipment (RPE) covers devices designed only to protect the mouth and nose.

The distinction is important because PPE, without RPE, is recommended for protecting the product from contamination and cross-contamination as well as for dermal protection for the operators at all times.

Whenever RPE is used, it is important to consider its use, storage, maintenance, and decontamination, and whether or not it could lead to a risk of cross-contamination from mechanical transfer.

PPE is Normally Worn to:

  • Help limit mechanical transfer of materials
  • Provide appropriate outer clothing to protect the product, e.g., made of non-shedding material
  • Provide a psychological and physical indication of transfer from the outside environment into a GxP facility where specific procedures, rules of conduct, and action apply
  • Provide dermal and chemical protection to the operator so that any drug exposure related effects are minimized

Risk Acceptance Requirement (Section 10 cross-reference)

Where risk reduction strategies (including PPE, containment, HVAC) are implemented, the manufacturer must demonstrate to all stakeholders that strategies are effective in maintaining cross-contamination and worker exposure at or below acceptable limits. This requires demonstration that:

  • Procedures and detection devices are in place to minimize mix-up potential even in accident or error scenarios
  • Any residues on product contact and non-product contact surfaces fall within health-based limits, in support of cleaning processes
  • Flow of personnel, product, material, and HVAC are designed to minimize potential for mechanical transfer
  • Any product that may escape the process is maintained below levels that could impact cross-contamination and worker safety

Commentary: The Role and Limitations of PPE / RPE

The Hidden Risk of RPE: Mechanical Transfer

RPE itself can become a cross-contamination carrier!

If RPE used in a high API concentration environment is taken to other areas without being properly stored/decontaminated, the API residue on the RPE surface causes mechanical transfer. Therefore:

  • The use, storage, maintenance, and decontamination procedures for RPE must be documented
  • RPE used in highly toxic environments should be treated as hazardous waste and must not be taken out
  • It is recommended to remove RPE in the change room / airlock area rather than taking it into the corridor

Four Functions of PPE (in order of importance)

  1. Limiting mechanical transfer: preventing personnel from carrying API dust to other areas (protects other products)
  2. Protecting the product: non-shedding protective garments prevent personnel-generated particles from contaminating the product (two-way protection)
  3. Psychological / behavioral marker: putting on specific protective garments signals entry into a GxP environment, reinforcing personnel compliance awareness
  4. Chemical protection of the skin: preventing dermal exposure to the API, especially important for potentially toxic compounds

Analogy: The Dual Meaning of a Chef's Hat

A restaurant chef's hat (to keep hair from falling out):
- protects the food from contamination by hair (protects the product)
- prevents the chef from inhaling food steam (protects personnel)
PPE in API manufacturing likewise provides two-way protection, but in a toxic drug environment the need for personnel protection is more urgent, and the selection of RPE must be based on quantitative risk assessment.

PPE Is the Last Line of Defense, Not the First

According to the risk reduction hierarchy logic of ISPE Vol.7 (the overall framework of Chapter 9):

  • First priority: engineering controls (containment equipment, isolators, LEV)
  • Second: administrative controls (SOPs, personnel restrictions, gowning procedures)
  • Last: PPE / RPE (personal protection)

Over-reliance on PPE without solving the problem at the engineering level is a common finding in GMP inspections.

Integration with Other Chapters: Risk Acceptance

All the measures in Chapter 9 (Risk Reduction) — HVAC design, containment systems, PPE — ultimately lead to Chapter 10 (Risk Acceptance): the manufacturer must demonstrate to all stakeholders that these measures effectively:

  • Keep cross-contamination below the health-based limit (HBEL/ADE)
  • Keep worker exposure below the OEL

This requires monitoring data, validation records, and a deviation investigation mechanism working together as a whole.

Summary

Chapter 9 (Part B) Consolidated Summary

Integrated application logic of HVAC, containment technology, and PPE

Key Takeaways — English Summary

  • Once-Through vs Recirculation: Recirculation is permitted with adequate treatment; for potent APIs (low OEL), double HEPA or 100% exhaust is required. Dust collectors handling potent products are not candidates for recirculation.
  • Quantitative CC Risk: Large particles (>10 µm) carry the greatest mass and pose the greatest cross-contamination risk. MERV 11 provides ~1.5 log reduction; MERV 15 provides ~3 log. Filtration efficiency data by particle size range is available from ASHRAE 52.2 and EN 1822.
  • Pressure Regimens: Typical steps of 10–15 Pa; minimum 5 Pa. Sink-type airlocks are preferred for potent API areas. Bubble-type airlocks show higher emission potential and are generally not recommended for hazardous compounds.
  • Containment Hierarchy: Open → LEV/Downflow Booth → Closed Vessels → Gloveboxes → Isolators. Higher containment levels reduce dependence on operator behavior.
  • PPE is the last line of defense: Engineering controls (closed systems, HVAC) take priority. RPE must be managed to prevent becoming a cross-contamination vector via mechanical transfer.

Practical Application Matrix

Links Between Sections

The technical choices in this section (the second half of Chapter 9) must all map back to:

  • Chapter 5: setting of OEL / HBEL / ADE (the basis for selection)
  • Chapter 8: facility and equipment design (including GMP area planning)
  • Chapter 10: documentation and verification requirements for risk acceptance
  • Chapter 11: quantitative analysis of failure scenarios with tools such as FMEA

  • Understand the conditions for accepting residual risk and how they link to the HBEL (ADE/OEL)
  • Understand how a risk matrix is constructed and how to read its color-coded heat map

  • Understand the triggers and documentation requirements for periodic risk review
  • Understand the stakeholder structure for risk communication and the required content of the summary document template
10

Risk Acceptance

p109–p110 | ICH Q9 residual risk decision framework, link to HBEL

Original Text (English)

10 Risk Acceptance

ICH Q9 states that risk acceptance is a decision to accept a defined level of risk. This can be a formal documented decision process with residual risks specified or a more informal process where the residual risk is not specified. The documentation and the acceptable residual risk should be commensurate with the risk.

Acceptance of residual risk based on the health-based limits described in this Guide should provide adequate protection of both patient and the worker.

Where risk reduction strategies are implemented in order to get to a risk acceptance decision, the manufacturer should be able to demonstrate to all stakeholders that the risk reduction strategies are effective in maintaining cross-contamination and worker exposure at or below acceptable limits.

This requires demonstration that:

  • Procedures and detection devices are in place to minimize the potential for mix-up even in the case of accident or error
  • Any residues on product contact and nonproduct contact surfaces fall within the health-based limits in support of cleaning processes
  • Flow of personnel, product, material, and HVAC are designed to minimize the potential for mechanical transfer
  • Any product that may escape the process is maintained below levels that could impact the potential for cross-contamination and worker safety
Analysis and Commentary

Core Concept: What Is Risk Acceptance?

Risk acceptance does not mean "ignoring the risk". It is a conscious decision, based on sufficient evidence, confirming that the residual risk is within an acceptable range.

ICH Q9 allows both formal and informal approaches, but the level of documentation must be commensurate with the risk.

The HBEL Link: ADE and OEL Are the Basis of the Acceptance Threshold

The guideline stresses that the basis for accepting residual risk should be the health-based exposure limit (HBEL), specifically:

  • ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure): patient protection (the product cross-contamination perspective)
  • OEL (Occupational Exposure Limit): operator protection (the occupational exposure perspective)

Risk may be formally accepted only when the actual residue level is ≤ the cleaning limit derived from the ADE/OEL.

Analogy: Risk acceptance is like "closing out" a file

It is like submitting a CAPA for review: you must provide evidence that the corrective actions are effective before your supervisor will approve closure. Risk acceptance works the same way. You must produce data showing that all four cross-contamination pathways (mix-up, retention, mechanical transfer and airborne transfer) are controlled below the ADE/OEL before you can formally sign off on acceptance.

Four Required Evidence Conditions

  • Procedures and detection devices to prevent mix-up are in place
  • Residue levels on product-contact and non-contact surfaces meet the health-based cleaning limits
  • Personnel, material and HVAC flows are designed to minimize mechanical transfer
  • The amount of product escaping from the process is below the safety-relevant threshold

Audit Focus

Auditors often ask: "Is your residual risk acceptance decision documented? Who approved it? Which ADE value is it based on?" Make sure this chain of logic is clear: toxicology report → ADE → cleaning limit → validation results → risk acceptance document.

11

Risk Management Tools: Overview

p111–p112 | ICH Q9 tool selection, risk ranking, organizational standards

Original Text (English)

11 Risk Management Tools

Risk can be assessed and managed using risk management tools. The level of effort or the tools used should be based on the level of potential risk and knowledge of the process. It is important that each manufacturer set standards for risk assessment tools including, but not limited to, the types of tools to be used, when to use certain tools, defining the categories, setting acceptable risk levels, action levels, etc.

It is essential that the numbers or designation are carefully chosen and defined and are not modified because the outcome is unwelcome.

ICH Q9 refers to a number of risk assessment tools that may be used. Organizations should determine which tools they wish to use or they can create custom tools:

  • Basic risk management facilitation methods (flowcharts, check sheets, etc.)
  • Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA)
  • Failure Mode, Effects, and Criticality Analysis (FMECA)
  • Fault Tree Analysis (FTA)
  • Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points (HACCP)
  • Hazard Operability Analysis (HAZOP)
  • Preliminary Hazard Analysis (PHA)
  • Risk ranking and filtering
  • Supporting statistical tools

The tools shown in this Chapter are examples that should ideally be developed and used by a team of stakeholders working together.

Analysis and Commentary

Principle of Tool Selection: Complexity Should Be Commensurate with the Level of Risk

The guideline does not mandate which tool to use, but it stresses that:

  • The level of effort invested in the tool should be proportionate to the level of potential risk
  • Each manufacturer should establish its own standardized tool system (including when to use each tool, scoring categories and acceptance criteria)
  • Once the standards are set, the scoring definitions must not be changed because the results are not what was hoped for

Key Principle: Scoring definitions must not be revised after the fact because the results look bad

This is a common compliance trap: after an FMEA produces a high RPN, someone adjusts the scoring definitions to make the numbers "look better". ICH Q9 and this guideline explicitly oppose this practice. The scoring criteria must be defined before the assessment and applied consistently throughout the project.

ICH Q9 Tool List (tools covered in this section are in bold)

  • Flowcharts and check sheets (basic methods)
  • FMEA: Failure Mode and Effects Analysis
  • FMECA: FMEA with criticality analysis
  • FTA: Fault Tree Analysis
  • HACCP: Hazard Analysis and Critical Control Points
  • HAZOP: Hazard and Operability Analysis
  • PHA: Preliminary Hazard Analysis
  • Risk ranking and filtering (Risk Matrix)
  • Statistical tools

Analogy: The toolbox mindset

A plumber does not use just one kind of wrench, and risk management likewise requires choosing the right tool for the nature of the problem. FMEA suits detailed analysis at the process-step level. A risk matrix suits quickly ranking the processes across a whole facility. Statistical analysis suits situations where quantitative monitoring data are available.

11.1

Risk Ranking / Risk Matrix

p112 | Probability vs Severity grid, color-coded heat map, unit operation classification

Original Text (English)

11.1 Risk Ranking or Risk Matrix

Qualitative risk assessments may be performed at manufacturing sites for the purpose of determining which unit operations pose the greatest risk or the greatest opportunity. A risk ranking or risk matrix provides a very simple high level objective process to consistently evaluate all the unit operations at a manufacturing site and rank the operations by risk.

To apply the risk ranking, all the unit operations at a site are identified. To complete the ranking, the following parameters should be considered:

  • Health Effects: ADE value, residue levels for cleaning, inactivation, etc.
  • Product Considerations: percent active in batch, batch size, product form (impact on API becoming airborne), maximum daily dosage
  • Operational Considerations: number of employees involved, frequency of the process, energy input, open or closed processing, surface area of product contact parts, batches per year, relative positions of source and at-risk materials, duration of process

For each parameter a numerical value, or high, medium, and low indicator is assigned, and for each unit operation, the scores are totaled. After all unit operations have been assessed, the risk ranking scores can be compared and activities prioritized.

Unit Operation Risk Ranking — Airborne Concentration Examples

  • High (H): sampling, weighing and dispensing, sample testing (analytical), charging, milling, granulation, extruding (cleaning), compounding
  • Medium (M): mixing, blending, extruding, compression, capsule filling
  • Low (L): coating, charging, drilling, printing, packaging
  • Negligible (N): membrane coating, discharging cores, drying, pouching, gel dispensing, curing, assembling, inspection
Analysis and Commentary

Core Logic of the Risk Matrix

A risk matrix plots probability/likelihood against severity on a two-dimensional grid, and each cell is color-coded to show the risk level:

Action Required for Each Color Code

  • Low - acceptable; monitoring is sufficient
  • Medium - evaluate whether improvement measures are needed
  • High - risk control measures are required
  • Very high - immediate action or a halt is required

Example Application in a Sterile Filling Facility

Take ranking the cross-contamination risk in a sterile filling facility as an example:

  • High risk: weighing and dispensing, API filling (high airborne concentration)
  • Medium risk: mixing/compounding operations
  • Low risk: packaging and labeling (closed systems)
  • Negligible: drying, visual inspection

The three scoring dimensions (health impact, product factors and operational factors) are summed to determine the overall ranking, and the highest-scoring process steps are addressed first.

11.2

Cause and Effect Diagram (Fishbone) / Ishikawa Diagram

p113 | Ishikawa diagram for cross-contamination root cause analysis, 6M framework

Original Text (English)

11.2 Cause and Effect Diagram (Fishbone Diagram)

Use the Ishikawa Fishbone diagram to brainstorm how the four modes of cross-contamination can occur. In addition, a second diagram can be used to identify how each of the four modes of cross-contamination can be or are controlled.

This can be a helpful precursor to more detailed analysis or data gathering exercises.

"The Fishbone diagram is a structured brainstorming tool that maps potential causes to an observed effect, enabling a team to systematically explore root causes before committing to remediation actions."

The four modes of cross-contamination addressed by the fishbone analysis are:

  • Mix-up — incorrect product or label identification
  • Retention — residues remaining from prior campaigns
  • Mechanical transfer — physical movement via personnel, equipment, materials
  • Airborne transfer — product aerosols migrating via air currents

Two diagrams are recommended:

  1. First diagram: How can each mode occur? (cause identification)
  2. Second diagram: How is each mode currently controlled? (control mapping)
Analysis and Commentary

The 6M Framework of the Fishbone Diagram

A standard fishbone diagram uses the "6M" classification framework to systematically identify root causes across six dimensions:

Man (people) Inadequate training, operator error, staff turnover, improper gowning procedures
Machine (equipment) Incomplete cleaning, wear generating particles, hard-to-clean dead legs in the design
Method Incomplete SOPs, failed cleaning validation, poorly arranged process sequence
Material Mislabeled containers, shared material racks, packaging material residues
Measurement Inadequate residue monitoring, unrepresentative sampling points, insufficient analytical method sensitivity
Mother Nature (environment) Insufficient HVAC pressure differential, air circulation carrying over cross-contamination, temperature and humidity affecting dust generation
6M root causes ► Cross-contamination occurs

Dual-Diagram Strategy

First diagram (finding the problem): for each cross-contamination mode, use the 6M to list every possible cause. This is the brainstorming stage, with no answer assumed in advance.

Second diagram (finding the controls): for the same mode, list the existing controls (engineering, administrative and procedural controls). Comparing the two diagrams, the gaps are the areas that need improvement.

Analogy: The fishbone is "reverse engineering"

You already know the problem (the fish head = the effect), and now you trace back along the bones to find the causes. It is like a traffic accident investigator working backward from a "rear-end collision" to "wet road + insufficient following distance + driver fatigue". Think of each bone as one possible branch of causes.

Practical Application in a Sterile Filling Facility

Example of a fishbone analysis for the "airborne" mode:

  • People: incomplete gowning brings in external contaminants
  • Equipment: HEPA filter integrity failure in the RABS/isolator
  • Method: vials opened in a non-grade A environment
  • Environment: pressure differential between the grade B and grade C rooms is reversed (out of control)
11.3

Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA)

p114–p117 | RPN = S × O × D, scoring tables, FMEA form structure

Original Text (English)

11.3 Failure Mode and Effects Analysis

Many pharmaceutical companies use FMEA techniques for risk ranking and have standard protocols for its use. It is used in Six Sigma evaluations and can be used for a wide range of risk assessments.

FMEA was originally developed by the US Military in the 1940s and further developed by the automotive and aerospace industries. Where companies do not have a formally documented system, it is recommended that they use a recognized system such as in IEC 60812.

FMEA Form Column Headings (Table 11.1)

11.3.1 Scoring Severity, Occurrence, and Detection (Table 11.2)

The possible scores in this example range from 1 (1×1×1) to 1,000 (10×10×10). Each organization should determine the scoring systems to be used in FMEAs.

Scoring or RPNs should be viewed as a general indicator/aid for risk ranking and not treated as an absolute indicator of risk. Caution should be used when prioritizing risks on just the RPN number.

One method to address possible inequalities of RPNs is to require review of any item (severity, occurrence, or detection) that has the highest value regardless of the resultant RPN.

Detectability is a vital issue: if reliable means exist to detect a failure, increased occurrence may be tolerable; if the event cannot be detected, then the occurrence and severity have to be low.

Analysis and Commentary

Origins of FMEA and Its Use in Pharmaceutical Plants

FMEA was first developed by the US military in the 1940s and later standardized widely by the automotive (AIAG) and aerospace industries. The pharmaceutical version adds pharma-specific factors: ADE values, occupational exposure limits and the four cross-contamination modes.

ICH Q9 lists FMEA as a recommended tool. The reference standard is IEC 60812, and companies may customize it on that basis.

RPN Formula

RPN = Severity (S) × Occurrence (O) × Detection (D)

Range: 1 (lowest risk) → 1,000 (highest risk)

S = 10: patient/operator injury, ADE < 1 µg/day
O = 10: occurs more than once per batch
D = 10: the current method cannot detect it at all

Note:
The Detection score is "inverted": the poorer the detection capability, the higher the score
10 = cannot be detected at all
1 = obvious, or an automatic monitoring alarm is in place

Limitations of the RPN: Do Not Rely on the Total Score Alone

The following two combinations have the same RPN but very different risk profiles:

  • Case A: S=10, O=10, D=1 → RPN=100 (severe and frequent, but detectable)
  • Case B: S=10, O=1, D=10 → RPN=100 (severe but rare, and completely undetectable)

Case B is actually more dangerous. The guideline therefore recommends: any single score that reaches the maximum value (10) should trigger a mandatory review, regardless of the RPN.

The Special Importance of Detectability

In pharmaceutical FMEAs, detectability is often the most underestimated dimension:

  • If an event can be detected in time, a higher frequency of occurrence can still be managed (because the process can be interrupted and corrected)
  • If an event cannot be detected, then even with a low probability and a high severity, patient harm may already have occurred without anyone noticing
  • Common detectability weaknesses in sterile filling facilities: media fills can only verify after the fact, and cleaning residue monitoring coverage is insufficient

Analogy: The RPN is like a credit score

Two people may have the same credit score, yet one has "never borrowed" while the other has "borrowed a lot but always repaid on time", so the underlying risk profiles are very different. The RPN is the same: an identical number can represent a completely different risk structure, and you need to look at the individual scores to truly understand the risk.

Exercise: Calculating the RPN

Scenario: isolator glove breach (glove integrity failure) on a sterile filling line

  • Severity (S): a glove breach could allow the operator to contaminate the sterile process → the patient could receive a microbially contaminated product → S = 10
  • Occurrence (O): gloves undergo routine integrity testing monthly, and historical records show a breach about 1–2 times per year → O = 3
  • Detection (D): an online glove integrity monitoring system gives an immediate alarm → D = 1
  • RPN = 10 × 3 × 1 = 30

Note: even though the RPN = 30 (relatively low), S = 10, so under the guideline's recommendation this item still requires a mandatory review.

11.4

Statistical Analysis

p117 | Process Capability (Cpk/Ppk), application to risk assessment

Original Text (English)

11.4 Statistical Analysis

Statistical analysis is used to analyze quantitative data to identify trends, variations and likelihood processes will provide the desired results.

Typical outputs of statistical analysis are Process Capability (Cpk) and Process Performance Indices (Ppk).

Statistical analysis capabilities are available in Excel or software packages such as Minitab. It is very important that the statistical analysis of data including interpretation of output from the software packages noted is completed by a properly qualified statistician or similar.

Advanced Topic: Statistical analysis is designated as an advanced topic in this Guide, indicating its application requires additional expertise beyond standard risk management practitioners.
Analysis and Commentary

The Role of Statistical Analysis in Risk Assessment

Statistical analysis gives FMEA and risk matrices quantitative support, shifting the statement from "we think the probability is low" to "historical data show Cpk = 1.8, so process capability is adequate".

Process Capability Index Concepts

Cpk (short-term process capability):
  Measures how well the process is centered relative to the specification limits under controlled conditions
  Cpk ≥ 1.33 → generally considered acceptable (4σ)
  Cpk ≥ 1.67 → higher standard (5σ)

Ppk (long-term process performance):
  Measures how the process performs in actual long-term operation
  Ppk is usually < Cpk (because it includes special-cause variation)

Application in risk assessment:
  Low Cpk → unstable process → the FMEA O (occurrence) score should be higher
  High Cpk + in control → can support a decision to lower the O score

Why Interpretation by a Qualified Statistician Matters

The guideline specifically stresses that statistical outputs (such as Cpk/Ppk) must be interpreted by personnel with statistical competence. Common misuses include:

  • Calculating Cpk directly from non-normally distributed data
  • Performing a capability analysis with too few data points (n < 30)
  • Misunderstanding the statistical meaning of Minitab output charts

Applying Statistical Analysis to Cleaning Validation

In cross-contamination risk management, statistical analysis is most often used for:

  • Cleaning validation trend analysis: tracking residue trends across multiple post-cleaning batches to confirm they do not exceed the ADE-based limit
  • Environmental monitoring data: analyzing the statistical distribution of particle/microbial monitoring results to set action and alert limits
  • Process parameter monitoring: using Cpk to assess the process capability of critical parameters such as fill volume accuracy
12

Risk Review

p119 | Periodic reassessment, trigger events, ADE review, change control link

Original Text (English)

12 Risk Review

ICH Q9 states risk management should be an ongoing part of the quality management process. A mechanism to review internal processes and external events should be implemented.

Periodic reviews should be performed on all aspects of the risk management system to ensure that it is being applied effectively and that any potential improvements are identified and incorporated into the process.

This should include a periodic review of the ADE, especially if established early in the drug development timeline, as new information is being gathered from preclinical and clinical studies on a continuous basis.

Any manufacturing deviations or system changes that could impact the potential for exposure (product or personnel) or might compromise the conclusions drawn from previous risk assessments should be closely reviewed. The system may need to be modified so that it does not present an increased potential for exposure.

Any review or, where necessary, reassessment should be documented accordingly.

An organization's quality management processes also should consider need for any reassessment to ensure that original assumptions and performance data remain valid. This may include or require:

  • Auditing of processes and activities
  • Qualitative or quantitative monitoring of performance

Any change to the risk profile should be communicated as described in risk communication.

Analysis and Commentary

The Nature of Risk Review: Ongoing, Not One-Off

ICH Q9 stresses that risk management is an ongoing lifecycle activity, not something that is finished once an FMEA has been done.

A risk review system requires two mechanisms to run in parallel:

  • Periodic review: scheduled, proactive and rhythmic
  • Triggered review: event-driven and reactive

Events That Must Trigger a Reassessment

  • ADE update: new toxicology data are published and the original ADE needs to be revised
  • Process deviation: cleaning validation failure, environmental monitoring excursion, positive sterility test
  • Equipment/facility change: introduction of new equipment, HVAC modification, room re-layout
  • Product mix change: introduction of a new compound (especially a highly potent API), discontinuation of a product
  • Regulatory update: new EMA/FDA guidance, a new edition of ICH Q9
  • Audit/inspection findings: internal or external audits find that the assumptions of the existing risk assessment do not hold

The Special Importance of Periodic ADE Review

The guideline specifically stresses periodic re-examination of the ADE, for these reasons:

  • An ADE established in early development is often based on limited toxicology data
  • As clinical studies progress, new safety data may lower the ADE (stricter) or raise it (more lenient)
  • A change in the ADE directly affects the cleaning limit, and in turn the conclusions of the whole risk assessment

Analogy: Risk review is like regular vehicle servicing

A car needs regular servicing (periodic review), but it also needs to go to the workshop immediately after an accident (triggered review). The reasoning "my last service was only three months ago, so I don't need to worry about this rear-end collision" is just as wrong in risk management. When an event occurs, you must reassess, no matter when the last periodic review took place.

Documentation Requirements

All risk reviews (whether periodic or triggered) must be documented and include:

  • The reason for the review (the trigger, or the scheduled date)
  • The scope and method of the review
  • Conclusion: the original assessment remains valid / needs revision
  • If revision is needed: the changes made and the approval records
  • Communication records (where applicable)
13

Risk Communication

p121 | Stakeholder layers, escalation, ADE/OEL change notifications, CAPA linkage

Original Text (English)

13 Risk Communication

ICH Q9 defines risk communication as the sharing of information about risk and risk management between the decision makers and others. Organizations should document, within their quality management systems, who needs to communicate what, to whom, and at what frequency, in support of a risk management process.

Different levels of information will be appropriate to different stakeholders who may be internal or external to the organization. It is important to have an escalation process that allows management to be informed of areas that have risk that is unacceptable or near unacceptable.

The holistic approach described in this Guide is predicated on the fact that all of those involved have received appropriate education or training and have ready access to supporting risk assessment and management documentation.

Risk Communication can be enhanced by incorporating linkages within the change control, product quality review and CAPA quality systems.

It is important that companies have a means to widely and effectively communicate to all parties that might be affected including company manufacturing sites, contract manufacturers and QA departments any identified amendments to ADEs (and OELs) or other items that may affect the risk assessment.

In addition if a company chooses to have product-specific ADEs (where the following product is known) or ADEs for specific routes of exposure or dosage types (such as OSD or parenteral) rather than address these issues in the risk assessment, the company should have a robust process to manage these values and make clear to all that may depend on these values what the criteria used to determine the ADEs are and how they should be modified for other usage.

Analysis and Commentary

Three Dimensions of Risk Communication

The ICH Q9 definition of risk communication emphasizes "two-way information sharing" rather than one-way notification. Effective risk communication needs to answer three questions:

  • Who: the communicating party (the decision-makers) and the recipients (the stakeholders)
  • What: different levels of information depending on each stakeholder's role
  • When: frequency (periodic) + trigger points (when the risk changes)

The Obligation to Communicate ADE/OEL Revisions

The guideline explicitly requires that when an ADE or OEL value is updated, the update must be communicated promptly and widely to all affected parties, including:

  • Manufacturing sites within the company
  • Contract manufacturers (CMO/CDMO)
  • The QA department

This is especially important for a CDMO: when a client updates the ADE of a compound, the CDMO must update its own cleaning limits and risk assessments in step.

Analogy: Risk communication is like a "broadcast system" plus an "express elevator"

Good risk communication needs two systems side by side: a broadcast system (so that everyone receives routine updates) and an emergency escalation elevator (so that when a risk exceeds its limit, the message reaches the decision-makers immediately). With a broadcast but no elevator, management never learns there is a problem. With an elevator but no broadcast, front-line staff lack day-to-day risk awareness.

Integration with the QMS: Change Control + PQR + CAPA

The guideline recommends embedding risk communication in three existing QMS systems:

  • Change control: any process/equipment/product change triggers a risk reassessment, and the conclusions are passed on through change control
  • Product quality review (PQR): the annual PQR includes a review of risk trends and serves as the vehicle for periodic review
  • CAPA: risk control action items are tracked and closed through the CAPA system, ensuring traceability
13.1

Summary Document Template

p122–p123 | QRMP document structure, required sections and content guidance

Original Text (English)

13.1 Summary Document Template

Introduction
[insert brief introduction to the purpose of the document/report]

Scope
[insert scope of the document/quality risk management process]

Responsibilities
[insert a listing of the disciplines/personnel participating in the quality risk management process]

Manufacturing Facility and Processes

  • Facility Description: [brief summary of manufacturing, including segregation philosophy, design for prevention of cross-contamination, layout, independent HVAC systems, airflows, HEPA filtration on exhaust air, cleanable surfaces, material flows, etc.]
  • Process and Manufacturing Equipment Description: [brief summary of the process from start to finish, including all points where API or formulated product is handled, transferred, isolated, and which pieces of equipment are used; emphasis on shared equipment (e.g., lyophilizers) where there is a potential for cross-contamination]
  • Standard Operating Procedures, Personnel, Validation Plans: [listing of all applicable SOPs, validation of cleaning procedures, studies, and personnel issues (training, hygiene, access restrictions)]

Risk Assessment

  • Risk Identification
  • Hazard Identification and Dose-Response Assessment: [statement on how hazard identification and dose-response assessment was performed, where monographs are archived, who is responsible, and summary of rationale for each ADE value established]
  • Risk Analysis: [description of risk analysis process; tools used; data collected such as monitoring data from internal/external equipment surfaces and manufacturing areas; comparison to ADEs]
  • Exposure Assessment: [summaries, graphs, or other information on outcome of analyses and exposure assessments; available monitoring results for the facility and specific equipment]
  • Risk Evaluation: [company scoring for risk with actions — unacceptable/acceptable/reduce risk; list of issues requiring risk reduction if FMEA was used]

Risk Control
[summary of control strategies; justification for accepting risk; balancing medical benefit to the patient and the level of risk accepted; use the CAPA system to manage risk reduction items]

Risk Communication
[statement about how this QRMP and its application to specific processes and products was documented and communicated to all appropriate stakeholders; how all those involved have received appropriate training]

Risk Review
[statement on how periodic reviews are performed on all aspects of the quality risk management system; how any deviations or system changes that could lead to exposures above accepted internal benchmarks are addressed; how the system is modified to prevent future occurrences]

Summary
[overall summary concluding that the QRMP has addressed all hazards and exposure pathways and controls have been implemented to maintain risks below acceptable levels]

Analysis and Commentary

The QRMP Summary Document: The "Closure Report" of the Quality Risk Management Plan

The QRMP (Quality Risk Management Plan) is the written record of the entire risk management process and contains the complete chain of logic from hazard identification to the risk acceptance decision. It is one of the core documents inspectors most often ask to see.

Inspection Focus: The "Shared Equipment" Clause in the Facility Description

The template specifically requires that the process description highlight all shared equipment (such as lyophilizers). This is because shared equipment is the highest cross-contamination risk point, and inspectors will focus on:

  • Have all shared equipment items been identified?
  • Does each shared equipment item have a validated cleaning procedure?
  • Are the cleaning limits calculated from the ADE?

Analogy: The QRMP is the "medical record of risk management"

Just as a medical record fully documents a patient's diagnosis, treatment and follow-up, the QRMP fully documents the process's hazard identification (diagnosis), risk control (treatment) and periodic review (follow-up). A good QRMP should let any new QA staff member or inspector quickly understand "where the risks of this site lie, how they are controlled, and who is responsible".

The ADE Link in the Hazard Identification Section

The template requires the Hazard Identification section to state:

  • How hazard identification and dose-response assessment are performed
  • Where the toxicology reports (monographs) are stored
  • Who is responsible for maintaining and updating this information
  • A summary of the basis for the ADE value of each compound

This ensures that ADE values have complete toxicological supporting documentation, rather than being numbers decided off the cuff.

CDMO Application: Practical Advice for Writing a QRMP

For a sterile filling CDMO, pay particular attention to the following when writing the QRMP:

  • Define the scope boundaries clearly: each time a client introduces a new product, assess whether a new QRMP is needed or only a revision of the existing document
  • Keep the shared equipment list complete: filling machines, CIP/SIP systems and filter assemblies should all be listed
  • Who supplies the ADE: the ADE of a client's API is usually provided by the client's toxicologist, and the CDMO needs to confirm the reliability of the source and retain the documentation
  • Make risk controls specific: "perform cleaning validation" is not enough. State, for example, "cleaning limit X µg/cm², calculated from ADE Y µg/day, validation report number Z"

Chapter 14: Appendix 1: Case Scenarios

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products | p125–p138
