Original Text — English

14 Appendix 1 – Risk-MaPP Application Examples

14.1 Introduction

The examples provided in this Guide are intended to illustrate four scenarios showing the application of the Risk-MaPP tools. Examples were selected to illustrate suboptimal facility and process configurations. This is intended to demonstrate how a well conducted Quality Risk Management Plan (QRMP) can be used.

None of the examples should be seen as setting a precedent, nor are they a benchmark of an acceptable standard. The examples are intended simply to illustrate that when science and risk-based approaches are applied diligently, facilities and processes that seem to present unacceptable levels of patient risk can consistently deliver product within defined margins for patient safety.

If the risk assessment is for a new product being brought into an existing facility, the QRMP should be part of the change control process.

There are four scenarios which can be summarized as:

  • Scenario 1: Obviously Acceptable
  • Scenario 2: Apparently Acceptable (but was not)
  • Scenario 3: Apparently Unacceptable (but was)
  • Scenario 4: Existing facility with a large product portfolio

To make comparisons easier, examples 1 to 3 use the same basic facility layout, processes, and existing product range. The API being introduced varies between each scenario.

Table 14.1: Compounds Currently Manufactured in the Facility

The ADEs were set by a qualified toxicologist.

The compounds being introduced in each scenario are: Scenario 1 — Another Anti-hypertensive; Scenario 2 — Anti-neoplastic; Scenario 3 — Sex hormone.

A steering committee was set up of the stakeholders including management, legal, regulatory, quality, operations, validation and cleaning specialists, engineering and EHS, and with significant input from toxicology.

The first task of the committee was to conduct a risk analysis, beginning with the logic diagram (see Section 14.6) followed by the use of risk management tools. All stakeholders took part in these exercises.

Outcomes are not as may be perceived by a superficial look.
Commentary

14.1 Introduction to the Appendix

Core Purpose of the Appendix

This appendix presents four realistic scenarios showing how Risk-MaPP tools are applied to decisions on introducing new products into a multi-product manufacturing facility. The key message: a facility that looks problematic on the surface may still be able to produce safely after rigorous QRMP analysis, while a situation that looks fine may hide a critical risk.

Important Disclaimer

These scenarios are not "model answers" or precedents to be cited. Every facility is different, and the scenarios cannot be applied directly. Their value lies in showing the thought process, not in providing fixed conclusions.

Overview of the Four Scenarios

  • Scenario 1 (obviously acceptable): introduction of a similar type of antihypertensive with a comparable risk profile; the assessment is straightforward.
  • Scenario 2 (apparently acceptable, but actually not): introduction of an antineoplastic drug, ultimately abandoned because of a cleaning validation bottleneck.
  • Scenario 3 (apparently unacceptable, but actually acceptable): introduction of a sex hormone, ultimately approved through engineering controls.
  • Scenario 4: a comprehensive assessment of the multi-product mix in an existing facility.

How to Read These Scenarios

Scenarios 1 to 3 use the same base facility (Kingswood, CT); only the new API introduced differs. This design lets readers compare directly how the decision path diverges when compounds of different risk levels are introduced into the same building. Think of it as "the same kitchen cooking different dishes": the equipment is the same, but the cross-contamination risk of certain ingredients is simply different.

The Role of the Logic Diagram

The Logic Diagram (Section 14.6) is the guiding tool for the whole assessment process and works like a decision tree. The assessment team starts from the first question and answers each node in turn, finally concluding whether a dedicated facility is needed or whether the problem can be solved through engineering controls. The assessment in every scenario starts here.

Key Points for CDMO Operations

When a client asks to introduce a new product, under this framework the QRMP should be carried out formally within the change control procedure rather than discussed case by case. Forming a cross-functional steering committee (including toxicology, EHS, regulatory, production and cleaning validation) is a prerequisite for a successful assessment.

Original Text — English

14.2 Scenario 1 (Obviously Acceptable)

Scenario 1 — Obviously Acceptable

In this example, the owner wishes to introduce an anti-hypertensive product (AntitenseTM) into the facility. The owner has an excellent history of compliance with the agencies that cover the territories in which the products are sold.

After the FMEA review, the final version of a QRMP summary was produced to show how ICH Q9 had been applied to minimize or prevent risk to product/patient.

Quality Risk Management Plan for Technology Transfer and Commercialization of Antitense™

Overview

The document covers the introduction of Antitense™ into an existing multiproduct manufacturing facility. A formalized risk management approach was applied to the Kingswood, CT manufacturing facility. The assessment supported the development of a QRMP that addressed the manufacture of the following products: antacid, anti-hypertensive, and NSAID.

A cornerstone of the QRMP is the establishment of Acceptable Daily Exposure (ADE) values for the active ingredients handled in the manufacturing facility. The ADEs serve as the starting point for the rest of the risk assessment process as described by ICH Q9, which ultimately leads to risk controls sufficient to provide adequate protection of workers and patients.

Table 14.2: Scenario 1 – ADE Values for Active Ingredients

The ADEs were set by a qualified toxicologist.

Facility Description

The facility under review is a single corridor facility with access from a GMP gowning area for personnel and a material airlock to a warehouse, in which a pharmacy and sampling area are located. Each of the unit processes is located off the common corridor. Air is supplied on a recirculation basis. Up to two compounds are processed at one time. See Figure 14.1.

Process Equipment Description

The range of operations and equipment used includes:

  • Sampling of the API
  • Weighing to batch weight of the API
  • High shear granulation and wet milling prior to charging to a fluid bed dryer
  • Discharge into the fluid bed dryer
  • Vacuum discharge to an inline cone mill transfer to a bin
  • Bin or V-Shell blend
  • Lubricant is added
  • V-shell discharged into a bin or bin connection to compression
  • Coating for some products
  • Products are bottle or blister packed

Standard Operating Procedures, Personnel, Validation Plans

The facility has an excellent history of compliance with the agencies that cover the territories in which the products are sold.

Commentary

14.2 Scenario 1: Obviously Acceptable

Scenario 1 - Obviously Acceptable

Scenario Setup: Why Is It "Obviously Acceptable"?

The owner wants to introduce a new antihypertensive, Antitense™, into the existing facility. The facility already manufactures an antihypertensive of the same therapeutic class (Anti-hypertensive A, ADE = 0.5 mg/day). The ADE of the new product is 0.3 mg/day, slightly lower than the existing product but of the same order of magnitude, and the existing cleaning process has already been validated to control similar risks.

Interpreting the Key Data: ADE Comparison

The ADE of Antitense™ = 0.3 mg/day, calculated as follows:

  • Minimum clinical therapeutic dose: 10 mg/day
  • Adjustment factor for inter-individual variability (AFH): 10
  • Adjustment factor for extrapolation to a no-effect dose (AFL): 3
  • ADE = 10 mg ÷ 10 ÷ 3 = 0.33 mg/day ≈ 0.3 mg/day

Compared with the existing Anti-hypertensive A (ADE = 0.5), Antitense™ is only slightly more stringent and does not cross an order-of-magnitude threshold.

Facility Conditions: Important Background

The facility in this scenario uses an air recirculation system, which becomes a problem point in Scenario 2. Scenario 1 is acceptable partly because the introduced compound has a risk level comparable to the existing product and does not require a special containment design.

Facility features: single-pass design, shared corridors, up to two compounds processed at the same time.

Risk Assessment

Risk Identification — Hazard Identification and Dose-Response Assessment

A review of all available data on each API (e.g., data included in the Clinical Investigators Brochure or New Drug Application or the scientific literature) was completed to identify all possible adverse effects. For each compound, the critical effect (the first adverse effect observed at the low end of the dose-response curve) was identified.

The ADE value was derived by dividing the no effect level for the critical endpoint by appropriate adjustment (uncertainty or safety) factors. The ADE was used to derive product contact surface cleaning target values. Additional calculations were made to reflect batch size, product contact equipment surface area, and maximum daily dose of the next product, to arrive at the final surface target value or rinse concentration.

Establishment of ADE for Antitense™

An ADE value of 0.3 mg/day was established for Antitense™. The ADE was based on the low clinical dose of 10 mg and incorporates appropriate adjustment factors: interindividual variability (AFH = 10) and extrapolation to a no effect level (AFL = 3).

Risk Analysis

A review of monitoring data for the existing products and processes was completed to ensure the facility can safely manufacture the products.

Risk Evaluation

The analysis quickly showed that the new compound posed less risk than some compounds already in manufacture, for which data existed showing that they posed an acceptably low level of risk to patient or products.

Risk Assessment Analysis

ADE Derivation Logic (Key Concept)

The ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure) is the cornerstone of the whole Risk-MaPP assessment. The derivation proceeds as follows:

  1. Literature review: identify all adverse effects of the compound
  2. Identify the "critical effect": the first adverse effect at the low end of the dose-response curve
  3. Find the no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL or equivalent) for that effect
  4. Divide by the appropriate adjustment factors (AFH, AFL, etc.) to obtain the ADE
  5. Use the ADE to derive the cleaning validation target values (cleaning limits)

Framework for Thinking: Compare Risks Rather Than Judging Absolute Risk

The key insight of risk assessment is to ask not "is this new compound safe?" but "is the risk of this new compound higher than that of the compounds we already manufacture?". If the existing system already controls Anti-hypertensive A (ADE 0.5) successfully, then for Antitense™ with an ADE of 0.3 it is only necessary to verify that cleaning meets a slightly stricter target, with no need to redesign the facility.

Quick Judgment in the Risk Evaluation

"The analysis quickly showed that the new compound posed less risk than some compounds already in manufacture": this sentence is the core of why Scenario 1 holds. Existing monitoring data already show that the facility can control more challenging compounds, and although the ADE of the new product is slightly lower, its overall risk profile does not exceed the existing capability.

Summary — Scenario 1

The new anti-hypertensive has an ADE that is slightly lower than the current anti-hypertensive agent which is manufactured under existing QRMP No. 170129. The new anti-hypertensive can be cleaned to acceptable levels using the current cleaning protocols and methods. The introduction of this new anti-hypertensive poses no additional risk of patient exposure.

Scenario 1: CDMO Conclusion and Practical Implications

Why Was the Assessment Completed "Quickly"?

Scenario 1 is called "Obviously Acceptable" because:

  • The new and existing products belong to the same pharmacological class (antihypertensives) with similar toxicological profiles
  • The ADE of the new product (0.3 mg/day) and that of the existing product (0.5 mg/day) are of the same order of magnitude
  • The existing cleaning process has already been shown by historical data to meet the stricter requirement
  • The existing monitoring database is sufficient and no additional exposure assessment is needed

Logic Diagram Path (Scenario 1)

Q1
Does the risk level of the new API exceed that of existing products?
Answer: No (similar ADE, same class of drug)
Q2
Can the existing cleaning system meet the new cleaning limit?
Answer: Yes (historical data show the existing procedure can achieve it)
Conclusion
It can be introduced without facility modification. It falls within the scope of the existing QRMP, and only the cleaning validation target values need to be updated.

Practical Implications for a CDMO

For a CDMO, Scenario 1 represents the ideal case for introducing a new product: the client's new product is toxicologically comparable to the existing products, and the facility already has the corresponding control capability. Key documentation work: update the QRMP to include the new product, update the cleaning validation plan to reflect the slightly stricter target values, and confirm that the existing SOPs cover all process steps for the new product.

Original Text — English

14.3 Scenario 2 (Apparently Acceptable (but was NOT))

Scenario 2 — Apparently Acceptable (but was NOT)

In this example, the owner wishes to introduce an anti-neoplastic agent into the facility. The owner has an excellent history of compliance with the agencies that cover the territories in which the products are sold.

After the FMEA review, the final version of a QRMP summary was produced to show how ICH Q9 had been applied to minimize or prevent risk to product/patient.

Quality Risk Management Plan for Technology Transfer and Commercialization of Anti-neoplastic Agent C

Overview

The document covers the introduction of anti-neoplastic Agent C into an existing multiproduct manufacturing facility. A formalized risk management approach was applied to the Kingswood, CT manufacturing facility. The assessment supported the development of a QRMP addressing: antacid, anti-hypertensive, anti-neoplastic, and NSAID.

Table 14.3: Scenario 2 – ADE Values for Active Ingredients

The ADEs were set by a qualified toxicologist.

Facility Description

The facility under review is a single corridor facility with access from a GMP gowning area for personnel and a material airlock to a warehouse. Each of the unit processes is located off the common corridor. Air is supplied on a once through basis. Up to two compounds are processed at one time. See Figure 14.2.

Segregation Philosophy

Following the FMEA analysis, changes were made to the facility to protect the existing products from cross-contamination by the proposed anti-neoplastic. The philosophy behind these changes:

  • The processes were closed by the retrofitting of engineering control methods. API handling for sampling and subdivision takes place in an isolator for anti-neoplastic Agent C. Closed transfer using bins and split butterfly valves (SBV) was introduced for all transfers for all compounds.
  • Fan assisted safe change room boundary filtration introduced to reduce the risk of fugitive API distribution by HVAC system.
  • Additional gowning: disposable coveralls, bootees, and hair nets in rooms where anti-neoplastic Agent C is being processed. Gowning removed at room boundary.
  • Access to process rooms controlled by electronic verification. Only correct materials and personnel allowed to enter.
  • Cleaning bay divided into clean and dirty sides with a pass through for equipment. Gowning to prevent cross-contamination for dirty side personnel.
  • Anti-neoplastic Agent C equipment subjected to a closed in place clean before leaving the process room.
  • All walls, floors, and ceilings of hard washable coved construction.
  • Material flows controlled by electronic verification (bar code readers) allowing separation in time between material transits in the corridor. Movement of more than one compound at a time with a 1-minute dwell is enforced.
Commentary

14.3 Scenario 2: Apparently Acceptable (but Actually Not)

Scenario 2 - Apparently Acceptable (but was NOT)

Scenario Setup: Why Is It "Apparently Acceptable"?

The owner wants to introduce an antineoplastic drug, Anti-neoplastic Agent C, an alkylating agent. At first glance, the owner has already:

  • Invested heavily in engineering controls in the facility (isolators, closed transfer, dedicated air handling)
  • Established a complete SOP system (personnel entry/exit control, cleaning procedures, etc.)
  • Used once-through ventilation to avoid recirculation contamination

Judging by the list of engineering controls alone, everything seems to be in place. So where does the problem lie?

Key Difference: A Jump in the Order of Magnitude of the ADE

The ADE of Anti-neoplastic Agent C = 0.001 mg/day (i.e. 1 μg/day), far lower than that of the compounds already in the facility:

  • Antacid: 50 mg/day (50,000 times higher)
  • Anti-hypertensive: 0.5 mg/day (500 times higher)
  • NSAID: 40 mg/day (40,000 times higher)

A gap of this magnitude means the cleaning limit requirement will be far stricter than the historical performance of the existing equipment.

Why Is the ADE So Low?

Anti-neoplastic Agent C is a direct acting genotoxic carcinogen. Its toxicological characteristics:

  • An alkylating agent that interferes with DNA replication and RNA transcription
  • Animal studies: developmental toxicity at doses as low as 2.5 mg/kg/day
  • Damages sperm DNA in adult rats
  • Increases the incidence of malignant tumors in multiple animal species
  • Human studies confirm carcinogenicity

The ADE is derived by cancer risk extrapolation: 1 μg/day corresponds to an additional cancer risk of 1 × 10⁻⁵ (i.e. an excess risk of ten in a million).

The Significance of "Once-Through" vs. "Recirculation" Ventilation

The Scenario 1 facility uses air recirculation, which is an unacceptable risk for a highly toxic compound such as the one in Scenario 2. In Scenario 2 the owner has switched to once-through ventilation, which is the correct engineering decision and ensures that airborne particles do not circulate within the facility.

Engineering Control Architecture (Specific to Scenario 2)

The controls introduced in Scenario 2 are more comprehensive than in Scenario 1 and include:

  • Isolators: sampling and weighing are performed inside isolators
  • Closed transfer: Split Butterfly Valve (SBV), Rapid Transfer Port (RTP)
  • HEPA filtration at the room boundary: fan-assisted safe-change filtration to prevent escaped API from entering the HVAC
  • Electronic access control: barcode readers control the movement of personnel and materials, with a mandatory 1-minute interval in the corridor
  • Clean-in-place (CIP): equipment is cleaned in a closed manner before it leaves the process room

Process and Manufacturing Equipment Description

The range of operations and equipment used includes:

  • Sampling of incoming API in an isolator; sample sent to QA lab in a closed transfer device — Rapid Transfer Port (RTP).
  • Weighing to batch weight in an isolator; closed transfer in a bin with a Split Butterfly Valve (SBV).
  • High shear granulation and wet milling prior to charging to a fluid bed dryer. The active is layered into the granulator; heel retention is very high, requiring manual scraping for yield. Process conducted in an isolator; transfer in a closed bin.
  • Discharge into the fluid bed dryer bowl in an isolator. Fluid bed dryer is a closed operation: vacuum discharge to an isolator with a hammer type mill; collection in a weigh hopper; vacuum transfer to a bin via SBV.
  • Bin blend or bin to V-Shell transfer using transfer valves and bins.
  • Lubricant added via transfer valve on side arm of V-shell or via charge using SBV to a bin.
  • V-Cone® discharged into SBV bins or blending bin used without transfer. Bins tested for blend uniformity using PAT.
  • Products that are tableted passed to compression with the bin connected via SBV.
  • Products B and C are coated; A is not. Data shows no risk of exposure from coating.
  • Products are bottle or blister packed.

Following the steps in the logic diagram (see Section 14.6), manufacture of the compound was possible.

Personnel access into the process rooms is controlled by card reader. Personnel are trained in gown/de-gown procedures. Personnel who clean equipment put on wet gear and boots at the designated position.

Process Equipment: Key Points of Closed-System Design

Design Logic of the Closed System

The whole process is designed around one core principle: fully closed operation at every point where API could escape. The key equipment points:

  • Isolator: sampling, weighing, granulation
  • Split Butterfly Valve (SBV): all closed material transfer interfaces
  • Rapid Transfer Port (RTP): transfer of laboratory samples
  • Fluid bed dryer: closed operation, vacuum discharge
  • V-Blender: material in and out through SBVs

Logic Diagram Path (Scenario 2: Initial Assessment)

Q: Is containment needed?
Anti-neoplastic ADE = 0.001 mg/day, a highly potent compound (HPAPI)
→ Engineering controls are needed
Q: Are the engineering controls sufficient?
Isolators, closed transfer, once-through ventilation and access control are installed
→ Appears adequate on the surface
Q: Can cleaning meet the target?
Further FMEA analysis is needed...
→ The problem is found here

HVAC

Figure 14.5🔍 shows the pressure cascade and airflow direction in the rooms. The flow is always away from the corridor and toward the rear wall. Supply is through HEPA filters; exhaust via roughing and HEPA filters to protect the ductwork. The MALs and PALs create lock chambers with door operation delay to allow the airlock to be purged. The system is once through — no risk of recirculating particulate.

Exhausts are protected by redundant HEPA filtration (two passes through 99.997% filters with pressure differentials indicated and alarmed). Routine challenge testing is performed.

For product to cross-contaminate another product it must: escape the process; pass through airlocks against low velocity airflow; travel into the corridor; enter another airlock; enter another room; enter the other process.

Key SOPs Brought About by FMEA Analysis

  • Access control procedures for personnel and materials into process areas
  • Cleaning procedures and gowning
  • Health-based cleaning protocol
  • Material handling procedures
  • Gowning/de-gowning procedures
  • HVAC performance monitoring and filter replacement
  • Procedures to cover closed operation
  • Sampling and subdivision protocols
  • Handling and storage of anti-neoplastic Agent C
  • Upset recovery protocols

Analysis of the HVAC and SOP Systems

Multiple Airflow Barrier Design

The Scenario 2 HVAC design demonstrates the principle of "defence in depth": even if a release occurs in the process room, the contaminant still has to breach multiple barriers before reaching the corridor or other process rooms:

  1. The process room itself is at negative pressure (relative to the corridor)
  2. Purge delay in the airlock
  3. The corridor is at positive pressure (relative to the process room)
  4. Double HEPA filtration of the exhaust (99.997% × 2)
  5. Once-through ventilation (no recirculation risk)

FMEA-Driven SOP Design

Note that the SOP list includes "Upset recovery protocols". This shows that the assessment team recognized that, although the system is controlled during normal operation, predefined response procedures are needed when an equipment upset occurs (such as a seal failure or spill). This kind of "worst-case scenario" SOP is an essential document for an HPAPI facility.

The Trap of "Apparently Acceptable"

At this point, looking only at the list of engineering controls (isolators, SBVs, HEPA, electronic access control), it does look complete. But the strength of Risk-MaPP is that it does not allow the assessment to stop here. The FMEA forces the team to examine the residue risk at each process step one by one, and the hidden bomb is only found in the next step.

Risk Assessment — Scenario 2

Establishment of ADE for Anti-neoplastic Agent C

Anti-neoplastic Agent C is an alkylating agent used in the treatment of numerous myeloproliferative and lymphoproliferative disorders and solid malignancies. It disrupts nucleic acid function by interfering with DNA replication and transcription of RNA. The recommended oral dosage ranges from 1 to 5 mg/kg/day for adults or 10 to 15 mg/kg IV every 7 to 10 days.

Anti-neoplastic Agent C is considered moderately toxic orally and produced adverse developmental effects in animals at dosages as low as 2.5 mg/kg/day. It was shown to damage DNA in spermatozoa from adult male rats and increased the incidence of malignant tumors by several routes of administration. The compound has been shown to be a human carcinogen.

The critical endpoint for deriving a health-based value is cancer. Because it is a direct acting genotoxic material, the ADE was derived by extrapolating cancer risks from animal and human tumor data. An ADE of 1 μg/day was calculated corresponding to a 1 × 10⁻⁵ excess cancer risk.

Risk Analysis — Plausible Pathways

Mix-up: Bar code reading control of all equipment and materials; separation of clean and dirty equipment by room; tagging of clean and dirty equipment.

Retention on Product Contact Surfaces: Swab monitoring of cleaning performance against health-based limits; visually clean inspection; routine monitoring shows consistent performance well below the health-based limit — except where the FMEA identified the blender issue.

Mechanical Transfer: Critical transfers are closed; all equipment and materials wiped down or bagged before leaving the process room; tack mats used at room boundaries.

Airborne Transfer: Processes occur in rooms protected by airlocks; corridor positive relative to process rooms; redundant HEPA filtration on exhausts.

Cleaning Review

Cleaning Process Development activities:

  • Cleanability assessments
  • Cleaning agent(s) selection
  • Design of cleaning processes
  • FMEAs for each cleaning process
  • Modification of cleaning processes based on FMEA results

Cleaning Risk Evaluation activities:

  • Collection of swab sample data on each cleaning procedure
  • Process capability evaluations of swab sample data
  • FMEAs of overall cleaning program to identify high risk procedures
  • Improvement/optimization of high risk procedures

Cleaning Process Review: a cleanability assessment was performed on the product and the product was found to be easily cleaned by the existing processes. The cleanability rank was a 2, with a rank of 4 for the hardest to clean product. Current cleaning agents were found to be appropriate for solubilizing and suspending the product.

In-Depth Analysis of the Risk Assessment

ADE Derivation for a Carcinogen: The No-Threshold Model

Anti-neoplastic Agent C is a direct acting genotoxic substance. Unlike ordinary toxic substances, for which the NOAEL ÷ adjustment factor method is used, genotoxic carcinogens are usually treated with linear no-threshold extrapolation:

  • Estimate the cancer risk from animal or human tumor data
  • Select an acceptable level of excess cancer risk (1 × 10⁻⁵ in this case)
  • Back-calculate the corresponding daily exposure = ADE = 1 μg/day

This explains why the ADE is 3 orders of magnitude lower than for ordinary compounds.

Assessment Framework for the Four Exposure Pathways

Risk-MaPP requires assessment of all "plausible pathways" of exposure:

  • Mix-up: incorrect labels, misplaced equipment → barcode control
  • Retention on product contact surfaces: inadequate cleaning → cleaning validation
  • Mechanical transfer: contamination carried out by moving equipment → closed systems
  • Airborne transfer: dust or airflow carrying contamination out → HVAC design

Cleanability: Initially Reassuring

Note: the cleanability assessment (cleanability rank = 2, with the hardest-to-clean product at 4) shows that Agent C is relatively easy to clean and that the cleaning agent is suitable. This led the assessment team to assume at first that cleaning was not a problem. But this reflects only the intrinsic cleanability of the compound and does not yet consider the challenges arising from the geometry and surface area of the equipment.

Ppk Analysis: The Challenge from "Quantity" to "Limit"

Think of cleaning validation as target shooting: the existing equipment has been landing close to the bullseye (high Ppk), so cleaning performance is stable. But once Agent C is introduced, the bullseye (the cleaning limit) moves to an extremely small value never reached before. Even if your shooting accuracy (process capability) has not changed, a bullseye that far away simply cannot be hit.

Risk Evaluation — The Critical Finding

New Product Review

The new anti-neoplastic product was evaluated against the existing program for its compatibility with the cleaning processes and its ADE was assessed against the existing swab sampling database for the facility.

The FMEA analysis of cleaning showed a significant risk occurring in the V-blender which, due to its significant surface area, required a level of residue significantly below the historically achievable results.

Cleaning Risk Evaluation — Ppk Analysis

The swab data for all cleaning processes were found to have high Process Performance Indices (Ppk) with undetectable levels Defects per Million Opportunities (ppm) — with the exception of the blender.

The Ppk for the blender was 0.34 with the potential for Defects per Million Opportunities of 14,994. This evaluation indicated that the current cleaning process for the blender is not satisfactory to safely clean the new product.

In order to introduce the new product into the facility, the cleaning process for the blender would need to be improved or a new blender would have to be purchased.

Risk Control

A conclusion was made that the risks to patients with the existing manufacturing process was unacceptably high. This was due to the potential carryover of residuals of Anti-neoplastic Agent C to other products from the V-blender.

A Design of Experiment (DoE) was performed to improve the Blender Cleaning Process. Cleaning agent concentration, cleaning time, cleaning temperature, and rinse time were evaluated. The new data indicate that the blender cannot be effectively cleaned to safe levels for the new product. The introduction of a dedicated blender was not possible and so the proposal was abandoned and the anti-neoplastic will not be manufactured at this site.

Risk Communication

No further communication on the proposed introduction of Anti-neoplastic Agent C is required because the proposal was rejected.

Summary — Scenario 2

The FMEA analysis of cleaning showed a significant risk occurring in the V-blender which due to its significant surface area required a level of residue significantly below the historically achievable results. Since the other processes required a V-blender and no space was available for a dedicated blender, the owner decided not to manufacture Anti-neoplastic Agent C.

Scenario 2: Key Findings and Lessons

Core Finding: The V-Blender Becomes the Blocking Factor

The turning point of the whole of Scenario 2 is the quantitative data from the FMEA cleaning assessment:

  • For all equipment except the V-Blender, the cleaning Ppk values are high and the ppm (defect rate) approaches zero
  • The V-Blender has a Ppk = 0.34 (the normal standard is Ppk ≥ 1.33 for adequate capability)
  • The corresponding defect rate DPM = 14,994 ppm (i.e. nearly 15,000 out of every million cleanings fail to meet the target)
  • Root cause: the V-Blender has a large surface area, and for a compound with an ADE = 1 μg/day the cleaning limit is extremely low, far beyond existing capability

What the Ppk Value Means

The process performance index (Ppk) represents the ability of the actual process output relative to the specification limit:

  • Ppk ≥ 1.33: good process capability (usually required by cGMP)
  • Ppk = 1.00: borderline, with a failure rate of about 0.27%
  • Ppk = 0.34: seriously inadequate, with residues exceeding the limit after cleaning in almost 1.5% of batches

Converted into patient risk: roughly one in every 67 batches (≈1000 ppm ÷ 14.994) could have a cleaning failure, leaving the next product contaminated with a carcinogen.

The DoE Attempt Fails: Where Is the Boundary?

The assessment team did not give up easily. They carried out a formal design of experiments (DoE) to systematically optimize:

  • Cleaning agent concentration
  • Cleaning time
  • Cleaning temperature
  • Rinse time

DoE conclusion: even under optimized conditions, the V-Blender simply cannot be cleaned to a sufficiently safe level. This results from the equipment geometry (large surface area) combined with physicochemical limits, and no SOP can overcome it.

Final Decision: Reject the Introduction

The solution could have been to buy a dedicated V-Blender (dedication), but the building could not accommodate it. With the equipment problem unresolved, the owner made the correct decision: to decline to manufacture Anti-neoplastic Agent C at this facility.

This is itself an important regulatory signal: the value of the Risk-MaPP process lies not in "enabling every product to be manufactured" but in providing the scientific basis to reject products that cannot be manufactured safely.

Complete Logic Diagram Path (Scenario 2)

Step 1
ADE = 1 μg/day → a highly potent substance (HPAPI)
→ Engineering controls / FMEA required
Step 2
Isolators, SBVs and once-through ventilation introduced → airborne and mechanical transfer pathways controlled
→ Appears able to proceed
Step 3
Cleaning FMEA: the V-Blender has a large surface area and the cleaning limit exceeds historical capability (Ppk = 0.34)
→ Key risk identified: cleaning cannot meet the target
Step 4
DoE optimization attempted → failed; dedicated blender purchase → no space in the building
→ Final conclusion: cannot be introduced, proposal rejected

Core Lessons for a CDMO

The most important takeaways for a CDMO:

  • Complete engineering controls do not mean cleaning validation will necessarily succeed. The two need to be assessed independently.
  • The challenge in cleaning validation is not only "can it be cleaned" but whether the equipment geometry (surface area) allows the required cleaning limit to be achieved.
  • Ppk analysis is an effective tool for quantifying cleaning risk. Do not look only at "pass/fail"; look at whether the process capability index is sufficient to meet the strict limit of the new product.
  • Being able to turn down a client's request (on a scientific basis) is itself a demonstration of the Risk-MaPP system delivering value.
  • Early in the quotation or technology transfer assessment, first check the historical cleaning Ppk of the equipment with the largest surface area in the facility, and only then assess whether a highly potent compound can be introduced.

Cross-Scenario Comparison: Scenarios 1 vs. 2

Scenario 1 vs. Scenario 2: Comparison Summary

The Deciding Difference: A Jump in Order of Magnitude Triggers a Different Assessment Path

The two scenarios use the same facility, yet the assessment results are completely opposite. The key points are:

  • Scenario 1: the new ADE (0.3) and the lowest existing ADE (0.5) are of the same order of magnitude, and the existing system can cover it.
  • Scenario 2: the new ADE (0.001) is two to three orders of magnitude lower, triggering entirely different engineering control requirements, and further analysis reveals physical limits of the cleaning equipment that cannot be overcome.

Core Message of This Chapter

Do not treat "strict-looking engineering controls = can be introduced" as the end point of the assessment. Risk-MaPP requires you to work systematically through all pathways, especially the quantitative process capability analysis of cleaning validation, in order to reach a conclusion that is truly science-based.

A Signal to Regulatory Reviewers

The ultimate self-rejection in Scenario 2 is itself evidence of a mature quality culture. What regulators most want to see is a company that can use scientific methods to find the boundary before a problem becomes a defect, and make the right decision, whether or not that decision serves its commercial interests.

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Ed.): Risk-Based Manufacture of Pharmaceutical Products | p139–p162

14.4 Scenario 3 Overview QRMP Facility Description Risk Assessment Risk Control Scenario 3 Summary 14.5 Scenario 4 Overview Background Risk Assessment FMEA Risk Evaluation and Control Scenario 4 Summary 14.6 Logic Diagram
14.4 Scenario 3 — Apparently Unacceptable (but WAS)
Original Text (English)

In this example, the owner wishes to introduce a sex hormone into the facility. Sex hormones have been under increased scrutiny because of the risk of effects on fertility and birth defects. The owner has an excellent history of compliance with the agencies that cover the territories in which the products are sold.

A steering committee was set up of the stakeholders including management, legal, regulatory, and quality, operations, validation and cleaning specialist, engineering and Environment, Health, and Safety (EHS), with input from toxicology.

The first task of the committee was to conduct a risk analysis, beginning with the logic diagram (see Section 14.6) followed by the use of FMEA tools. All stakeholders took part in these exercises.

As soon as the committee had assembled sufficient data, they sought informal discussion with the regulatory agencies and discussed the proposal in concept. Supporting scientific data was made available and a draft of the QRMP document was tabled.

Following the logic diagram, segregation was possibly required for this product because it is being supplied to a country that has historically required segregation for sex hormones. An FMEA analysis was then undertaken with a team of stakeholders, this technique was chosen because it addresses a wide range of ICH Q9 topics, including:

  • Risk identification
  • Risk ranking (using the RPN scores)
  • Exposure control assessment
  • Risk analysis

After the FMEA review, the final version of the QRMP was produced to show how ICH Q9 had been applied to minimize or prevent risk to product and operators.

Commentary

Why "apparently unacceptable"?

Historically, regulators in many regions have required sex hormones to be made in a dedicated facility. At first glance, introducing one into an existing multi-product facility looks like a clear violation. The key lesson of this scenario, however, is that a rigorous risk assessment process can scientifically justify shared-facility manufacture.

Key points of the scenario

  • New substance: sex hormone Agent H (very low ADE = 0.001 mg/day)
  • Challenge: regulations in some countries have historically required dedicated facilities
  • Strategy: early informal discussion with the regulator + FMEA + QRMP
  • Conclusion: with appropriate controls, manufacture in the shared facility is feasible

Analogy: regulatory requirement vs. scientific justification

It is like a driver who is unsure whether a road section is open to traffic: rather than driving in regardless or giving up, the driver first asks the traffic authority. Talking to the authority early and presenting scientific data is the heart of a compliance strategy.

The importance of regulatory communication

As soon as the committee had sufficient data, it proactively held an informal discussion with the regulatory authority and submitted a draft QRMP. This "communicate first, then act" approach lowered the risk at the later review stage and demonstrated the company's transparency and sense of responsibility.

14.4 Scenario 3 — QRMP Overview
Original Text (English)

Quality Risk Management Plan (QRMP) for Technology Transfer and Commercialization of Sex Hormone Agent H

Overview

This document summarizes the QRMP for XYZ Pharma at the Kingswood, CT site. The plan embraces the principles of ICH Q9 and uses risk management tools to support manufacturing strategies designed to minimize risks to product and operators.

The proposal is to introduce a sex hormone Agent H into the facility. The existing compounds all have QRMPs in place.

A formalized risk management approach was applied to the manufacturing facility. This involved a holistic assessment that identified the potential hazards and risks to product and operators for all products handled in the facility to ensure that appropriate controls were in place to manufacture these products safely.

A cornerstone of the QRMP is the establishment of ADE values for the active ingredients handled in the manufacturing facility. The ADE values serve as the starting point for the rest of the risk assessment process as described by ICH Q9.

Table 14.4: Scenario 3 — Active Ingredients in the Facility

Commentary

The role of the QRMP

The Quality Risk Management Plan (QRMP) is an integrated document that systematically applies the ICH Q9 framework to a specific facility or product introduction. It is more than a risk assessment report; it is an action plan that sets out:

  • Which hazards were identified
  • Which assessment methods were used
  • Which controls were implemented
  • How they are monitored and reviewed

What the ADE differences mean

The table shows clearly that the ADE of Agent H (sex hormone) is far lower than that of the other compounds:

  • Antacid A: 50 mg/day (relatively safe)
  • Anti-hypertensive B：0.5 mg/day
  • Agent H: 0.001 mg/day (lowest, highly potent)
  • NSAID D：40 mg/day

ADE = 0.001 mg/day = 1 µg/day, which means residues on equipment surfaces must be controlled at an extremely low level. This is the core reason the scenario is "apparently unacceptable".

Definition of ADE (Acceptable Daily Exposure)

The ADE is the upper limit of daily exposure below which no adverse effect is expected in the most sensitive populations (including patients and operators), covering lifetime exposure. It is derived by a qualified toxicologist from toxicological data and includes appropriate safety factors.

14.4 Scenario 3 — Facility Description & Controls
Original Text (English)

Facility Description

The facility under review is a single corridor facility with access from a GMP gowning area for personnel and a material airlock to a warehouse, in which a pharmacy and sampling area is located. Each of the unit processes is located off the common corridor with both MALs (Material Airlocks) and PALs (Personnel Airlocks); both are single chamber and are pressurized to create a pressure cascade between the processing area and the corridor.

A suite of rooms are provided for dirty side wash, clean side dry, and separate clean storage of equipment. The area is served by a single Air Handling Unit (AHU) with a positive pressure corridor. Air is supplied on a once through basis (no recirculation). Room boundary redundant filtration is provided. Up to two compounds are processed at one time.

Segregation Philosophy (Post-FMEA)

  • The sex hormone API processes were closed. Surrogate testing showed that all other exposures provided an acceptable level of risk and plausible pathways had sufficient redundancy to be protective of other products.
  • Additional gowning introduced: operators wear disposable coveralls, bootees, and hair nets in sex hormone processing rooms. This gowning is removed at the room boundary.
  • Access to process rooms controlled by electronic verification; only the correct materials and personnel are allowed to enter.
  • Sex hormone handling equipment subjected to a closed in-place clean before leaving the process room.
  • All walls, floors, and ceilings are of hard washable coved construction.

Process and Equipment

  • Sampling of incoming API in an isolator with RTP (Rapid Transfer Port) to QA lab
  • Weighing in isolator — closed transfer bin with SBV (Split Butterfly Valve)
  • High shear granulation and wet milling in isolator; heel retention requires manual scraping
  • FBD discharge via SBV; vacuum discharge to in-line cone mill → bin via SBV
  • Bin blend; lubricant via transfer valve; PAT for blend uniformity
  • Compression via SBV-connected bin; coating (data shows no risk of exposure)
  • Bottle or blister packing

HVAC (Figure 14.10🔍)

Pressure cascade: flow always away from corridor and operator zones toward rear wall. Supply through HEPA filters, exhaust via roughing and HEPA filters. MALs and PALs create lock chambers with door operation delay (purge before door release). System is once through — no recirculation risk. Hanging vinyl strip screens at room entrances.

Commentary

Single corridor facility layout

The facility is organized around a single corridor, with each process room separated by a MAL (material airlock) and a PAL (personnel airlock). Advantages of this design:

  • The pressure cascade controls the direction of airflow
  • The airlock "dwell period" prevents instantaneous contamination when a door is opened
  • The corridor is kept at positive pressure relative to the process rooms, so contamination cannot flow in from the corridor

The core logic of closed processing

The ADE of the sex hormone is only 1 µg/day, so with open processing even a trace of dust could exceed the limit. The solution is engineering controls:

  • Isolator: sampling and weighing are performed in an isolated environment
  • SBV (split butterfly valve): sealed material transfer that prevents dust release
  • RTP (rapid transfer port): safe transfer of samples to the QA laboratory
  • Clean-in-place (CIP): equipment is cleaned before it leaves the process room

Analogy: what an SBV does

A split butterfly valve works like a "docking connector": one half is fitted to the material container and the other to the equipment port. They open together when docked and each seals when separated, so the powder never contacts the outside air, greatly reducing the risk of dust release.

Multiple layers of protection in the HVAC design

The HVAC system is "once-through" (no recirculation), which means:

  • Contaminants cannot be carried by recirculated air to other areas
  • Exhaust air passes through HEPA filtration (coarse + high-efficiency, two stages of protection)
  • Airlock purging ensures that contamination from opening a door does not enter the corridor

With this design, the risk of the "airborne transfer" route is reduced to an acceptable level through multiple barriers.

14.4 Scenario 3 — Risk Assessment
Original Text (English)

Risk Identification

A risk assessment process was used to derive criteria to support decision making and control of risks to product and workers. The risk assessment involved an evaluation of all data relevant to support hazard identification, assessment of potency (dose-response) and severity, exposure assessment, and characterization of risk. Risk controls were implemented in a manner commensurate with the risk.

A review of all available data on each API was completed to identify all possible adverse effects. The critical effect (first adverse effect at the low end of the dose-response curve) was identified. The ADE value was derived by dividing the no-effect level for the critical endpoint by appropriate adjustment (uncertainty or safety) factors or to extrapolate to an acceptable level of cancer risk. ADE was used to derive product contact surface cleaning limits.

Establishment of ADE for the Sex Hormone (Agent H)

An ADE value of 1 µg/day was established for sex hormone Agent H. The rationale is summarized in the quality document "Risk Assessment for Sex Hormones." The ADE was based on the low clinical dose and incorporates appropriate adjustment factors to address:

  • Interindividual variability
  • Extrapolation to a no-effect level
  • Differences in bioavailability
  • Residual uncertainties regarding potential developmental effects

Risk Analysis — Plausible Pathways

Mix-up:

  • Bar code reading control of all equipment and materials where they pass into processing rooms
  • Separation of clean and dirty equipment by room; tagging of clean and dirty equipment

Retention (Product Contact Surfaces):

  • Swab monitoring against health-based limits; visually clean inspection
  • Routine monitoring shows consistent performance well below health-based limit (Figure 14.11🔍)
  • Cleaning procedures include disassembly; dedicated blending bin

Mechanical Transfer:

  • Critical transfers are closed
  • All equipment and materials wiped down or bagged before leaving process room
  • Tack mats at room boundaries; outer gowning removed at boundaries

Airborne Transfer:

  • All processes in rooms protected by airlocks with interlocked doors and dwell period
  • Corridor positive pressure to process room; redundant HEPA filtration (99.997% × 2 passes)
  • For cross-contamination to occur, product must: escape process → pass through airlocks against low-velocity flow → travel corridor → enter another airlock → enter process room
  • Gowning protocols and access restriction prevent unauthorized entry
Commentary

The four cross-contamination routes

  • Mix-up: the wrong material enters a process room (human error)
  • Retention: material from the previous batch remains on equipment surfaces
  • Mechanical transfer: residue clings to personnel, equipment or material surfaces and is carried out
  • Airborne transfer: particles travel through the air system

The "multiple barrier" argument for airborne transfer

The ISPE text lists in detail every barrier that airborne material must cross:

  1. escape the process
  2. pass through an airlock against the low-velocity airflow into the corridor
  3. travel along the corridor
  4. pass again against the low-velocity airflow into another airlock
  5. enter another process room and process

A physical barrier stops the material at every step, so the probability of the whole path occurring is extremely low. This "multiple barrier argument" is the core logic of modern GMP risk assessment.

Deriving the cleaning limit from the ADE

The ADE (1 µg/day) is the starting point, combined with:

  • The maximum daily dose for the patient receiving the next product
  • The product-contact surface area of the equipment
  • The batch size

These give the maximum allowable residue on equipment surfaces (µg/cm²), which serves as the acceptance criterion for cleaning validation.

Surrogate testing

For potentially hazardous substances such as sex hormones, the real API cannot be used in exposure tests for cleaning validation. A surrogate should have similar physicochemical properties (solubility, adhesion) but lower toxicity, so that it can be handled safely.

14.4 Scenario 3 — Cleaning Review, FMEA & Risk Control
Original Text (English)

Cleaning Review — Process Capability

The existing swab sample database was statistically evaluated against the new product's ADE. The swab data for all cleaning processes were found to have high Process Performance Indices (Ppk) with undetectable levels of Defects per Million Opportunities, with the exception of the blender.

The Ppk for the blender was 0.34 with potential for 14,994 Defects per Million Opportunities. This indicated the current cleaning process for the blender is not satisfactory to safely clean the new product.

A Design of Experiment (DoE) was performed to improve the blender cleaning process. Cleaning agent concentration, cleaning time, cleaning temperature, and rinse time were evaluated. The new data indicate the blender can be effectively cleaned to safe levels for the new product.

  • Ppk after DoE: 2.01
  • Defects per Million Opportunities: 251

Because these data indicate a small possibility of product residue, the blender will require a visual inspection and monitoring after each cleaning. To minimize regulatory impact, it was decided to dedicate the blending bin.

Risk Control

It was concluded that sex hormone Agent H could be introduced into the multiproduct manufacturing facility without causing a risk to patients. The control measures adopted were based on a belief that technique and skill-based procedures have a tendency to fail. The controls implemented are of a procedural, mechanical, and electronic nature and are designed to fail safe. Implemented controls include:

  • Electronic access and inventory control — entry and exit from any room permitted only for materials and personnel required at that time. Secondary systems ensure it cannot be bypassed.
  • Critical processes were closed and tested to ensure they afforded both operator and product protection robustly.
  • Automated cleaning introduced to minimize variability from manual cleaning. Small parts still manually cleaned with updated procedures to reduce errors.
  • Air flows and pressures were reversed to maintain security in the event of an upset. Redundant HEPA filtration protects the rest of the facility.
  • Control system effectiveness was verified with quantitative data.
  • Hierarchy of controls closely followed to ensure selection of appropriate technology.
  • Systems designed to alarm when out of range and default to a safe condition.

Risk Communication

A stakeholder team comprising toxicology, formulations, development, operations, quality, regulatory, EHS, and senior management was set up. Key stakeholder members from regulatory, quality, operations, and management were present during FMEA sessions. A web-based bulletin board was set up for collaborative document sharing.

Risk Review

The Quality Risk Plan is reviewed every six months. Stakeholders are rotated so that new members join for each review. Assessments from operators, a log of excursive events, and operability of the system are reviewed. An incentive program solicits input from operators — suggestions leading to risk reduction or quality improvement are implemented and evaluated continuously.

Commentary

Interpreting Ppk (process performance index)

Ppk measures overall process performance (including shifts in the process mean):

  • Ppk < 1.0: the process is unstable and the risk of going out of specification is high
  • Ppk = 0.34 (before improvement): about 15,000 ppm probability of exceeding the limit, unacceptable
  • Ppk = 2.01 (after DoE): about 251 ppm, acceptable (with enhanced monitoring)
  • Industry standard: cleaning validation usually requires Ppk ≥ 1.33

The key turning point in Scenario 3

Unlike Scenario 2 (where the blender could not meet the requirement and the introduction was ultimately abandoned), in Scenario 3:

  • DoE successfully improved the cleaning process (Ppk 0.34 → 2.01)
  • The blending bin was made dedicated, eliminating the highest-risk step
  • All the other controls together justified the risk as acceptable

This is the essence of "apparently unacceptable, but actually feasible": engineering measures combined with scientific data overturned the initial pessimistic judgment.

Hierarchy of controls

ISPE stresses that controls should be selected in the following order of priority (highest to lowest):

  1. Elimination: remove the source of the hazard
  2. Substitution: replace it with something less hazardous
  3. Engineering controls: isolators, closed transfer
  4. Administrative controls: procedures, training
  5. Personal protective equipment (PPE): protective clothing

In this scenario, closed processing (an engineering control) is the core, supported by procedures and electronic access control.

The "fail-safe" design principle

ISPE specifically emphasizes that controls should be designed to "fail safe": when the system malfunctions, it should go automatically to a safe state (for example, an alarm or a lock-out) rather than depend on human reaction. Electronic door access control and HEPA differential pressure alarms both follow this principle.

Why the six-month rolling review matters in practice

A risk assessment is not "done once and finished". Periodic review, rotation of committee members (bringing in fresh perspectives) and feedback from operators keep the risk plan in step with the real process. This reflects the continual improvement spirit of ICH Q9.

14.4 Scenario 3 — Summary
Original Text (English)

The stakeholder team has used the range of tools proposed in ICH Q9 and in this Guide to carefully evaluate the facility. During the initial qualitative analysis, deficiencies were identified and actions were implemented to mitigate these deficiencies. An evaluation team spanning relevant disciplines ensured that all hazards and exposure pathways were assessed and controls were implemented to maintain risks at acceptable levels so that the introduction of the new sex hormone Agent H product could be manufactured within the existing facility.

Commentary

Three key learning points from Scenario 3

  1. Avoid over-conservative decisions: an initial feeling that something is "not feasible" does not mean it really is; data are needed.
  2. Engineering controls first: DoE-driven cleaning improvement and closed process design made shared manufacture of a highly potent substance possible.
  3. Cross-functional collaboration + early regulatory communication: lowers the risk at later review and builds trust.
14.5 Scenario 4 — Existing Facility with Large Product Portfolio
Original Text (English)

An existing facility is currently manufacturing 32 products using 10 different APIs. The products and APIs are shown in Table 14.5.

The processes used to manufacture the products are:

  • Process 1: Sample, weigh, mill, granulate, mill, dry, mill, blend, compression, and pack (10 steps)
  • Process 2: Sample, weigh, mill, granulate, mill, dry, mill, blend, compression, coat, and pack (11 steps)
  • Process 3: Sample, weigh, mill, granulate, mill, dry, mill, blend, mill, blend, compression, mill, blend, compression, coat, and pack (16 steps)

All processes are fairly open (i.e., there are no containment devices or engineering controls used).

Commentary

The complexity of Scenario 4

This is the largest and most complex of the four scenarios. Key features:

  • 32 products and 10 APIs: the cleaning matrix is very large (10×10 = 100 combinations)
  • Fully open processing (no engineering controls): relatively high cross-contamination risk
  • Matrix approach to cleaning validation: the most stringent combination is used as the standard for the whole matrix
  • The initial analysis showed non-compliance with EU GMP: a systematic re-review was needed

Analogy: the matrix approach to cleaning validation

Imagine 10 APIs paired with each other like a 10×10 chessboard. The idea of the matrix approach is that you do not need to validate every square (100 combinations); you find the most demanding square (the lowest cleaning limit) and use it as the standard for the whole validation. If the hardest square passes, the rest are automatically satisfied.

14.5.1 Scenario 4 — Background: Product Portfolio
Table 14.5: Products Manufactured in the Facility

Highlighted rows: Orange = Anti-hypertensive1 (lowest ADE relevant combination); Red = Opioid (worst-case for cross-contamination risk assessment)

Commentary

What open processing means for risk

"All processes are fairly open" means no isolators, no closed transfer devices and no engineering controls. For Anti-hypertensive1 (ADE = 25 µg/day) and the Opioid (ADE = 50 µg/day), this is a significant cross-contamination risk factor.

Key points of the portfolio analysis

The ADEs of the 10 APIs span three orders of magnitude:

Table 14.6 (Selected): Cleaning Limits Matrix (µg/cm²)

* Values below initial LOD; required analytical method improvement.
Original matrix approach: overall acceptance = 0.1 µg/cm² (most restrictive combination).
After analytical improvement and pulling Anti-hypertensive1→Anti-hypertensive2 from matrix: new overall limit = 2.0 µg/cm².

Commentary

Logic of the cleaning matrix approach

In the original matrix, the lowest limit among all combinations = 0.1 µg/cm² (Anti-hypertensive1 → Anti-hypertensive2).

Problem: EU GMP Section 3.6 requires that the test method can reliably detect this limit, but all the original LODs were above 0.1 µg/cm², so the requirement was not met.

Resolution steps:

  1. Improve the analytical methods (see Table 14.7)
  2. The new LOD still cannot detect 0.1 µg/cm² (new LOD for Anti-hypertensive1 = 0.05)
  3. Take this combination (Anti-hypertensive1 → Anti-hypertensive2) out of the matrix and confirm it by chemical analysis each time
  4. The overall limit of the new matrix rises to 2.0 µg/cm², and the new LOD is sufficient to detect it

The requirement of EU GMP Section 3.6

EU GMP Section 3.6 states that if the residue limit derived from the toxicological assessment cannot be reliably detected by a validated analytical method, the product concerned may not be manufactured in a shared facility. This is the regulatory basis for the initial non-compliance in Scenario 4.

14.5.2 Scenario 4 — Risk Assessment
Original Text (English)

Is there a specific requirement for dedicated facility?

Regulations were reviewed. Due to the matrix approach and limits of detection, products cannot be made in shared facilities per EU GMP Section 3.6 where: "relevant residue limits, derived from the toxicological evaluation, cannot be satisfactorily determined by a validated analytical method."

The team was tasked with improving analytical methods to achieve limits of detection below 0.1 µg/cm².

Table 14.7: Limits of Detection for Cleaning Methods

Even with improved methods, Anti-hypertensive1 LOD (0.05 µg/cm²) cannot reliably detect 0.1 µg/cm². Solution: pull Anti-hypertensive1 → Anti-hypertensive2 sequence out of the matrix. Every time Anti-hypertensive2 is produced after Anti-hypertensive1, a chemical analysis will be completed to verify residue below limit. New overall matrix limit = 2.0 µg/cm² (detectable).

Data Gathered for Risk Analysis

Prior to Risk Analysis phase, the following information was gathered:

  1. Floor plans, flow plans, HVAC diagrams, room pressurization diagrams
  2. SOPs: Risk Management, Labeling, Man/Material Movement, Line clearance, Cleaning and Cleaning Validation, HVAC maintenance, Gowning/gloving
  3. Historical Data:
    • Cleaning results (30 samples; one failure — root cause: procedure not followed; CAPA: additional training)
    • Alarm reports: pressure differential out of range — 2011: 3×; 2012: 5×; 2013: 3×; 2014: 4×; 2015: 4×
    • Audit results
    • Regulatory actions
  4. Incident reports
  5. Deviations
  6. Change control list
Commentary

The starting point of the risk assessment: regulatory compliance check

The first question in the logic diagram is: "Is there a regulatory requirement for a dedicated facility?" In Scenario 4 the initial answer was almost "yes", because the test method could not detect the most stringent cleaning limit. This drove the whole improvement effort.

Why improving the analytical method matters

Improving the LOD (limit of detection) is equivalent to improving "confidence" in cleaning effectiveness. Only when the LOD is better than the cleaning limit can equipment be reliably declared "clean to the standard". If the LOD is higher than the cleaning limit, a "pass" result only means "no problem was found within the detection range of the method", which has insufficient scientific meaning.

Handling a combination "pulled out of the matrix"

Once Anti-hypertensive1 → Anti-hypertensive2 has been pulled out of the matrix, every time this sequence occurs the following is required:

  • Swab sampling before Anti-hypertensive2 is produced
  • Chemical analysis to confirm that the residue is < 0.1 µg/cm²
  • Only after it passes can the equipment be confirmed clean and released

This is a case-by-case control strategy for excluded items; it meets the regulatory requirement without changing the matrix approach entirely.

Interpreting historical cleaning data

One failure in 30 cleanings (3.3%) was caused by "failure to follow the procedure" (human error), not by failure of the cleaning process itself. This is an important distinction: the process capability is sound and the problem lies in behavior. The CAPA chose to reinforce training (rather than change the process), which is well targeted.

Table 14.8: Risk Ranking of APIs

A worst-case approach determines which product combination is used in risk assessments. Factors considered: ADE, %API, volumes manufactured, frequency of manufacture, process type, openness of equipment.

Factors making the opioid the highest risk product:

  1. ADE is fairly low compared to other compounds
  2. Manufactured fairly regularly (79 batches/year)
  3. Maximum daily dose in the middle range
  4. Process type has minimal differential impact — most products use the same process; those with more steps have greater batch sizes and ADEs

The opioid covers all 10 process steps used in the facility — no additional products need to be analyzed as worst case.

Commentary

The logic of worst-case selection

The Opioid was chosen as the worst case rather than Anti-hypertensive1, even though the latter has the lower ADE (25 vs 50 µg/day). The reasons:

  • Anti-hypertensive1 has few batches (36/year) and a small batch size (7 kg)
  • The Opioid has frequent batches (79/year), a medium batch size (115 kg) and a large contact surface area
  • The Opioid uses Process 2, which covers all the process steps in the facility

Risk = hazard (low ADE) × exposure (high frequency, large contact area). Ranking by ADE alone is not enough.

The "worst case" here differs from the "worst case" in cleaning validation

The source text specifically explains that the worst case used for cross-contamination risk assessment (the opioid) is not the same as the worst case for cleaning validation.

  • Cross-contamination worst case: considers hazard + process exposure frequency + contact surface area
  • Cleaning validation worst case: usually the substance that is hardest to clean (highest cleanability rank)

What "covers all process steps" means

The Opioid (Process 2, 10 steps) was chosen for the risk assessment not because it is the most dangerous but because its process covers every process step in the facility. A single assessment can therefore represent the process nodes of all products and avoid duplicated work.

14.5.2.1 Scenario 4 — Fishbone & FMEA Analysis
Original Text (English) — Table 14.9: FMEA Analysis (partial)

S = Severity; O = Occurrence; D = Detection. RPN = S × O × D.
Corporate RPN thresholds: <125 Acceptable | 125–343 Risk Reduction needed | >343 Cease until reduced.

Commentary

RPN (risk priority number) calculation and thresholds

RPN = Severity (S) × Occurrence (O) × Detectability (D)

  • RPN < 125: acceptable risk, no immediate action required
  • 125 ≤ RPN ≤ 343: risk reduction measures should be implemented (production may continue)
  • RPN > 343: unacceptable risk, production must stop until the risk is reduced

Analysis of the high-RPN items

RPN = 700 (highest): appears twice, both for "equipment not clean (visual inspection not adequate)"

  • Severity 10 (patient exposure) × occurrence 7 (visual inspection often fails to find it) × detectability 10 (no chemical analysis) = 700
  • The core problem: visual inspection alone cannot reliably confirm that equipment is clean to the 0.1 µg/cm² level

RPN = 490 and 350: mechanical transfer problems (gowning not changed, equipment carrying residue)

Key finding: the highest risks come from "systemic weaknesses" (inadequate visual cleaning verification, incomplete gowning procedures) rather than from facility design.

Purpose of the fishbone analysis

The fishbone (Ishikawa) diagram is a preparatory tool for FMEA, used to brainstorm systematically all possible causes of the four cross-contamination modes (people, methods, equipment, environment, materials, measurement). Once the fishbone is complete, the formal FMEA table analysis follows, ensuring that no failure mode is missed.

What "detectability = 10" means

A detectability score of 10 means "almost impossible to detect": the existing controls cannot find the problem at all. In the row "Equipment not clean / Visual not adequate", visual inspection cannot detect trace residue, so D = 10, which sends the RPN off the scale. This shows why chemical analysis (swab testing) is an indispensable means of cleaning validation.

14.5.2 Scenario 4 — Risk Evaluation, Control & Communication
Original Text (English)

Risk Evaluation

Corporate RPN thresholds (from Corporate Guidance on Risk Management):

  • Acceptable Risk: RPNs less than 125
  • Risk Reduction Required: RPNs 125 to 343 (activities may continue)
  • Unacceptable Risk: RPNs above 343 — cease until risk is reduced

Risk Control

Activities must cease until risk reduction is implemented for:

  1. Mix-up: staging in the corridor
  2. Retention: visual inspection not adequate to meet acceptance criteria
  3. Mechanical transfer: gowns not changed when changing API
  4. Mechanical transfer: residue on materials and equipment brought into/out of process room

Activities can continue but risk reduction should be implemented for:

  1. Mix-up: wrong label on container
  2. Retention: equipment not clean / did not follow procedure
  3. Airborne transfer: loss of pressure differential

Risk Communication

Immediate notification was provided to senior management on the unacceptable risk areas. Weekly updates on progress will be provided to senior management and operations until risk is reduced to allow production to resume.

Change control will be used to alert of possible changes that could alter the assessments, conclusions, and risk profile.

CAPA will be used to manage all risk reduction activities.

Training on how to manage the risk of cross-contamination will be provided to all staff involved in manufacture and/or packing.

Risk Review

The risk assessment will be reviewed:

  1. When products are added or removed from the facility portfolio
  2. When facilities, equipment and/or processes change or are added
  3. When there is a change to a product's properties (MDD, ADE, etc.)
  4. At least annually
Commentary

Practical meaning of the four "must stop production" items

  1. Staging in the corridor: materials and equipment are staged together in the corridor, which easily leads to mix-up of materials from different APIs. Remedy: prohibit corridor staging, so that all materials go directly into and out of the process rooms.
  2. Inadequate visual cleaning verification: for an API with a low ADE (such as Anti-hypertensive1), the naked eye cannot confirm cleaning to 0.1 µg/cm². Remedy: introduce swab sampling with chemical analysis.
  3. Gowning not changed when changing API: if operators moving from one API process area to another do not change their protective clothing, they mechanically carry residue with them. Remedy: establish a mandatory gown-change procedure.
  4. Equipment residue carried out of the process room: equipment removed before adequate cleaning spreads residue to the corridor and other process rooms. Remedy: equipment must be cleaned and accepted before it leaves the room.

Three levels of risk communication

  • Immediate notification: unacceptable risk, so notify senior management at once
  • Regular updates: weekly updates until the risk is reduced to an acceptable level
  • Systematic management: CAPA manages all improvement actions; change control ensures the assessment remains valid

Four triggers for re-review

A risk assessment is not a static document; any of the following requires re-assessment:

  • A change in the product portfolio (additions/removals)
  • A change to the facility, equipment or process
  • A change in product characteristics (MDD, ADE revision)
  • Periodic annual review (the minimum frequency)

This is consistent with the "continual improvement" spirit of ICH Q10 and is also a basic regulatory expectation (EU GMP, FDA) for the quality system.

14.5 Scenario 4 — Risk Summary
Original Text (English)

There are several items that must be remediated in order to resume production. These are:

  • Staging of materials in the corridor
  • Visual detection for clean equipment is not always adequate based on the acceptance criteria
  • Gown changes when changing API
  • Residue escaping rooms on materials and equipment

The team is preparing remediation plans and will update senior management and operations on a weekly basis until the risk is reduced to allow production to resume.

Commentary

Core lessons of Scenario 4

  • Matrix approach + analytical method improvement: when the cleaning limit of the most stringent combination cannot be detected, first improve the analytical method or redefine the matrix boundary, rather than abandoning the shared facility outright.
  • Open processing needs stricter procedural control: in a facility without engineering controls, personnel behavior (gowning, cleaning procedures, material management) is the main source of risk.
  • The limits of visual cleaning verification: for substances with a low ADE, visual inspection is far from sufficient and must be backed by chemical analysis.
  • Worst-case selection needs an integrated view: hazard characteristics (ADE) are only one factor; manufacturing frequency and contact surface area are equally important.
14.6 Logic Diagram — Risk-Based Manufacturing & Sourcing Decisions
14.6 Logic Diagram (Structural Reproduction)

The Logic Diagram spans pp. 161–162 and is referenced throughout Appendix 1 as the decision framework guiding all four scenarios. It covers three focus areas: cGMP/Regulatory Focus, Health and Hygiene Focus, and Asset Accommodation and Risk Management Approach. The following reproduces the key decision nodes in HTML flowchart form.

Stakeholder communication
Commentary

The three decision blocks of the logic diagram

The Logic Diagram is the core tool of the whole of ISPE Vol. 7 Appendix 1. It covers three decision dimensions:

  • cGMP/regulatory focus: is there a regulatory requirement for a dedicated facility? Can the analytical method detect the cleaning limit?
  • Health and hygiene focus: are the risks to patients and operators within an acceptable range? If not, can they be reduced through controls?
  • Facility fit and risk management: can the process be designed and implemented in the existing facility? Is the management framework complete?

How the logic diagram is applied in practice

In practice, the logic diagram is not a linear checklist but an iterative decision framework:

  • The answer to each question may trigger a new investigation (such as improving an analytical method)
  • After controls are implemented, re-assessment is needed (such as recalculating Ppk after DoE)
  • The final conclusion requires every path to pass before the risk can be declared acceptable

The "binary screening" of regulatory requirements

The first two nodes of the logic diagram form the "regulatory screening threshold":

  1. Is there an explicit regulatory requirement for a dedicated facility? If yes, a dedicated facility is needed directly and no further analysis is required.
  2. Can the analytical method detect the cleaning limit? If not, EU GMP 3.6 does not permit a shared facility (until the method is improved).

Only when both preconditions are met can the risk analysis stage begin. This is also the root cause of the initial "non-compliance" in Scenario 4 and drove the whole analytical method improvement effort.

Design philosophy of the logic diagram

The logic diagram embodies the core spirit of ICH Q9: risk management is a dynamic process, not a one-time assessment. Every question node allows the path "if it is not feasible now, can it be improved and tried again?", which avoids both extremes of over-conservatism (giving up outright) and over-boldness (ignoring the risk).

Reference List Overview

The references cited in ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 7 (2nd Edition) span regulatory guidance documents, ICH harmonized guidelines, occupational health standards, pharmaceutical engineering literature, and cleaning validation science. A total of 111 references are cited.

Overview of the references

This guide cites 111 references in four main categories:

  • Regulations: ICH Q9/Q10, EU GMP, FDA CFR, EMA HBEL guideline
  • ICH harmonised guidelines: M7, Q3C, Q3D, Q7, Q8, Q11, Q12
  • Occupational health and toxicology: methods for establishing OELs/ADEs, literature on uncertainty factors
  • Cleaning validation science: MACO calculation, cleaning chemistry, residue analysis

Commentary — Core Regulatory Documents

The most important document: the EMA HBEL guideline (reference #6)

EMA/CHMP/CVMP/SWP/169430/2012 is the most important regulatory reference in this guide. It establishes the regulatory framework in which health-based exposure limits (HBELs) replace the traditional 1/1000 dose criterion in shared facilities. After it came into effect in 2014, manufacturers worldwide had to re-evaluate the basis of their cleaning validation acceptance limits.

ICH Q9 & Q10 (#1 & #8) — the two pillars of quality risk management

ICH Q9 (2005) established a systematic framework for quality risk management of medicinal products and defines the principles for applying tools such as FMEA, HAZOP and FTA. It is the theoretical foundation of this Vol.7 guide.

ICH Q10 (2008) established the concept of the pharmaceutical quality system (PQS) and embeds QRM in product lifecycle management; it links directly to the CAPA and risk review mechanisms of Vol.7.

EU GMP EudraLex Vol.4（#4, #5, #10, #11）

The 2015 revisions of Chapter 3 (Premises and Equipment) and Chapter 5 (Production) explicitly require manufacturers to use HBEL/ADE as the scientific basis for cross-contamination control, replacing the earlier "visibly clean" criterion.

Annex 2 (Biological Products) and Annex 15 (Qualification and Validation) define the special requirements for manufacturing biologically active substances and for cleaning validation.

21 CFR Part 211 (#13) — the core FDA cGMP regulation

The current good manufacturing practice regulations of the US FDA, covering the complete requirements for facilities and equipment, personnel, process control, finished product testing and so on. All risk management measures in Vol.7 presuppose compliance with this regulation.

ICH M7 (#7) — DNA reactive impurities

Introduces the threshold of toxicological concern (TTC) concept for genotoxic impurities, with a lifetime exposure of 1.5 µg/day as the reference. In a multi-product shared facility, if the preceding product contains genotoxic impurities, the cleaning validation limit must be especially strict.

FDA Process Validation Guidance（#26）

The 2011 guidance introduced the concept of continued process verification (CPV), which emphasizes statistical process control (SPC) in place of traditional one-time validation. The CPP monitoring system in Vol.7 echoes this guidance directly.
